Abstract: Estimating how an AI system will perform on a new benchmark need not require another large model. We use public evaluation counts to choose a weak Beta prior, then update it exactly from at most 31 revealed outcomes. The predictor uses no item semantics, pretrained weights, or inference API. Its single online evaluation in the AIMS competition returned a team-reported Brier ALC of 0.188477, a 24.6% reduction from the constant-0.5 reference. The complete submission is 7,754 bytes; a server microbenchmark measures 12.71 microseconds per prediction with 31 matching labels. We describe how source evaluations are compressed into 301 prior means and show why a concentration of two lets new evidence rapidly determine the prediction. The resulting posterior gives exact uncertainty about the modelled success rate without numerical integration or neural inference. A public evaluation covers 79 system-benchmark pairs and eight seeds, reaching Brier ALC 0.200250 for the submitted method. Accompanying code and sufficient statistics reconstruct the fitted prior byte for byte and replay all 632 evaluation trajectories. The result demonstrates how much predictive value a compact Bayesian model can extract from sparse target feedback at very low online cost.

Abstract: Pretrained Time-Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) provide strong representations for downstream classification tasks. Yet adapting a TSFM under changing operating conditions remains challenging. A model must improve on each newly observed condition without sacrificing conditions it has already learned, often from only a small labeled calibration set and without knowing the operating condition at inference time. We find that this difficulty has an important geometric structure. Under an operating-condition shift, the embedding space of a TSFM does not move uniformly: some local regions change substantially, while others remain nearly unchanged. Consequently, a global adaptation can unnecessarily move embeddings that were already correctly classified. To address this, we propose the novel PACT (Piecewise Adaptation via Chart Transport). PACT partitions the frozen source embedding space into local charts and learns lightweight residual transports for the regions affected by each new condition. An explicit identity path is always retained, and a learned gate softly selects among the identity and accumulated transports for each input. The pretrained TSFM encoder, classifier head, and previously learned transports remain fixed. With only 5% labeled calibration data per arriving condition and no condition identity at test time, PACT maintains an average macro-F1 above 0.95 across a four-condition bearing-fault stream. Under homogeneous tasks and label spaces, similar retention-adaptation behavior is observed across public benchmarks. Code is available at https://github.com/Sachithx/PACT.

</details>

Abstract: Neural decoding of visual experience from electroencephalography (EEG) has achieved strong performance in intra-subject settings, but current methods fail to generalize across individuals: on the standard 200-way benchmark THINGS-EEG-2, top-1 accuracy drops from above 80% within subjects to about 32% for an unseen subject. Adapting a pre-trained encoder to new subjects and sessions typically requires labeled data, a bottleneck whose collection time severely limits practical device deployment. In a practical Brain-Computer Interface (BCI), however, the possible outcomes are known in advance and form a gallery of candidates, and the unlabeled EEG of the user accumulates as soon as the device is used. We propose a new setting, incremental transductive calibration, in which the decoder is calibrated regularly on all the unlabeled EEG received so far and on the known gallery, without any labels. This setting is closer to the conditions of a deployed BCI than the zero-shot and batch evaluations in current use. We evaluate it with a protocol in which queries arrive one at a time, with unknown and possibly unequal frequencies, and each query is decoded before it is used for calibration. In this setting, we introduce GALA : GAllery-Level Alignment, a label-free calibration method which registers the unlabeled EEG embeddings onto the gallery through shrinkage whitening, hubness correction and a Sinkhorn--Procrustes alignment with query frequencies estimated from the buffer, updated after every query. On THINGS-EEG-2, with a gallery of 200 images, it raises the top-1 accuracy on a new subject from 28% to 67% over 53 minutes of use. Across 60 evaluation settings that vary the gallery size, the query frequencies and the number of repetitions averaged per query, GALA is the most accurate label-free method in every setting, 6.6 points above the best competing method on average at the end of the stream.

</details>

Abstract: Automated red teaming is essential for identifying safety failures in large language models (LLMs), but its practical scalability depends on the resources required to discover those failures. Multi-agent systems can combine complementary capabilities, yet repeated planning, redundant exchanges, and ineffective information routing can make their coordination expensive. Moreover, a target model's response indicates the outcome of a test without identifying which collaboration decision should change. We present Learning to Coordinate (L2C), an LLM-guided framework that evolves an executable multi-agent workflow for each red-teaming case. L2C represents agent responsibilities, execution dependencies, and input bindings in a versioned graph, and associates each graph with records of the information actually delivered and produced. A controller trained through supervised fine-tuning integrates these records with target responses, evaluator verdicts, and remaining budget to retain the workflow, refine instructions, restructure collaboration, or lock further editing. This design connects feedback to explicit changes in subsequent execution while allowing a useful organization to continue without repeated controller intervention. The evaluation protocol covers HarmBench with three execution backbones and two target models, together with component ablations, dataset transfer, and evaluator checks. Across the six executor-target configurations, L2C improves ASR over EvoSynth by 1.4 to 4.5 percentage points while reducing mean online tokens by 35.6% to 37.3%. L2C connects adaptive collaboration to the resource requirements of automated security evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: Advancing physical artificial intelligence (Physical AI) requires moving beyond static object recognition to ground how real-world mechanics (e.g., impact, friction, and force transfer) unfold over time. While multimodal aligners seek to capture these interactions by matching multi-sensor streams, prevailing architectures compress each temporal sequence into a single pooled embedding. We show that this paradigm fails on twin events: distinct physical executions recorded under identical task conditions. Collapsing the temporal dimension averages out transient cross-modal dynamics, effectively reducing physical event alignment to static scene recognition. Formally, we prove that as sequence length increases, any temporally pooled representation converges to the stationary recording condition. Consequently, as windows grow, no choice of loss function or embedding geometry can disambiguate twin events from pooled features alone. Instead, we show that physical events are uniquely identified by the event gap, namely the instantaneous, synchronous co-fluctuations across modalities that are erased by pooling and accumulate only along unpooled trajectories. To recover this signal, we introduce CounterPoint, a lightweight plug-in module that strips out each modality's temporal mean to score frame-level cross-modal agreement. Integrated across four state-of-the-art (SOTA) multimodal aligners, CounterPoint shifts representations from coarse condition matching to fine-grained physical event alignment, consistently outperforming existing temporal alignment methods.

</details>

Abstract: Federated learning enables collaborative model training without sharing datasets. However, existing federated learning methods for autonomous driving typically require multi-round communication and lack closed-loop evaluation, while one-shot methods mainly focus on perception tasks. To address these limitations, we propose \method, a one-shot federated learning method for end-to-end autonomous driving. It comprises multi-client calibration, anchor-based feature alignment, and token-wise consensus distillation to distill knowledge from heterogeneous models. Closed-loop evaluation on Bench2Drive demonstrates the effectiveness of \method in both one-shot and multi-round federated learning. In the one-shot setting, it achieves a Driving Score of 55.85, which is 4.07 points higher than prior work. In the multi-round setting, it reaches 60.35 after five communication rounds, the best performance among all compared methods under the same number of communication rounds.

Abstract: This paper presents a framework for computer-aided music composition and performance based on quantum reinforcement learning and multi-agent interaction. We introduce the agentic orchestra, where musical agents evolve distinct mindsets through trial-and-error engagement with a musical environment, rather than through training on large datasets. The agents are implemented as parameterised quantum circuits. They evolve distinct compositional behaviours, referred to as mindsets. This enables the exploration of musical decision spaces using quantum superposition and entanglement within a hybrid quantum-classical loop. We present two complementary agent architectures: atomistic agents, which process pitch and rhythm independently, and holistic agents, which handle pitch and duration as unified note events. Mindsets are shaped through musically grounded reward functions, which can be combined and weighted to develop different mindsets across agents. The resulting system positions quantum computing agents less as autonomous composers and more as adaptive musical partners, each developing its own musical personality through interaction rather than imitation. Demonstrations show how agents generalise their learned behaviour to previously unseen material, operating in interactive and offline batch modes using MIDI-encoded music. While quantum computers are still in development, experiments conducted on IBM quantum hardware confirmed that musically meaningful learning is achievable with existing quantum computers. For composers and performers, the agentic orchestra opens new possibilities for emergent, process-oriented musical creativity, offering a scalable foundation for quantum computer-aided music composition as quantum computing technology continues to advance.

</details>

Abstract: Knowledge engineering (KE) turns raw files into a reasoning-ready knowledge base, a task now within reach of LLM agents. KE has progressed from chunk and graph retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to memory agents, agentic KE and multimodal KE. However, a live knowledge base needs omnimodality, a closed-loop lifecycle and multi-granular application at once, yet existing systems meet at most two and no benchmark measures all three. We formalize Omnimodal Knowledge Engineering (OmniKE) and release OmniKE-Bench, six tasks with 1,829 model-free items over 3,960 real files in five modalities. We further propose KnowledgeClaw, an agentic system that builds an omnimodal typed hypergraph, keeps it current with a closed-loop lifecycle agent and routes each query to the granularity it needs. Against 30 baselines, it ranks first on all six tasks with an overall OKE of 55.9, +14.6 over the strongest baseline. Our software and data are publicly available at https://github.com/LHRLAB/KnowledgeClaw.

Abstract: Building autonomous agents that continually explore open worlds and acquire new capabilities through experience is a long-standing aspiration in artificial intelligence. Existing research often pursues lifelong learning within predefined tasks, curricula, or objectives, with self-improvement largely confined to individual agents. We introduce OpenKAGE, an agent-group evolution framework for open-ended lifelong learning. Within this framework, agents autonomously explore open worlds and continually acquire new capabilities by accumulating and reusing knowledge from their own interactions and the experience of others. OpenKAGE builds on two key mechanisms: (1) a two-layer knowledge representation, with a memory layer for situated memories and an experience layer for transferable experience and skills that retains the applicability conditions needed for reuse; and (2) a knowledge-sharing mechanism that semantically consolidates textual experience into a continuously evolving shared knowledge graph, while executable skills are shared verbatim. In Minecraft, OpenKAGE supports open-ended lifelong learning, enabling agent groups to sustain exploration and reach advanced technological milestones. Furthermore, the accumulated knowledge can be transferred to a newly initialized agent group on a different backbone model, improving its survival and exploration progress.

</details>

Abstract: Scientific problems have no answer key, so evaluating an agent means deciding what counts as a better solution before any agent is run. Existing benchmarks either match a symbolic answer against a key or, where they require executable work, score it by unit tests, by a rubric, or by position on a competition leaderboard. None of these is the criterion by which a field decides that one published method beats another. STEMBench adopts that criterion directly. Each of its twelve research problems in high-energy physics, astrophysics and neuroscience is scored by its own field's figure of merit, and each comes with two measured anchors: a submission that uses no domain knowledge, and an expert reference reproducing a published method, both obtained by running code shipped with the benchmark. The interval between them states how much of a problem is crossed by scientific insight rather than by engineering. Across eight frontier models, task-level wins and domain-level leadership dissociate: a model that wins five of the twelve tasks leads no domain mean. In the high-effort cross-model comparison, at least one model's mean score exceeds the expert anchor on seven of twelve tasks. The remaining five span uncertainty estimation, latent and causal inference, and cross-site clinical classification. Numerical scores and official-submission validity capture different aspects of performance and are reported separately. We release the tasks, graders, anchors and harness.

</details>

Abstract: AI coding agents have become one of the most popular applications of large language models. Yet, agentic AI comes at the cost of a tremendous amount of GPU compute resources, which limits its profitability and its viability at scale. In this paper, we investigate whether smaller, cheaper models can cut this cost without sacrificing accuracy.
  We evaluate 36 popular large and small open-weight models released since 2024 on a software engineering benchmark, and find that recent small models are closing the accuracy gap compared to their large counterparts. We investigate the structure of this gap and find that the gap is highly concentrated around hard tasks that small language models fail to solve while achieving remarkably good accuracy for easy and medium complexity tasks. Following this observation, we show that by utilizing a hybrid inference strategy that tries a small model first and escalates to a large one only when needed, we can achieve up to 33% reduction in total GPU-hour costs and 16.4% on all tasks from SWE-bench Verified, without sacrificing accuracy. Strikingly, this simple cascade nearly matches an oracle-style task router, highlighting a practical path for deploying cost-aware coding agents in production without complex learned task routers and with minimal engineering complexity.

</details>

While a multitude of algorithms exist, ranging from exhaustive search to AlphaEvolve, recent advances in general-purpose coding agents such as Claude Code and OpenAI Codex raise the question: could providing such systems with a "model discovery recipe" be enough to discover or improve models? We present automodel, an agent skill for general model discovery. With case studies in astrophysics, cardiovascular dynamics, precision agriculture, traffic flow, and vehicle tire dynamics, we show how commercial agents equipped with automodel can successfully identify novel and scientifically-relevant models. Our findings suggest that encoding model-discovery methodology as a portable agent skill can provide a simple, interactive, powerful and broadly applicable alternative to domain-specific automated discovery pipelines. The skill is available at: https://github.com/unlayer-ai/automodel.

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Scientific figures carry evidence for the claims that cite them, so reviewing a paper means checking that a figure agrees with the text, and not only describing what the figure shows. Benchmarks of figure-text verification typically hand the model the figure, the claim, or the knowledge that an inconsistency exists, which leaves untested whether a model can find the figure worth checking in a complete paper, relate a visual element to the claim it conflicts with, and recognize the conflict. We study this autonomous setting on 31 full manuscripts, each carrying a reviewer-documented figure-text inconsistency from PRISMM-Bench that is hidden from the model. Six multimodal models review each PDF under a neutral prompt, and we then add one input at a time to the same manuscripts. Of 1,445 open-ended reviews, 60.6% mention the flawed figure, yet a grading rubric accepts only 3.3% as diagnosing its contradiction; a blind audit finds the rubric lenient, so we read these as rubric-accepted rates and compare conditions rather than absolute values. In a separate controlled run, naming the figure, without stating that it is flawed, raises rubric-accepted diagnosis from 4.3% to 20.4%, while a consistency instruction and coarse visual cues do not. When the contradiction is visible only in the rendered figure, text-only review yields no accepted diagnosis, while review of the rendered pages with the figure named reaches 15.8%. A baseline that lets the model choose which figures to check, ranked by centrality, stays at 4.8%. Deciding what to verify is thus a large part of the difficulty, and the selection rule we tested does not resolve it. Multimodal document understanding should be evaluated on whether a model can propose what to verify, not only answer questions about a figure it is handed.

</details>

Abstract: Traditional long-horizon LLM agents often hand off context through full-history prompting, retaining all previous action--observation interactions. As execution proceeds, this history keeps growing, increasing inference cost and potentially degrading accuracy. Existing methods control this growth by preserving compact conclusions while removing earlier observations, but doing so can discard evidence that later becomes relevant. We introduce Semantic Context Handoff (SCH), whose central abstraction is state--evidence separation: a compact semantic state carries the agent's current interpretation, while supporting action--observation evidence remains separately addressable and can re-enter context when later execution makes it relevant, without separate summarization or retrieval-model calls.
  Empirically, SCH improves long-horizon agent performance while substantially reducing context cost. In a controlled 150-task SWE-bench study, SCH's evidence recovery improves Pass@1 from $80.0\%$ to $86.9\%$ at essentially unchanged token use and execution time. In the complete-system comparison, SCH achieves 86.9% Pass@1 while using 43% fewer tokens and 41% less wall-clock time than the strongest baseline by task performance. Across models and benchmarks, SCH consistently reduces token use relative to full-history prompting while remaining competitive with or improving upon the evaluated baselines. Trajectory analysis confirms that SCH recovers evidence from substantially earlier parts of the interaction history rather than merely preserving a recent context window.

</details>

Abstract: High-quality causal reasoning datasets are essential for developing models with reliable causal understanding, particularly in specialized domains. However, existing datasets frequently suffer from incorrect annotations and structural imbalances, which can lead to biased predictions and limited generalization. To address this issue, we propose CaDO (Causal-Engine-Guided Multi-Agent Data Optimization), a multi-agent framework for automated diagnosis and repair of causal reasoning datasets. Our framework consists of three key stages: formal causal reasoning, multi-level diagnosis, and automated data repair and generation. First, a Formalizer Agent translates natural language questions into formal causal queries to minimize semantic deviations and make retrieval and verification more explicit and tractable. Second, a dual-level diagnosis mechanism employs independent Critic Agents to identify sample-level errors while analyzing query-type coverage and label distributions to detect dataset-level structural imbalances. Finally, the framework employs reusable repair operators grounded in formal causal semantics to correct erroneous samples, while Explorer and Expander Agent collaboratively produce diverse and semantically consistent new samples. Experimental results show that CaDO significantly outperforms the strongest of five data cleaning baselines in 16 out of 18 experimental settings, improving downstream model accuracy by up to 9.1%. Moreover, at least 95% of the samples flagged by CaDO are defective, and on CLadder, CaDO incorrectly modifies 92.2% fewer correct labels than an LLM-based judge, demonstrating its effectiveness and reliability. Our code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/thisissyu/cado.

</details>

Abstract: Pre-trained visual world models generate multi-step action plans from current and goal observations, yet terminal-proximity objectives fail to prevent forbidden-zone traversal or rigid-body collision sliding. Purely visual interfaces may lack analytical dynamics, structured states, or explicit collision geometry, requiring internal prediction of action-conditioned process risk. We propose Risk-Triggered Search (RTS) for frozen Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) world models. Rather than relying on static state features that cannot distinguish candidate plans sharing an observation, RTS unrolls action-conditioned future latent prefixes in the frozen world model and trains a lightweight risk head with process-violation supervision. To intervene selectively while preserving safe nominal behavior, RTS uses the same predicted prefix-risk criterion as both the search trigger gate and hard feasibility gate: safe nominal plans execute directly, whereas alarms trigger local Cross-Entropy Method (CEM) search to minimize nominal deviation within the feasible set. To prevent isotropic Euclidean minimal modification from shrinking obstacle-directed actions into zero-amplitude stalling, we introduce an amplitude-preserving metric $d_{\mathrm{AP}}$ that retains tangential progression while maintaining a unique zero at the nominal plan. Under unified gating and this unique-zero condition, bypassing safe-state search yields closed-loop trajectories strictly identical to every-step same-kernel search. Evaluated under a process-safety benchmark and Safe Arrival Rate (SAR) metric across five tasks, RTS improves SAR over Direct by 12.67--41.67 percentage points on navigation and articulated control and +2.08/+4.17 pp on Cube/PushT boundary cohorts, maintaining 100% action identity with every-step search while cutting controller time by 17.71%--70.41% across 1,968 paired trajectories.

</details>

Abstract: Synthetic polysomnography (PSG) data has great potential in healthcare applications, for example in substituting real data that is sensitive and challenging to collect. Previous research on synthetic PSG data has been largely focused on short segments of individual signals, overlooking the inter-signal dynamics related to sleep disorders. In this study, we developed a generative adversarial network (PSG-GAN) for co-synthesizing longer segments of multiple PSG signals with diverse and complex characteristics typical of obstructive sleep apnea (OSA). The synthetic signals appeared realistic, and the electroencephalography and electrooculography band powers were similarly distributed across sleep stages. Moreover, the PSG-GAN learned to mirror signal interactions such as changes in photoplethysmography-based heart rate during and after breathing obstructions. An automatic scoring model trained solely with automatically scored synthetic signals reached good performance on sleep staging (\k{appa}=0.71 and 0.63) and respiratory event segmentation (\k{appa}=0.57 and 0.60), on two test sets consisting of healthy volunteers and OSA patients, respectively. This demonstrates the potential of synthetic data in supporting algorithm development for sleep diagnostics. The source code is available at https://github.com/rikuhuttunen/psg-gan.

Abstract: LLM-based agents accumulate extensive interaction histories when solving complex, long-horizon tasks, making efficient memory management critical to their performance and scalability. While existing memory management methods primarily focus on what to retain or retrieve, they often overlook how much perceptual detail should be allocated to individual past interactions. We posit that effective agent memory depends not only on the information preserved, but also on the granularity at which each past interaction is perceived. Motivated by this insight, we propose Resolution as Attention (RAA), a memory perception mechanism that uses visual resolution as a means of attention allocation across interaction histories. RAA renders past interactions as images and dynamically adjusts their resolution at every step according to their relevance to the agent's upcoming decision, preserving fine-grained information for critical interactions while reducing visual-token consumption for less relevant ones. To adaptively determine the perceptual fidelity of each interaction, we train a lightweight relevance estimator with teacher-model guidance and further optimize it to preserve the agent's decision consistency at a reduced visual-token cost. Experiments across multiple tasks and backbones show that RAA reduces token consumption by over 50% without compromising task performance. Moreover, RAA can be integrated as a plug-in perception layer with existing memory management methods, yielding further token savings and revealing adaptive perception as a complementary dimension of agent memory.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-based artificial societies exhibit rich behavior but often lack scientific grounding: personas and environments are assumed, interaction networks are synthetic, and simulators are generated as free-form code, making results difficult to tie to real populations and sensitive to the backbone model. We argue that simulated worlds should be sampled from observed data, while simulation logic should be implemented by composing operations from a shared, task-independent vocabulary. In this paper, we introduce ABSClaw, a zero-code agent that compiles natural-language requests into executable, reality-grounded social simulations. ABSClaw grounds who acts, where they act, and with whom they interact in survey and census data, geospatial sources, and real networks. It expresses state transitions through 26 atomic operations. To support an open and extensible ecosystem, ABSClaw further allows capabilities to be added as lifecycle-scoped skills without modifying its core. Furthermore, we propose ABSBench, a benchmark of 193 real-world social-simulation tasks. On ABSBench, ABSClaw achieves the best overall performance among the evaluated social simulators. Three real-world case studies show that grounding personas, environments, and interaction networks each improves agreement with observed outcomes. With one runtime skill, ABSClaw executes 13 population-scale simulations, collectively involving 25 million agents. Together, these results demonstrate ABSClaw's real-world fidelity, extensibility, and scalability.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal knowledge graph reasoning (MKGR) aims to infer missing facts by jointly exploiting relational structures, textual descriptions, and visual evidence. Existing embedding-based methods effectively capture graph topology, but their fixed multimodal representations are less effective for query-specific fine-grained semantic reasoning, while generation-based methods are vulnerable to noisy retrieval and may produce invalid entities. To address these challenges, we propose NaviGATE, a noise-aware generative framework for MKGR. NaviGATE first aligns graph-derived structural representations with textual semantics, then performs structure-aware internal knowledge activation by fine-tuning the multimodal language model with multimodal entity summaries and injected structural tokens. In the noise-aware dual-view instruction tuning stage, NaviGATE jointly optimizes retrieval-free and retrieval-aware views: reliable retrieved candidates are reinforced through contrastive logit sharpening, while misleading candidates are suppressed through parametric consistency distillation. In inference, structure-constrained decoding restricts generation to valid entities in the target knowledge graph. Experiments on standard benchmarks show that NaviGATE consistently outperforms strong unimodal, multimodal, and generation-based baselines, and remains robust under noisy retrieval scenarios.

</details>

Abstract: Pre-trained models can read screens and reason about games, yet on an unfamiliar game, an agent still has to learn to play. We ask how an agent can self-improve on such a game from a few of its own attempts, in real-time visual games where the agent sees only the screen and the game keeps running while the agent thinks. We study two kinds of policy that can carry this improvement. In model as policy, a model selects actions during play; we adapt real-time chunking so that committed actions keep executing while the model reasons, and train a 2B controller for this interface. In code as policy, a model writes, tests, and revises a program between attempts, and the program acts during play. Across 170 GameWorld tasks, with at most eight one-minute executions per run, code as policy reaches 47.80% mean progress, against 30.90% for our trained controller, and leads in all five game categories. Improving the controller's weights on a single game, by contrast, yields only modest gains. These results favor letting a model think offline and storing what it learns in a program that acts in real time. Project repository: https://github.com/TabibitoQZP/SIGame

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) and LLM-based agents are increasingly deployed, monitoring their behavior becomes important for detecting unsafe or unintended actions. Activation probes offer an efficient approach, but existing methods face trade-offs between accuracy, interpretability, and computational overhead. Probes on raw activations lack interpretable inputs, whereas sparse autoencoder (SAE) probes use interpretable features but often rely on additive classifiers and large SAE encoders, limiting interpretability and increasing memory overhead. We introduce GateWatch, a compact, interpretable classifier that expresses its entire decision rule as an explicit Boolean logic over a small number of binarized SAE features. We explore three approaches to constructing these circuits under a specified gate budget. For efficient deployment, GateWatchServe computes only selected SAE features, enables streaming of their binary values across layers during inference, and evaluates multiple GateWatch classifiers in parallel using compact lookup tables. Across 24 tasks on Gemma-2-9B, GateWatch achieves 80.49% mean test accuracy using only 3.8 SAE features and 3.5 logic gates per probe on average, just 1.17 percentage points below full 16K-feature SAE probes, while matching or outperforming them on 10 tasks and reducing probe memory by 99.98%. Inside vLLM, 1,000 concurrent multi-layer, multi-token GateWatch classifiers retain 97-98% of baseline throughput and 99.8% of KV-cache capacity.

</details>

Abstract: Adapting a film into a game requires selecting source elements and translating them into player actions, rules, and feedback. We introduce Movie2Game-Bench (M2G-bench), a benchmark for evaluating film-to-game adaptation beyond playability. M2G-bench combines general evaluation, movie-specific evaluation, and playtesting by three GUI model players. The protocol connects source content to gameplay steps and implementation evidence, capturing both content preservation and interactive realization. The benchmark comprises 22 films and 658 source-specific reference items. Evaluating 14 models on the same films with their selected primary harnesses reveals complementary strengths in game quality, adaptation, and player experience. A shared weakness concerns playable sequences: this category has the lowest content-presence mean in all 14 models, averaging 37.9 out of 100 compared with 54.8 for iconic elements. Among judgments of retained emotionaldevelopment content, narrative choices account for 60.0% and scenario operation for 7.2%. These findings identify two linked priorities for film-to-game adaptation: preserving source situations that can support gameplay and connecting retained content to player actions and consequences.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a common approach for training large language model (LLM) agents on long-horizon tasks. However, learning from sparse terminal rewards makes credit assignment and exploration difficult. We approach these challenges through a policy-induced capability region, the set of states from which the current policy can reliably complete a task. Near its boundary, an agent may need only a few actions to reach a more solvable state, even when the goal remains distant. This reduces long-horizon learning to two coupled subproblems of reaching that state and completing the task from there. We address them with paired advantages and a progressive reverse-to-forward curriculum. The advantages provide separate signals for reaching more solvable states and completing the task from them, while the curriculum expands the capability region backward along successful reference trajectories and then into off-reference states through on-policy exploration. On ALFWorld and WebShop, our method outperforms competitive agentic RL methods at both model scales, achieving success rates of 90.1%/96.1% and 80.2%/82.6% with Qwen2.5-1.5B/7B-Instruct, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: Deployed world-action models (WAMs) can keep improving only if they make good use of their own experience. Existing methods supply retrieved rollouts or demonstrations to the policy as context, yet such experience often turns previously solved tasks into failures. What, then, makes good experience for a WAM? We show that experience is valuable only in use: its value depends not on whether the source rollout succeeded but on what is stored, when it is used, and where it enters the policy. We therefore propose F$^3$, which makes these three decisions with Form, Fit, and Fusion. Form compresses successful and failed rollouts alike into short, verified operation records; Fit teaches the policy to read a record and to predict when it helps; Fusion injects the record's images, text and actions into the policy as a prefix and an action prior. Trained once on only 200 examples and deployed with a growing experience library, the model achieves state-of-the-art results on MolmoSpaces and RoboLab without any training on RoboLab: over native Cosmos 3 Nano, it raises success by 12.0 pp and 10.8 pp, reduces steps to success by 12.2% and 11.3%, and cuts regressions by 57%.

</details>

Abstract: Rules govern how player actions change interactive worlds. Different rules create distinct gameplay experiences within similar scenes and action spaces. For customizable gameplay, generative world models must follow user-defined rules beyond real-world behaviors. However, realizing user-defined rules presents two challenges: Rule-State Persistence and Rule-to-Visual Realization. The former requires preserving rule definitions, dependencies, and world states across saving and loading, so that restored worlds follow the established rules. The latter requires accurately realizing rule-induced changes on designated objects and attributes, particularly when affected objects are unseen. In this paper, we propose WorldRuler, which organizes rule definitions, dependencies, and object states in an executable 3D rule graph and connects rule execution, video generation, and perceptual feedback in a closed loop. The graph supports joint saving and restoration of rules and world states, while dynamic object binding grounds rule-specified entities in visual instances and uses stored states to constrain generation from their first appearance. We further introduce object attribute state replacement and counterfactual reconstruction training, enabling accurate attribute control and compatible appearance and motion changes while limiting interference with unrelated content. We develop an automated data collection and annotation pipeline using GTA-V and Habitat to construct RuleVerse, a dataset of rule-driven interactions with detailed state and event annotations, including cases where rules take effect before affected objects become visible. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of WorldRuler in preserving rule-state consistency across saving and loading and accurately realizing rule outcomes on designated objects and attributes.

</details>


### [82] [Knowing What You Know: A Trait Theory of Reviewer Value in Human-AI Cognitive Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24932)

Abstract: When a capable model and a human reviewer cooperate on the same decision, which human still adds value? We argue this is a question about a single cognitive trait, metacognitive calibration: the alignment between a reviewer's behavioral confidence and the actual correctness of their judgments. We develop a trait theory of reviewer value to make it precise. The theory makes five claims: value is governed by calibration rather than by activity, speed, agreement, or tenure; calibration leaves observable traces in behavioral telemetry; those traces are non-diagnostic on their own, because the fast, low-revision signature of a calibrated expert is indistinguishable from that of a confident novice until it is gated by outcomes; human calibration and a model's self-knowledge are the same construct, so the joint system should defer to whichever agent is better calibrated on each item; and individual calibration is causally identifiable only above the level of the single judgment. We ground each claim in a six-month production window of an AI-assisted accounting system (541621 reviewer events, 483 reviewers), and find that a purely behavioral expertise profile inverts the ground truth, scoring an overconfident cohort (5.2 percent correction survival) higher than genuine experts (99.2 percent); that reviewer quality is flat across tenure, so the trait is not learned on the job; and that the same calibration logic that separates good from bad reviewers, applied to the model, concentrates 92.5 percent of human effort on genuine errors. We close with the boundary the theory must respect: under majority-vote aggregation, individual causal value is unidentifiable.

</details>

Abstract: Streaming 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) allows for online reconstruction of dynamic scenes from multi-view videos for Free Viewpoint Video (FVV). Current time and storage efficient methods rely on selecting a subset of anchor Gaussians and neighborhood interpolation for fast streamable 3DGS. However, these methods can overlook object-specific motion and introduce inconsistencies near object boundaries. We propose OCLDA-GS, an object-centric streaming framework that dynamically reallocates object and motion-aware Gaussian anchors to allocate anchor Gaussian support for objects undergoing large motion while at the same time, restricting the gaussian neighborhood within object groups for more precise object boundaries. Furthermore, it estimates object-wise rigid transformations (e.g. a hand moving) and leverages these estimates to learn per-anchor local deformation correction for small deformations (e.g. wrinkles on a shirt). Then, we conduct experiments on three dynamic multi-view video datasets and demonstrate improvements over the previous baselines alongside improved quality between refinement timesteps. Under matched refinement budgets, our method improves PSNR over Instant Gaussian Stream (IGS) by 0.169 dB on N3DV, 0.202 dB on Meeting Room and +0.199 on Google Immersive, while reducing the mean PSNR drop between refinement timesteps by 9.2% and 13.8% in N3DV and Meeting Room, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: We present the Autoregressive View Model (AVM), which generates a scene as a stream of views without constructing an explicit 3D representation. Through a unified in-context interface, AVM conditions on images or videos to generate short clips along a requested camera path. Visual context grounds reconstruction and interpolation, while a learned prior enables extrapolation into unseen regions. AVM retrieves relevant views from inputs and generated history to form a bounded active context. We fine-tune a pretrained bidirectional video diffusion transformer with flow matching in an N-in-1-out formulation. We then tune a causal model on its own rollouts to improve photorealism, reduce denoising steps, and limit long-horizon drift. We scale training to approximately 8M scenes and video clips. We evaluate generation capacity and scene persistence across benchmarks for camera control, text control, visual quality, dynamics, sparse and 4D reconstruction, and long-horizon consistency. The results demonstrate that AVM adapts to diverse input and output settings through one in-context interface.

</details>

Abstract: Attention maps cheaply show where a vision-language model (VLM) looks, but attention sinks make them unreliable for localization. Contrasting the question with a generic prompt suppresses sinks, yet only some layers localize the queried object. Existing methods find these layers with box annotations or fixed rules. We show that attention itself can identify them. A question changes how much a head attends to the image and where this attention goes. The two effects separate exactly, and the second, measured in bits on unlabeled questions, indicates which layers localize. WhereBits turns these where-to-look bits into a layer prior and combines it with per-head contrastive scores. Its three design constants were fixed once on labeled COCO data, and a new model or resolution needs only unlabeled questions. Across eight VLMs, WhereBits matches the pointing accuracy of box-tuned layers on COCO and RefCOCO and ranks target tokens better. This advantage holds on a held-out model family and at higher resolutions. Its COCO pointing accuracy is 0.72, against 0.22 for mean attention. In training-free zoom-in, its crops raise V* accuracy by 6.0 points under forced-choice evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: Biometric recognition in surveillance, video, and web imagery must contend with extreme variation in pose, blur, illumination, occlusion, and distance, where visual quality metrics fail to predict whether inputs are recognizable to the deployed encoder. Existing face image quality assessment methods rely on visual heuristics, curated annotations, or costly generative pipelines, leaving their predictions detached from the encoder's decision geometry. We introduce TransFIRA, a lightweight framework that grounds recognizability directly in embedding space. TransFIRA derives class-center similarity (CCS) and class-center angular separation (CCAS) from a pretrained encoder's embeddings and learns to predict them from images without quality annotations. Using predicted CCS for template weighting and CCAS>0 as a natural filtering threshold yields state-of-the-art face verification performance on BRIAR, raising TAR at $10^{-6}$ FMR from 0.085 with uniform averaging to 0.598. The predicted scores also reveal how image degradations affect encoder-specific recognizability. TransFIRA+ extends this framework to cross-camera multimodal person identification in untrimmed surveillance video. In this setting, body recognizability scores saturate within a narrow range, making direct weighting effectively uniform. We introduce a post-hoc sigmoid calibration that transforms these saturated scores into informative weights without retraining. After calibration, the BRIAR-trained TransFIRA predictor improves fused face and body identification on two unseen multi-camera datasets, outperforming detector-confidence weighting and uncalibrated CCS-rank weighting. Together, TransFIRA and TransFIRA+ demonstrate that encoder-grounded recognizability generalizes across biometric modalities and recognition settings.

</details>

Abstract: Compact convolutional backbones such as EfficientNet achieve strong parameter efficiency through local depthwise separable convolutions, yet two structural questions remain central to hybrid design: where does global self-attention improve recognition over pure convolution, and can repeated blocks share weights without losing representational capacity? We investigate both questions on ImageNet-1k (50,000-image validation) using controlled EfficientNet-B0 experiments. First, we show that wrapping a classification backbone in multi-scale feature pyramids (BiFPN) fails to outperform a plain MBConv trunk (69.99% - 71.81% vs. 72.86% plain B0) until paired with stage attention (73.15%). In contrast, keeping the MBConv trunk intact and inserting lightweight zero-gated residual self-attention adapters at stages P3 (28x28 windowed), P4 (14x14 global), and P5 (7x7 global) reaches 74.02% standalone and outperforms a bit-for-bit matched plain-B0 control by +0.954 percentage points (73.88% vs. 72.92%) across an 88-epoch stateless replay schedule, with learned attention amplitude rising monotonically with semantic depth (27.2% at P3 -> 45.4% at P4 -> 66.3% at P5). Second, we evaluate recurrent weight sharing across repeated MBConv blocks with pass-specific BatchNorm statistics (+1,536 parameters per pass). While post-hoc kernel averaging degrades accuracy to 17.03% and single-template stage tying (-27.6% parameters) plateaus at 66.10%, alternating two shared block templates ([B1, A, B, A]) in Stage 6 cuts total model parameters by 10.9% (582K weights) and recovers by +10.82 points in a single epoch to 70.92% (-1.84 points vs. unshared control).

</details>


### [88] [NIRJALA: Draining the Sea out of Gaussian Splatting with Three-Path Optics and Reversible Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23450)

Abstract: Water does not just tint an underwater photograph; it rewrites it. Each pixel mixes attenuated scene radiance, backscattered light, and scene light scattered forward on its way to the camera, and many combinations of scene and water explain the same pixels. Most Gaussian-splatting methods for underwater scenes model only the first two effects and remove the water by dividing by a transmission that decays exponentially with range, so their inversion gain grows without bound. We present NIRJALA (Sanskrit for "without water"), a 3D Gaussian Splatting framework that recovers water-free appearance and geometry together. TRIVENI, a three-path image formation model, adds a range-saturating forward-scattering term to direct transmission and backscatter; we prove that this keeps the inversion gain bounded at every range and bound how errors in the estimated backscatter and attenuation propagate into the recovered colours. PRATIBIMBA, a reversible physics anchor, ties a flexible neural water model to a ten-parameter physical model in both directions, so scene content cannot hide inside the water. SAMARASA collects the physics-informed objectives, including a range-binned colour prior. To score water removal against ground truth, we built SAJALA ("with water"), a 30-scene suite path-traced in Blender Cycles with water-free images, depth, and normals under controlled water colour and turbidity. On SAJALA, NIRJALA raises PSNR from 24.34 to 24.76 dB and lowers depth AbsRel from 0.109 to 0.091 against the strongest baseline; on real SeaThru-NeRF scenes it lowers LPIPS from 0.194 to 0.175 while rendering at 44 FPS. We also discuss where appearance metrics and geometry disagree underwater, and the limits of our evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: Training 3D vision-language models (VLMs) on large medical imaging datasets collected across institutions is constrained by data quality. Vast, uncurated pools of image-report pairs are characterized by redundancy, class imbalance, and cross-institutional heterogeneity. We address this challenge of data curation for improving 3D VLM pretraining by proposing an offline, training-free, reference-guided data curation recipe. Our approach leverages a small, high-quality reference set to independently extract quality signals for each modality of a large, uncurated pool and merges them through a rank-aggregation step into a single ranking that guides sample selection. We validate this method on datasets comprising 35k 3D abdominal CT volume-report pairs from two institutions and 58k 2D chest X-ray image-report pairs. On 3D CT, using our reference-guided data curation strategy, our curated model matches the zero-shot disease classification performance of a state-of-the-art baseline (Merlin) while using $3.3\times$ less training data and a reference set of 5k samples. On 2D chest X-rays, our method yields a curated model that matches or exceeds the zero-shot disease classification performance of a model trained on the full pool while using up to $10\times$ less training data and a reference set of only 74 samples. Ablations show that the selection is stable under resampling of the reference, that smaller reference sets still outperform random sampling, and that label rebalancing does not improve performance. To support reproducibility, we release our code.

</details>

Abstract: Absolute 3D human pose estimation (HPE) aims to recover both a person's body pose and their global position in 3D space. Privacy-preserving millimeter-wave (mmWave) radar provides distance and direction measurement capabilities, but its sparse point clouds make HPE under-constrained. Low-resolution thermal imaging can provide complementary observations of body direction and structure. However, our evaluation shows that a strong feature-level fusion baseline loses much of its localization and HPE advantage when generalizing to unseen subjects, with radial localization becoming less accurate than a radar-only baseline. Motivated by this finding, we introduce mmTherm, a lightweight framework that explicitly incorporates the complementary geometric properties of thermal imaging and mmWave radar. It combines body structure and joint directions estimated from thermal images with range information from radar to localize the body and reconstruct its 3D pose. Experiments on OctoNet show that mmTherm reduces cross-user absolute joint error by 33% and root localization error by 38% relative to the strongest corresponding baselines, while requiring substantially fewer parameters and less computation at inference.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action models (VLAs) encode images with 2D encoders that capture little 3D structure. A common approach is to feed the policy estimated depth, but a UAV chooses its own altitude, must judge distances tens of meters away, where monocular depth is less reliable, and must relate its forward and downward views. On long aerial routes, adding depth features directly brings no significant gain (42.5% vs. 42.6% success), so we study how 3D information should be given to the policy. Analyzing flight failures, we find that two forms of 3D grounding fail in opposite ways: explicit 3D lifting of depth collides less on the way but more often fails to finish at the target, while implicit alignment with a 3D geometry model finishes more often but collides more. We propose GRID, which combines both: (1) it lifts monocular depth into ray-corrected 3D positions in the UAV's frame for both cameras, and (2) during training only, it aligns its visual features with a frozen multi-view geometry model, at no inference cost. GRID adding explicit lifting to implicit alignment significantly increases how often the policy reaches the target ($61.5\% \rightarrow 69.5\%$, $p<0.001$), and adding implicit alignment to explicit lifting significantly increases how often it completes the task once there ($57.7\% \rightarrow 61.4\%$, $p<0.05$). On TravelUAV (3,005 test episodes), GRID achieves the best success rate on every split, with relative gains over the state of the art of 9.5% (seen), 17.1% (unseen objects) and 25.4% (unseen map).

</details>

Abstract: Medical imaging agents already return answers on heterogeneous imaging surfaces, yet deployment still faces a limited verification budget where each wrong accept can harm clinical trust. When a thin check and a deeper evidence pass disagree on the same case, deciding when to stop, open more evidence, repair, or reject without a shared controller remains ill-posed. Tool-using workflows often lock a surface-specific evidence order per dataset, or a private always-open full-depth verification path; those pipelines stay costly, leave many wrong accepts, and cannot share one controller across modalities. We present MedHarness, a registered multi-surface runtime reliability layer that reformulates post-answer control as shared decision-object attachment after a proposer's initial prediction. Under Shared Decision Object, Minimum-Sufficient Verification, and Intermediate Repair, one shared policy with surface recipe adapters lets a new imaging surface join the same stop / open-evidence / repair / reject controller by recipe registration. On a 512-case live pool, MedHarness cuts wrong accepts versus a full-verification baseline by about 47% and raises accepted accuracy to 83.2% vs. 72.9% at 86.1% coverage versus full release under that baseline. On a failure-enriched multi-surface stress ranking, the same controller cuts estimated remote service calls per case by about 71% versus that baseline under a matched context-load setting. A held-out three-surface check and an alternate language-model transfer keep the same control ordering. The code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MedHarness-paper-release-A7F3/.

</details>


### [93] [Trust the Ray, Not the Depth: Ray-Lateral Agreement for Multi-View 3D Human Pose Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23513)

Abstract: Put a monocular mesh estimator behind every camera of a calibrated rig, and the reconstructed bodies land a meter or more apart. The cause is geometric: each estimate sits at the depth implied by a focal length the network assumed, not the one the camera has. Its error therefore points almost entirely along its own viewing ray, and the calibration tells us that direction exactly. To address this, we introduce the Ray-Lateral Agreement (RLA), which follows one rule: trust the ray, not the depth. RLA first slides every estimate along its own rays, which removes the depth bias and leaves the shape untouched. It then fuses the views one point at a time, under a Gaussian error whose precision follows each viewing ray. The maximum-likelihood solution is closed-form, interpolates between averaging and midpoint triangulation, and stays well conditioned where triangulation is singular. Robustness is the harder half, because correct views also disagree along their rays. Any weight built on the full residual therefore punishes good views together with bad ones. Ray-lateral agreement instead scores every view, at every point, by its residual across that view's own ray alone. We evaluated RLA on multi-camera public benchmark datasets. Across two, three, and four cameras and three per-view pose estimators, RLA consistently achieves lower worst-case error than all triangulation baselines.

</details>

Abstract: In vision-language models (VLMs), a few sink tokens attract a disproportionate share of attention. While previous studies have examined why these tokens attract attention, we investigate how the sink-key direction affects attention to image and question tokens. Across four probed VLMs, boosting the magnitude of positive sink-aligned components increases attention on average, whereas boosting negative components decreases it; these responses persist through later layers. This identifies the native sink key as a reference for controlling attention beyond its own position. Motivated by this finding, we propose SHRINK, a training-free method that partially reduces sink-aligned components across visible keys during generation. On nine benchmarks under sampled reasoning, it improves mean accuracy by 5.1 and 2.7 percentage points on LLaVA-OneVision-7B and Qwen2.5-VL-3B, with smaller gains on the other two main models, Qwen2.5-VL-7B and Gemma-3-4B. Beyond SHRINK, rescaling sink-aligned key components can replace the attention operations of two existing attention-steering methods, GIFT and ACG, with comparable accuracy on hallucination benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Vision language models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance in 3D computed tomography (CT) classification, but high accuracy does not reflect whether their predictions rely on the correct anatomical evidence. We introduce ORGANIC3D, a framework for evaluating and improving organ level image text alignment through controlled anatomical interventions. Our benchmark assesses 12 vision encoder checkpoints and 7 generative VLMs using matched patient comparisons, organ erasure, lesion replacement, and laterality perturbations, revealing substantial alignment failures even among top performing models. To learn organ level image text correspondence, ORGANIC3D aligns each finding sentence with the anatomy aware image features and trains on edited scans to anchor this alignment in the organ's image evidence. We further extend this framework to generative VLMs, promoting evidence dependent question answering and report generation. Trained exclusively on public data, ORGANIC3D achieves SOTA classification AUROC on CTRATE, Merlin, and the heldout RADChestCT dataset and reaches 77% laterality accuracy, compared with at most 66% for prior models. Its generative VLM matches or exceeds all evaluated publicly released VLMs on visual question answering, achieves the highest report accuracy, and retracts 96 to 99% of target organ findings after organ removal, versus a maximum of 69% for other models. Together, these results establish ORGANIC3D as a framework for testing whether 3D CT vision language models ground their predictions in anatomical evidence and for improving organ level alignment alongside predictive accuracy.

</details>

Abstract: VGGT reconstructs cameras and geometry from unposed images in one pass, but its global attention grows quadratically with the number of views. Training-free accelerators thin this attention and work well on redundant video, but lose pose accuracy when views share little geometry. This paper traces these losses to two kinds of cross-view information that a uniform sparse approximation can fail to preserve together: diffuse layers aggregate distributed context, whereas matching layers depend on query-specific interactions that carry little attention mass. A per-layer statistic measures the log-mass missed by a mean token. Its row-free proxy is the logit variance, and it selects the estimator for each layer. Diffuse layers use shared, importance-sampled hub keys, with log-weights that make the softmax numerator and denominator unbiased. Matching layers stay exact: every aggressive thinning tested cost accuracy. At equal cost, the selected layers outperform the four shifted windows tested. On 13 unseen low-redundancy scenes with 32 views, LMSA stays within 1.4 AUC@5 of dense attention, compared with 10.3 for AVGGT and 4.5 for SAF3R; its difference from contemporaneous GPA-VGGT is not significant, and only its gains over AVGGT pass both scene-level tests. With 100 views, it stays within 1.9 AUC@5 while its aggregator runs $2.0\times$ faster. Used post hoc to choose the layers GPA-VGGT protects, the statistic improves it by 1.2 AUC@5 for 5% more FLOPs. Applied untuned to $π^3$, it beats AVGGT but remains further from dense accuracy.

</details>

Abstract: Latent-space planners select actions by comparing the embedding of a predicted future image with that of a goal image. This assumes that distance in the embedding reflects how far the scene is from the goal. We profile this assumption with simulator-executed futures, which removes prediction error. For six frozen DINO and V-JEPA encoders, feature distance barely tracks the object's distance to its goal: the rank correlation is at most 0.11, against 0.87 for object-mask centroids. It is dominated by background and robot patches, and frozen-feature readouts capture the arm far better than the object. Inspired by the ventral (what) and dorsal (where/how) visual streams, we propose DOCK, which learns an object representation whose changes agree with measured physical motion. A grasped object moves with the gripper, so forward kinematics on the joint angles gives a proxy for the object's displacement without annotation. From 9,951 held-object transport trajectories in 21 simulated kitchen scenes, we mine 404K displacement pairs and train three components: an object token pooled from patch features (what), a spatial ruler that makes differences of readout coordinates equal to measured displacements (where), and a dynamics predictor over object tokens (how). Used as a planning cost, the learned representation places the object in 15 of 21 tasks, as many as an oracle that reads the true object position, while mask geometry reaches 10 and frozen feature distance 9. It still solves the most tasks when the unseen robot arm is visible, and its ranking transfers to held-out layouts with unseen object instances and categories without any adaptation.

</details>

Abstract: Semi-supervised multi-organ segmentation should learn more than a dozen organs from a handful of labeled scans, and the smallest organs occupy less than one percent of the foreground. We identify a failure mode of self-training that is specific to this regime. When a teacher misses a small organ in a scan, every voxel of that organ receives the confident pseudo-label background, and the student is trained toward the organ absence. Repeated over many scans, this loop extinguishes tail organs. In a pilot study, removing the gallbladder from the pseudo-labels reduced its Dice on AMOS from 36.5 to 1.0. We argue that a missing organ should be treated as unresolved rather than absent and propose Existence-aware Self-Training (ExiST). It builds pseudo-labels by leave-one-out consensus of independently trained networks, screens them with a quality gate on validation scans, and infers, per scan and per organ, whether the organ was found. For every unresolved organ, the background voxels of that scan receive the set-valued target, trained with a marginal cross-entropy that accepts either answer and a Dice term that skips uncertain voxels. We show that this loss never pushes a candidate organ toward absence and never favors one member of the set, which leaves the decision to labeled evidence. ExiST outperforms the current state-of-the-art methods and reaches 77.1 mean Dice on Synapse with 20% labeled scans and 75.9 on AMOS with 5% labeled scans.

</details>

Abstract: Unlabeled test data can improve the zero-shot classifier of a vision-language model, yet transductive and test-time adaptation methods often fall below the zero-shot baseline when only a few vocabulary classes are present in a batch or when streams are correlated. We trace this failure to a shared design: every class owns independent parameters estimated from its own samples, so absent or rare classes are left at biased text anchors or fitted to foreign samples. We propose Graph-Regularized Anchor-displacement Field Transduction (GRAFT), which models the adapted prototypes as text anchors plus a displacement field that is partially pooled over a semantic class graph through a Gaussian Markov random field prior, together with an explicit class-presence model. Inference is closed-form and gradient-free on cached features. Anchor-only shrinkage and a single global shift are the two extreme settings of the prior; absent classes are provably harmonic extensions of the present ones, and the pooling strength can be chosen by empirical Bayes. Under the realistic batch and online protocols on eleven benchmarks and four ImageNet shifts, GRAFT improves over the zero-shot model in every setting and over anchor-based transduction where few classes are present.

</details>

Abstract: Backward-compatible retrieval enables a new encoder to query the gallery that an old model has already encoded, so that a cross-modal retrieval system can be upgraded without re-encoding billions of items. Existing compatible methods either fit the old query and stay near the accuracy of the old model, or run the old and the new encoder for every query. We propose VIBRA, which matches relevance on the old gallery, corrects the errors of a single model with the barycenter of several views, which is itself a query in the old space, and trains the new encoder to inherit this consensus, so that models absent at deployment still shape every query. We show that the barycenter gains margin over the average of its views exactly when their hardest irrelevant items differ, and we characterize the share of this gain that one encoder can reach. On five benchmarks with five upgrades among six CLIP, MetaCLIP and SigLIP2 backbones, VIBRA is the best compatible method in all 50 settings, improving Recall@1 by up to 5.6 points over the strongest baseline and by up to 18.0 points over the old model, while adding less than 1 ms to the latency of the new encoder.

</details>


### [101] [Weight Space Programming for Vision Language Model Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23615)
*Avinash Goutham Aluguvelly*

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: In weight-space programming, a language model edits a network's weights directly, without gradients. We ask what a successful repair of this kind relies on. We plant faults in the weights of two vision-language models, SmolVLM-256M and LFM2-VL-450M, and check each edit made by a coding agent against the original weights. Without being told where to look, the agent finds rescaled rows in the projector and noise in the vision encoder of both models, infers the unknown scale factors, restores most or all of the rescaled weights to their exact original values, and returns accuracy to near its original level; programs that it writes from such weights repair unseen checkpoints of the same model without feedback. These repairs depend on a trace of how the faults were computed. The original weights have 16-bit precision, and faults computed in 32-bit arithmetic leave low bits that only the damaged weights carry. Rounding those bits away leaves each model's answers to our test questions unchanged, and the agent, unaided, then restores no weight exactly: on SmolVLM it no longer finds the damage, and in a single session on LFM2-VL its only gain comes from rescaling answer-token embeddings, which changes how often the model answers yes. Marking the damaged weights again in their low bits brings the edits back to them. Rounded weights still hold statistical evidence of the rescaling, in the distribution of stored values and in row norms, and one sentence in the prompt that names such a statistic directs the agent's edits to the damaged rows on both models. Swapped rows or columns, which leave the set of stored values unchanged, are not found unaided on either model within our session budgets. We recommend that studies of weight repair report the number format of the weights and include a rounded control.

</details>


### [102] [Isthmus: Bridging Supervision Islands Restores Uncertainty and Stability in One-Step Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23616)
*Luming Yang,Siqing Li,Haoxian Liu,Zhenyu Bu,Jiazheng Zhu,Alper Yilmaz*

Abstract: One-step flow maps are increasingly used as posterior samplers, where the point of sampling is the uncertainty that the samples carry. We find that the one-step sampler loses this uncertainty and becomes less stable. Compared with multi-step samples of the same network, its samples lose variance and decorrelate. Supervising the one-step jump directly makes this worse; the samples collapse further toward the conditional mean and break down when training is extended. We trace both failures to the locations where training places its pairs of start and end times. Only pairs on the diagonal of the time triangle receive targets from data, and the pair used for one-step sampling is either left unsupervised or supervised on an island, a set of pairs that the self-consistency identity does not connect to the diagonal, where the objective neither determines the one-step map nor anchors its training. We bridge the island with an isthmus, which places 5% of the training pairs along the whole edge of the triangle that joins the one-step pair to the diagonal. The error of the one-step map is then bounded by the self-consistency residual along this edge. With the network, the loss and the inference code unchanged and at no added cost, the isthmus improves one-step sampling across several flow-map objectives, in MRI reconstruction, face inpainting, and unconditional generation on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet 32x32. On brain MRI, it restores one-step calibration to 0.96 and the correlation with multi-step samples to 0.93; both vary far less across seeds, and the agreement holds when training is extended to three times its length.

</details>

Abstract: An anatomical pose provides interpretable joint structure, but no fixed set of physical landmarks can fully specify a person's movement. We introduce PRISM, a complementary representation that characterizes repetitive movement using tracked masks and dense optical flow. Torso-relative flow measures intensity, frame-wise mask variation measures spatial extent, signed-flow recurrence estimates frequency, and persistent sliding-window geometry describes the rhythmicity of the movement. Across 1,890 synthetic conditions, the measurements recover controlled speed, amplitude, and frequency while exposing observation limits. An idealized hand-motion experiment demonstrates variation omitted by identical exact COCO-17 trajectories; controlled corruption experiments separately examine measurement sensitivity. Audits of two pose methods on the same data cohort and 36,930 external skeleton clips document incomplete observation and dependence on processing choices. In 1,404 annotated complex stereotypical motor movement bouts from six autistic participants, total flow converges with pose motion, while matched-coordinate examples and repeated-session profiles show differences in intensity, extent, frequency, and rhythmicity within behavior labels. A separate VLM comparison evaluates quantitative frequency estimation, and a profile-conditioned decoder links the measurements to illustrative skeletal movements. PRISM connects numerical profiles to visible examples, providing an interpretable way to compare how the same behavior is expressed beyond its label or event count.

</details>

Abstract: The pursuit of improved performance in deep learning has often been accompanied by increasing architectural complexity and computational demands. We explore an alternative direction: achieving more with less through structured supervision. We introduce LightSEAD, a lightweight SE(3)-aware keypoint detector initialised with calibrated Harris pseudo-labels and enriched through viewpoint-aware and appearance-aware supervision and selective knowledge distillation. LightSEAD comprises only six convolutional layers and 84,289 learnable parameters, achieving competitive repeatability across HPatches, KITTI 06 and 4Seasons while demonstrating its downstream applicability to relative pose estimation on MegaDepth-1500. Further optimised for mixed-precision inference, LightSEAD Coarse achieves approximately 909 FPS on an NVIDIA RTX 4090 and 139 FPS on a Jetson Orin Nano Super.

Abstract: Synthetic eye data are critical for rapid development of eye tracking (ET) in AR/VR applications. However, existing synthetic eye generators are largely image-centric and lack a high-quality underlying 3D anatomy of the eye, which limits their control mainly to the gaze and provides no anatomical labels. To address this problem, we propose EyeDiT, an anatomy-grounded, multi-modal 3D eye generation and simulation model trained with optical coherence tomography (OCT), labeled anatomy point clouds and near-eye images. Our pipeline first trains a Multi-Modal Anatomy Variational Autoencoder (VAE), which encodes an anatomy-aware latent representation from all input modalities. Then, our Eye Latent DiT is trained to generate latent samples conditioned on the optical axis, the pupil position and radius, and the subject identity. Together with Gaussian decoders for novel-view simulation, our model enables control over the pupil, subject identity and viewpoint for data synthesis. We show that our model outperforms previous eye image synthesis models in novel-view synthesis (24.9 against 21.8 dB PSNR from a single view), and that a public ET neural network trained on our synthetic data shows a smaller sim-to-real gap than when trained on data from other generators (3.5$^\circ$ vs. 8.4$^\circ$ gaze error), approaches a network trained on real data, and further improves ET performance when combined with real data (2.3$^\circ$ vs. 2.6$^\circ$). More information is available on the project page: https://lyhan12.github.io/eyedit

</details>


### [106] [Image-Condition-Controlled Diffusion Network with Large-Scale CT Pretraining for CT-Free Attenuation Correction in Dynamic Cardiac PET](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23672)

Abstract: Positron emission tomography (PET) provides quantitative information on myocardial perfusion. In dynamic PET, tracer kinetics are tracked through a series of reconstructed time frames. Reliable attenuation correction is essential for accurate measurements. Clinical attenuation maps ($μ$-maps) are typically derived from computed tomography (CT), which adds radiation exposure and may introduce PET-CT misregistration due to respiratory and cardiac motion. Moreover, supervised CT-free methods rely on paired non-attenuation-corrected (NAC) PET and CT data, which are scarce, whereas large-scale unpaired CT data are more readily available. To address these challenges, we propose Large-scale Unpaired CT-Informed Diffusion (LUCID), a framework that transfers anatomical priors learned from 10,000 chest CT volumes in the public CT-RATE dataset to $μ$-map synthesis for dynamic cardiac $^{82}$Rb PET. First, a convolutional neural network (CNN) predicts an initial $μ$-map from each NAC PET frame. Second, the CT-pretrained diffusion backbone refines a low-noise perturbation of the CNN prediction through a single denoising step, conditioned on the corresponding NAC PET frame via ControlNet. Finally, frame-wise predictions are averaged voxel-wise to obtain the final $μ$-map. We further introduce the voxel-wise temporal standard deviation (TSD) to quantify inter-frame variability without requiring a reference $μ$-map. We evaluated LUCID on an in-house dataset of 102 patients and found that it achieved the best quantitative accuracy while maintaining competitive inter-frame consistency. Ablation studies showed that refinement with a diffusion backbone pretrained on in-house CT data from the training patients was less accurate than the CNN prediction alone, highlighting the importance of large-scale CT pretraining.

</details>

Abstract: Degradations in smart-glasses images obscure small text and fine-grained structures needed by vision-language models (VLMs) for visual assistance. We introduce an high-resolution (HR)-referenced semantic evaluation protocol that compares VLM outputs on restored images with those on corresponding HR references. Our evaluation reveals two failure modes: generative SR can synthesize misleading details, whereas restoration-oriented SR can leave critical evidence blurred or ambiguous. These findings motivate improving faithful detail recovery in restoration-oriented SR, where a key training challenge is to provide sufficiently difficult inputs while retaining recoverable visual information. We propose ReDiC (Recoverability-Difficulty Curriculum), which combines a model-independent recoverability proxy with model-relative restoration difficulty to adapt training degradation severity. ReDiC increases severity when inputs are recoverable and restoration is easy, maintains challenging but recoverable conditions, and reduces severity when estimated recoverability is low. It modifies only training degradation generation, leaving the SR architecture and reconstruction objective unchanged. Across NAFNet, SwinIR, and MambaIR, ReDiC improves average PSNR by approximately 2.5 dB and HR-referenced semantic scores by about 3 points over the corresponding baselines on synthetic benchmarks, with further gains confirmed by LLM-based and human evaluations on 200 real smart-glasses captures.

</details>

Abstract: Powder X-ray diffraction (PXRD) is the primary structural evidence for covalent organic frameworks (COFs) and many other materials. Yet decades of measured patterns exist only as images in published figures. Automatic recovery is difficult, because PXRD figures vary widely in format, from a single curve to crowded stacks with labels, insets, and neighboring plots that challenge traditional computer vision. This work presents a pipeline in which a vision large language model (LLM) interprets the layout of each figure, locating the diffraction plot and its curves and sketching a coarse trace of each, while classical computer vision (CV) refines every trace to pixel-level accuracy. Applied to 3,954 COF articles, the pipeline produces 11,445 calibrated diffraction curves, released in the open database COF-PEAK. Compared with a hand digitization of 60 panels, extracted peak positions differ by a median of only 0.07 degrees. Whereas papers usually report only a few peak angles, which limits further analysis, the digitized curves enable tracking how peak prominence, a possible marker of crystallinity, changes with synthesis conditions. Vision LLMs and classical CV can thus turn such figures into reusable experimental data.

</details>
