Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) struggle with accurately capturing camera-object relations, especially for object orientation, camera viewpoint, and camera shots. This stems from the fact that existing MLLMs are trained on images with limited diverse camera-object relations and corresponding textual descriptions. To address this, we propose a synthetic generation pipeline to create large-scale 3D visual instruction datasets. Our framework takes 3D assets as input and uses rendering and diffusion-based image generation models to create photorealistic images preserving precise camera-object relations. Additionally, large language models (LLMs) are used to generate text prompts for guiding visual instruction tuning and controlling image generation. We create Ultimate3D, a large-scale 3D-grounded 2D visual instruction dataset. Ultimate3D consists of 240K VQAs with precise camera-object annotations, and corresponding benchmark. MLLMs fine-tuned on our proposed dataset outperform commercial models by a large margin, achieving an average accuracy improvement of 29.7% on camera-object relation recognition tasks. Our code, dataset, and benchmark will contribute to broad MLLM applications.

</details>


### [261] [Edge-Efficient Joint RAW Image Compression and Restoration for Smart Wearable Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24805)

Abstract: Smart wearable devices, such as augmented reality glasses, increasingly rely on cameras to understand and interact with their surroundings. However, their compact sensors often capture noisy RAW images, while limited compute and power make high-quality on-device restoration difficult. This creates an emerging need to reduce both local computation and transmission cost without sacrificing image quality. Two common solutions are to offload restoration or to compress the raw data before transmission. The first makes RAW transmission costly; the second reduces the payload but can spend bitrate preserving sensor noise. We address this fundamental mismatch with an asymmetric framework for joint RAW compression and restoration that directly optimizes the transmitted representation for clean image reconstruction. A lightweight on-device encoder compresses noisy RAW measurements into a bitstream, while a higher-capacity backend decoder jointly denoises and demosaics the encoded representation. We use knowledge distillation to train a smaller encoder for tighter compute budgets. Experiments on real and synthetic data show improved rate-restoration performance over strong cascaded compression-restoration pipelines and learned compression baselines. On SIDD, our method improves PSNR by more than 1.5 dB over cascaded baselines at approximately 0.25 bits per pixel. We further validate the method on real 12 MP RAW images captured with commercial smart glasses and measure sender-side latency on both smartphone and smart-glasses hardware. The distilled encoder achieves a 27.6$\times$ NPU inference speedup over on-device NAFNet for 256$\times$256 output patches. The code is available at https://github.com/coolsyn2000/CRAW

</details>


### [262] [Gaspard: Expressive unified speech and gesture synthesis with continuous autoregressive flow-matching and decoupled classifier-free guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24862)

Abstract: Human communication involves highly coordinated multimodal signals such as speech and co-speech gestures. Existing approaches for generating both modalities commonly follow a cascaded pipeline, where speech is generated first and subsequently used to drive gesture generation; speech generation is therefore independent of gestures. We introduce Gaspard, a unified speech--gesture generation model based on continuous autoregressive generation with next-step flow-matching predictions, and trained on Seamless Interaction, a large-scale multimodal dataset. The heterogeneous temporal properties of speech and gesture motivate modality-specific prediction heads and decoupled classifier-free guidance over the two generation streams. This guidance rebalances the influence of speech and gesture at inference time, improving expressivity while mitigating dynamics collapse in continuous autoregressive gesture generation. Objective and perceptual evaluations show that Gaspard is a strong gesture generation model against unimodal baselines and that it produces highly synchronous speech and gestures compared to a matched cascaded system.

</details>


### [263] [ShadowTransfer: A Geographic Transfer Benchmark for Overhead Shadow Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24904)

Abstract: Overhead aerial imagery feeds dense-prediction pipelines that are routinely trained in one geographic region and deployed in another, yet no benchmark measures how shadow detection models transfer across regions. Existing overhead shadow datasets either lack a geographic transfer protocol or rely on automatically generated labels. We introduce ShadowTransfer, a benchmark of 4,500 human-verified shadow masks across three U.S. cities at two native resolutions, paired with a leave-one-city-out evaluation protocol. Under vanilla training, holding out the test city costs 3.5 to 7.8 mIoU across four architectures, the gap remains significant when the training set grows from 225 to 900 images, and six of eight adaptation methods degrade more than vanilla training on average. We propose a three-probe framework that diagnoses where and how the transfer breaks: the failure operates in feature space, beyond the reach of pixel-histogram matching, shows up as a confidence collapse on shadow pixels while background confidence stays intact, and is recoverable by retraining different architectural components depending on the model: encoder for some, decoder for others. These findings replicate on INRIA building footprints, suggesting they describe geographic transfer beyond shadows. Guided by the diagnosis, we build Spectral Information Bottleneck (SIB), a plug-in module with one component per finding. Across the 9 (architecture, held-out city) cells, SIB has the smallest mean gap to the within-city upper bound of the ten methods we evaluate (6.42 mIoU), level with FDA. The benchmark, LOCO protocol, code, and trained models are released to support reproducible evaluation of geographic transfer in overhead dense prediction.

</details>

Abstract: Self-supervised learning has produced powerful visual representations, yet learning strong dense features remains challenging. While consistency between augmented views provides strong image-level supervision, extending this principle to local visual content is less straightforward. Existing approaches often match individual patches across views or identify coherent regions, introducing uncertainties in correspondence estimation and assumptions about how visual content should be grouped. We introduce ReCoCo (Regional Composition Consistency), a principled approach that extends cross-view consistency to the regional level without requiring such correspondences or semantic grouping. ReCoCo represents the content shared between augmented views through compositions of their patch-level predictions over learned visual prototypes. By aligning these compositions, the model learns from regions containing multiple visual concepts without requiring them to be separated beforehand. ReCoCo integrates naturally with teacher-student self-distillation and introduces no additional learned parameters. Extensive experiments demonstrate substantial improvements in frozen-feature segmentation, clustering, and regional retrieval while maintaining competitive image-level recognition, alleviating the trade-off between dense and global representation quality.

</details>

Abstract: Agentic image restoration composes operations to handle mixed degradations, yet its toolbox often requires storing multiple restoration networks. We propose PrismRestore, which constructs a compact bank of callable restoration actions from a single frozen all-in-one model. Our approach builds on task-dependent yet overlapping channel responses in the shared model. An offline task-prior search uses activation and gradient statistics to group channels with similar response profiles, then searches task-specific modulation coefficients over non-representative channels while leaving representative channels unmodulated. The resulting prior bank enables explicit action selection and composition without additional restoration backbones or task-specific adapter modules. A state-conditioned policy learns to compose these actions using Group Relative Policy Optimization. Its objective separately normalizes fidelity and perceptual feedback within groups of trajectories sampled for the same input, and combines terminal evaluation with return-to-go derived from stepwise quality changes. Experiments on synthetic composite-degradation and real-world multi-task benchmarks show improvements over the evaluated all-in-one baselines and restoration quality competitive with multi-model agents. PrismRestore achieves these results with a single restoration backbone and faster inference than the evaluated agentic baselines.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a Unified Law for Visual Tasks, which could be further divided into Four Laws of Computer Vision, with the goal of enabling a single framework or method to uniformly train on and perform nearly all visual tasks. Our starting point is an inspiration and hypothesis: if the world information is encoded into image, which we call World Snapshots, then image processing is world processing, and image generation is World Snapshot generation. Furthermore, if all visual and visualization tasks can be reformulated as generation tasks, then a unified method, World Snapshot Models (WSMs), can perform the vast majority of visual tasks, much as large language models have largely unified natural language processing. This paper validates these laws across more than 1,000 trained and evaluated visual tasks (defined by distinct input-output mappings) and presents a unified training framework for future visual-task research and applications.

</details>

Abstract: Generating physically based rendering (PBR) texture maps for a 3D mesh from a single reference image is highly ambiguous, as the observed appearance entangles material identity, surface reflectance, and illumination. While current generative methods produce realistic albedo maps, they still struggle to infer accurate metallic and roughness attributes for relighting. We present UltraTex, a cascaded multi-view framework that moves beyond appearance matching by integrating physical knowledge into multi-view diffusion-based PBR generation. UltraTex first jointly predicts albedo and world-space shading normals to generate more detailed albedo maps, and then predicts metallic-roughness (MR) maps conditioned on the generated albedo, reference image, and material semantic priors. To inject material semantics, a lightweight adapter transforms hidden-layer features of a vision-language model (VLM) into intrinsic material representation tokens, providing part-level knowledge anchors for MR estimation. To model material-light interaction, photometric representation alignment guides intermediate diffusion features toward ground-truth material renderings under controlled lighting. This makes metallic and roughness variations observable through highlight intensity, specular sharpness, and local reflectance contrast, providing stronger guidance for fine-grained material prediction without explicit lighting supervision. Extensive experiments show that UltraTex generates high-quality textures and outperforms existing open-source and commercial methods in visual fidelity, material accuracy, and physical plausibility.

</details>

### [268] [Chorus: Shared N-gram Embeddings with Layer-wise Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23386)
*Stephen Chung,Wenyu Du*

Main category: cs.CL

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Language models can gain capacity at little computational cost from n-gram embedding tables: large tables of learned vectors for short sequences of consecutive tokens, which are looked up and added to the model's input or to its intermediate layers. Recent designs commonly use a separate n-gram embedding table for each layer, which makes the tables costly in memory and prevents layers from sharing what they learn. Sharing a single table, however, gives every layer the same embedding and leaves no room for layer-wise preferences, and it performs poorly in practice. We propose Chorus, in which one set of n-gram embedding tables serves the whole network and every layer reads it in its own way. Each layer maps the refined embedding through its own projection layer, after rescaling its components by factors computed from its current hidden state. Chorus matches the quality of existing n-gram methods with 87% smaller tables: with 1.2B table parameters it predicts text better than FactorEngram and DeepSeek's Engram do with 9.6B. It outperforms Engram, TN-gram and FactorEngram in every setting where we compared them, across table sizes from 20M to 9.6B parameters. We also show that the recently proposed memory-efficient optimizer Ember works well with Chorus, saving 99.2% of table-optimizer memory compared with AdamW without degrading training performance. These results enable further scaling of n-gram embedding tables along the table-size axis by reducing memory use and allowing shared knowledge to be reused across layers.

</details>


### [269] [Prioritize, Don't Decide: What We Learned Auditing a Deployed Human-in-the-Loop CV Sorter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23359)
*Hadi Mohammadi,Jeroen Sparla,Alexandros Paliouras,Georg Krempl,Robert A. Bagheri*

Abstract: A single academic vacancy can draw hundreds of applications, and the first screening round consumes most of a committee's time. We describe a CV Priority Sorter deployed at a national platform for academic vacancies and integrated with its vacancy database. It proposes a reading order for a pile of CVs and leaves every decision to the committee, pairing a frozen multilingual transformer encoder with a per-session linear model that retrains after each label and is discarded when the session ends. It works: on a pool of 300 synthetic CVs, a committee reading in the tool's order reaches 95% of the relevant candidates after half the pile, where an arbitrary order needs almost all of it. This paper is about what we found when we audited that claim, rather than about the system itself. Three findings changed the product. The pool turned out to be sortable by document length alone, which makes precision-style metrics on it uninformative; regenerating it under a shared length budget removes that shortcut and leaves the reading-depth result standing. The four-level agreement score such systems report, ours included, is a binary probability cut at fixed thresholds, so it follows the classifier's confidence rather than its ranking and falls as labels accumulate. And uncertainty sampling, the obvious way to implement a "suggest the next CV" button, leaves model quality intact but raises the committee's total reading load above the no-label baseline, because the CVs it asks about are the ones that would otherwise be skipped. A fourth result shows where to look next: a hosted LLM embedding model lifts zero-label ordinal discrimination from 0.66 to 0.80 under length control, then stops mattering once the session model has two dozen labels; a local cross-encoder matches that cold-start gain without sending data out. We report the fixes each finding forced.

</details>

Abstract: Linguistic formality is one of the dimensions of stylistic variation in speech; however, simply measuring its level does not allow us to determine whether the chosen mode of communication is appropriate in a given situation. The aim of this article is to present the concept of a formal adequacy index (based on Polish-language material), which enables a quantitative assessment of how well the formality of a text matches the expectations arising from the communicative context. The proposed approach is based on the separate modelling of observed formality, determined on the basis of the linguistic features of the text, and expected formality, determined on the basis of the characteristics of the communicative situation. Adequacy is assessed by comparing these two measures, taking into account contextual tolerance thresholds, which may differ for a lack of formality and an excess of formality. The potential applications of the index are illustrated by an analysis of Polish-language emails containing apologies for being late, written by human language users or generated by a large language model. The implementation presented here is preliminary in nature and requires further calibration and empirical validation. The main contribution of this article is the proposal of a general methodological framework for the quantitative study of formal adequacy in various communicative contexts.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents achieve state-of-the-art performance across domains through chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. Despite their ubiquitous use, little is understood about the relationship between the externalized trace and the model's actual computation. We address this by investigating how LLM agents make decisions in iterative games with incomplete information. Using a series of mechanistic interpretability techniques, we find that the model forms and iteratively revises an internal representation of the game throughout the CoT. Once the model prematurely commits to an incorrect belief, it rarely recovers. This failure is manifested in both the literal trace and the layer activations. Ultimately, as multi-agent systems are more prevalent, our work opens the door to a more focused diagnosis of their strategic reasoning errors. For illustrative purposes, we offer an interactive visualizer of our methodology on selected sample traces at https://interp-self.github.io/gametrace/

</details>

Abstract: The criminal legal system is composed of many complex decision points, such as which charges are pursued, how bond is determined, plea deal negotiations, and sentencing. Given the profound impact of such decisions, there is a growing need to increase the transparency within this system. While prior machine learning approaches have focused on modeling single decisions (e.g., recidivism), this paper proposes a transformer-based time series model to forecast the outcome of the next proceeding in a case (e.g., charges accepted, bond set, plea deal made, etc.). By modeling a case as an event sequence, we enable a more nuanced analysis to support better oversight and understanding of decision making. We conduct an empirical analysis of thousands of cases using a newly constructed dataset derived from publicly available court records of a mid-sized U.S. city. We fit models that combine textual data from clerk notes with structured information about the defendant, prior charges, and courtroom. Our transformer model achieves $.57$ micro-F1 on a 20-class multi-label task, a significant improvement over several baselines and comparable to a GRU trained on the same input. We demonstrate how the model can be used to provide insights into decision-making and help court observers identify emerging issues within the jurisdiction studied.

</details>

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are widely used for question answering but are prone to hallucinations, yet the underlying causes, particularly the role of factual prevalence in training data, remain underexplored. In this work, we study this through academic authorship attribution, hypothesizing that hallucinations are more likely for less prevalent papers. Using citation count as a proxy for factual prevalence, we curate a dataset of 1.92 million papers across eight scientific disciplines with stratified samples by citation level. Experiments on three state-of-the-art LLMs confirm our hypothesis, revealing a robust inverse relationship between hallucination rate and citation count across all models and disciplines, with hallucination rates exceeding 98% for rarely cited papers and falling below 37% for highly cited ones. Our in-depth analysis further shows that these failures primarily reflect retrieval limitations rather than knowledge absence, and hidden-state probing of open-source models shows that factual prevalence is encoded in pre-generation representations, providing convergent mechanistic evidence for the prevalence-hallucination relationship. The code and data are available at https://github.com/quinnzhong1/dreaming-up-authors.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated through their ability to predict future outcomes, implicitly assuming that predictive success reflects an understanding of the underlying system. However, accurate predictions can often be achieved without recovering the latent mechanisms that generate observable outcomes. To examine this assumption, we introduce LUCID, a benchmark that evaluates whether models can infer latent market structures from event descriptions and historical financial observations. Models generate event-induced causal graphs representing inferred market structure and are evaluated through a reconstruction-based framework. Our evaluation of 6 open-weight and proprietary LLMs shows that adding their generated graphs generally reduces reconstruction quality compared with using historical observations alone, although a few metrics improve. These findings suggest that inferring informative, event-specific market structures from observable evidence remains challenging for the evaluated LLMs.

</details>

Abstract: Researchers are increasingly using large language models (LLMs) to validate research questions. We find that LLMs make open research questions sound partly studied and overstate what related papers establish. Models recognize most of these overstatements when asked to check the papers' full texts separately. On 40 questions drawn from newly accepted papers, GPT-6-Astra correctly judges 98% to be unstudied, but states that status plainly in only 8% of ordinary answers. All six frontier systems show reporting gaps of 30-90 percentage points. Across 22 models given identical papers, higher capability is associated with more accurate judgments and larger reporting gaps. This reporting failure is asymmetric: models correctly identify studied questions in 88-100% of ordinary answers when we supply the answering papers. To correct this, we build a fixed answer from each model's judgment and separate checks of cited papers. This raises Astra's clear reporting of open status from 8% to 95%, with improvements in all six systems. Finally, we predict reviewers' novelty grades by combining several models' written ratings with a rule learned from graded examples, reaching the performance range reported by Think-Probe-Respond. Unlike TPR, our approach needs no access to model weights or internal activations, so it also works with closed-weight frontier models.

</details>

Abstract: As governments and organizations adopt foundation models for public services and workplace productivity, sensitive citizen, employee, and administrative text may enter LLM workflows through inference, retrieval, fine-tuning, or local model development. We examine whether text anonymizers transfer to new deployment settings by extending RAT-Bench to Malaysia using local microdata, Malaysian PII formats, and culturally grounded transcripts in Malaysian English and Bahasa Malaysia. We evaluate NER- and LLM-based anonymizers using an LLM attacker that infers attributes from anonymized text, measuring both re-identification success and text utility. Across Malaysian English and Bahasa Malaysia, the two LLM anonymizers provide the strongest explicit privacy-utility trade-offs, reducing Easy/Hard re-identification risk to 23-29% while preserving BLEU scores of 0.77-0.94. Non-LLM tools show sharper failure modes, either preserving utility while leaving high residual risk or reducing risk through severe over-redaction, especially in Bahasa Malaysia. These findings suggest that anonymizers should be evaluated against deployment-relevant languages, identifier formats, and cultural contexts before being relied on in public-sector, workplace, or local AI pipelines.

</details>

Abstract: Asking a model to check retrieved text can also tell it which source to trust. We introduce FutureBench, a renewable framework for evaluating knowledge-context conflict using timestamped questions, controlled passage triplets, and no-context probes. Across 3,629 questions and six models, Verify (a prompt combining checking with passage priority) raises false-answer adoption in all five models tested on pre-cutoff events, mainly by displacing answers that were correct under Baseline, which allows both passage evidence and model knowledge. On post-cutoff events, only o4-mini increases adoption (+13.7 percentage points). A four-prompt comparison on 902 post-cutoff questions finds that passage priority without checking raises adoption, while moving to Verify lowers it in all five models. Adversarial-Aware, which warns of misleading passages and prioritizes model knowledge, restores 82.5-98.9% of displaced pre-cutoff answers with under one point of change in true-passage accuracy. FutureBench supports repeated evaluation on fixed cohorts and extension to new events as models change.

</details>

Abstract: Speculative decoding is widely used to speed up generation in large language models (LLMs), with a small draft model proposing tokens that one forward pass of the target model verifies. Draft models that read the target's hidden states, such as DFlash and EAGLE-3, typically inherit the target's hidden width. We show that the draft's whole residual stream can run at half or a quarter of that width while largely preserving acceptance. For DFlash, which borrows the target's vocabulary projection, the trained output adapter folds into it, giving one narrower projection with logits unchanged up to floating-point rounding. We test two drafter families, DFlash and EAGLE-3, on two targets, Qwen3-8B (NVFP4) and GPT-OSS-20B (MXFP4), in SGLang on a B200 and a DGX Spark (GB10). At batch size 1 and temperature 0, narrowing the draft to half or a quarter width speeds up end-to-end decoding by 1.06-1.12x on B200 and 1.13-1.44x on GB10. The speedup holds at larger batch sizes. From batch 32 to the largest batch each GPU fits, narrowing reaches up to 1.21x on both GPUs, and no narrow draft is slower than full width.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) offer a flexible alternative to autoregressive models, enabling any-order infilling. While most existing decoding methods treat decoded tokens as fixed, some recent models allow previously decoded tokens to be revised only by replacing them with alternative tokens, a paradigm we refer to as replacement-only editing (ROE). Although ROE can correct individual tokens, it is inefficient for sequence shifts caused by missing or extra tokens and struggles when errors occur across many positions. To address these limitations, we propose LED (Levenshtein Editing for Diffusion Language Models), a post-training framework that extends ROE with insertion and deletion, enabling Levenshtein-style refinement of generated sequences. It uses two training designs. First, it reconstructs edit supervision on the fly during supervised fine-tuning using longest-common-subsequence alignment, enabling insertion and deletion learning without manually annotated labels. Second, it adopts multi-stage training, where the state from one stage becomes the input to the next, exposing the model to its earlier errors and enabling it to correct them. When applied to the 16B-parameter LLaDA2.1-Mini Base, LED raises the average score across twelve benchmarks from 54.86 to 57.10 under confidence-guided decoding while increasing tokens per forward from 6.14 to 7.05, whereas under confidence-free decoding it raises the average score from 27.52 to 47.19. We further show that LED achieves higher pass@k at large k than thresholded decoding without editing, demonstrating improved generation diversity and revealing the diversity potential of DLMs without confidence guidance. LED also improves robustness to large block sizes, raising the average score from 36.97 to 51.34 at block size 128. Together, these results suggest that LED supports more robust self-correction in DLMs.

</details>


### [280] [Coverage Governs Accuracy: When to Expand the Lexicon and When to Fall Back in Low-Resource Manchu OCR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23900)

Abstract: Character-level OCR for low-resource Manchu often produces near-miss spelling errors. Lexicon-constrained decoding can correct such errors with lexical knowledge, but a fixed dictionary cannot cover the many unseen word forms produced by agglutinative word formation. We study whether morphological expansion alleviates this limitation, and how the expansion size and confidence fallback affect recognition. We segment the training transcriptions with a dictionary-based stem list, extract roots and suffix chains from them, generate about 510k candidates, select the expansion size through frequency ranking on the validation set, and decompose accuracy under hard lexicon constraints into the product of coverage and in-lexicon precision. Across three CRNN training seeds, adding 250k candidates to a base dictionary of about 29k words raises the type accuracy on unseen combinations of seen roots from 63.6% to 86.3%, and overall token accuracy from 82.4% to 84.2%. Adding all candidates lowers overall token accuracy to 81.5%, showing that the coverage gain comes with a loss of in-lexicon precision. A further comparison shows that the base dictionary with confidence fallback performs best overall among the CRNN systems tested, reaching 76.1% type and 85.5% token accuracy and outperforming the expanded lexicon both with and without fallback. A Transformer recognizer shows similar trends. These results indicate that morphological expansion relieves the constraint of a fixed dictionary, but a larger lexicon does not guarantee better recognition; in the present low-resource Manchu OCR setting, a selective combination of reliable lexical constraints and open recognition is more effective.

</details>

Abstract: CoT-style LLM recommenders generate a natural-language preference note as a chain-of-thought prefix to ranking, but the note is trusted without verification, and the ranker never consults behaviorally similar users. We propose ReflectRec, organized around a preference-to-history round-trip verifier whose recall score serves as an operational signal of preference-to-history recoverability, enabling conditional regeneration for low-recoverability preferences and selection among regeneration attempts. Around this verifier, we wire a semantic Stage-1 prompt, conditional regeneration, and Forward-Look retrieval that injects future watches of behaviorally similar users as a collaborative hint. On MovieLens-100K and MovieLens-1M, the full pipeline outperforms CoT4Rec-ICL by +16.25 / +4.46 pp Hit@10, with positive gains across all ValScore buckets and three LLM backbones (Qwen3-30B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Llama-3.3-70B). We also observe a similar verifier-guided regeneration pattern on Amazon Electronics.

</details>

*Tianxiang Chen,Jingyuan Zhou,Guoyao Yang,Kaidi Yang*

Abstract: Large language models can infer and follow specific rules through in-context learning (ICL), yet may fail to remain faithful to provided evidence, giving rise to out-of-context (OOC) hallucination. Existing work has largely investigated the underlying mechanisms of ICL and OOC hallucination along separate lines. However, this separation obscures the mechanistic basis for these seemingly opposing outcomes. In this work, we study this contrast through the unified view of functional roles in dynamic context. The key observation is that role information can be internally represented without reliably constraining output. We derive a first-order decomposition to characterize the role effect, showing that its substantial influence depends on two necessary conditions, namely sufficient contribution magnitude and coherent signed aggregation. Causal analysis further shows that role span contributions affect key token choices involving response structure, content selection, and factual values. We further show that role-consistent choices depend on sufficient role span support at these key decisions, while failing choices can be reversed when this support overcomes the deficit. These findings support a common mechanistic view of ICL and OOC hallucination: each functional role defines the pattern of output decisions over which role span contributions must remain effective, and differences in this organization account for the seemingly opposing outcomes.

</details>

Abstract: We present a pilot study characterising the effect of post-training quantisation on multilingual machine translation quality and inference cost for three African languages - Hausa, Swahili, and Yoruba - across three model families: a small general-purpose LLM (Qwen2.5-1.5B), a larger multilingual instruction-tuned LLM (Aya-23-8B), and a dedicated multilingual MT model (NLLB-200-distilled-1.3B). Evaluating on the FLORES-200 devtest set (1,012 sentences per direction) under FP16, bitsandbytes INT8, and NF4 INT4 precisions on consumer-grade NVIDIA T4 GPUs, we find: (i) the specialised MT model outperforms both LLMs by 32.7-43.3 chrF on the Hausa and Swahili directions, and by 12.9-24.7 chrF on Yoruba, despite being roughly a sixth the size of the 8B instruction-tuned LLM; (ii) post-training quantisation preserves aggregate translation quality within approximately 1 chrF across all model families and language directions, with a single worst case of 2.19 chrF; (iii) on consumer GPUs, INT8 and INT4 quantisation provide substantial memory savings (49-61% at INT4) but do not reduce inference latency relative to FP16 - in fact, latency increases by 1.3x to 5.3x; (iv) a clear performance gradient tracking training-resource availability is visible across languages (Swahili > Hausa > Yoruba). These findings motivate a research agenda focused on hardware-aware compression and error-type analysis beyond aggregate metrics for low-resource deployment.

</details>

Abstract: Laws are increasingly turned into programs that decide cases, such as whether a firm was a small or medium enterprise (SME) on a given date, and large language models (LLMs) now write them. The programs are usually graded against answers written by someone who read the same law, so a program can score well by sharing the grader's reading rather than by getting the law right. LexTemporis grades them instead against records of what actually happened: 10,901 firm-years of SME status disclosed by Korean listed companies, with every version of the governing rules. An appendix adds comparison cases from US income tax and German hospital minimum volumes. The date changes outcomes mainly through provisions outside the main statutory text: one transitional provision raises agreement with the disclosures from 83-86% to 89-90%. The disclosures also test the author's reading of a contested provision, favouring it in 130 of 189 firm-years where readings diverge. Four LLMs wrote the rules, given different selections of the law. A pre-registered comparison finds no condition better across all four models. In exploratory analyses, supplying the right provisions looks better graded against the author's reading than against the records: the advantage shrinks for three models (for one, to between a quarter and three-fifths of its size), and per firm-year it is several times larger where the author's reading is wrong, partly by construction, since there reproducing that reading more faithfully must lose. Grading legal programs against the grader's own reading can overstate how well they work.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-hop Question Answering (MQA) requires large language models (LLMs) to retrieve and synthesize multiple pieces of evidence into a coherent reasoning path, which is essential for complex tasks. However, prior work on knowledge updating for MQA has largely focused on simple fact edits, overlooking a more challenging and realistic setting---\emph{Knowledge Injection}---in which the model must simultaneously accommodate both conflicting updates and entirely novel information. Under this setting, a hop-level answer is often wrong simply because the model trusts the wrong knowledge source: it clings to outdated parametric memory when the injected knowledge conflicts with it (K2), and it cannot answer when the required fact is entirely new (K3).
  To investigate this, we introduce NEXUS, the first benchmark tailored for multi-hop reasoning under Knowledge Injection, which explicitly covers both conflicting (K2) and incremental (K3) knowledge. We further propose MIRAGE, an efficient, training-free framework whose core component, the Correlation-aware Dual-path Reasoner (CDR), resolves such per-hop source-trust errors via P(True)-based confidence routing, complemented by a lightweight template-guided corrector for residual structural deviations. Extensive experiments across four LLM families and two benchmarks demonstrate that MIRAGE consistently improves both final-answer accuracy and hop-level reasoning accuracy, establishing a new state of the art in this challenging setting.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to create stories for children. Since bedtime stories can shape children's perceptions of social roles, this raises concerns about whether LLMs adapt narrative portrayals according to the intended recipient's gender. We conducted a large-scale study to examine gender stereotyping in LLM-generated bedtime stories. Using matched daughter/son prompts and a five-generation design, we analyze 62,873 retained stories from 16 LLMs across 35 country settings and 12 narrator roles. We introduce a multi-level evaluation framework combining context-adjusted lexical rate contrasts, relative stereotype alignment, narrator-recipient interaction analysis, human evaluation, and representation-level interventions to examine both the consistency and interpretability of these differences. Our results reveal systematic differences in narrative framing: son-directed stories emphasize higher agency and lower communion compared to daughter-directed stories. While narrator gender expresses limited moderation on these patterns, high sociability exhibits a more pronounced interaction. Human evaluations demonstrate consistent agreement, although correspondence with lexical measures varies across narrative dimensions. Additionally, hidden-state analyses reveal decodable recipient-gender information. These findings demonstrate persistent yet context-dependent representational disparities in LLM-generated children's stories and motivate narrative-specific bias evaluation beyond short-form benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: The growing demand for psychological counseling has motivated the development of LLM-based systems that support human counselors, but training such systems requires large-scale, high-quality dialogue data. Authentic counseling transcripts are difficult to collect and share because of privacy concerns, making synthetic dialogues an important alternative. However, existing synthetic datasets, despite covering diverse scenarios and client profiles, often consist of brief exchanges and fail to capture session-level counseling processes. We propose CounselTraj, a dialogue-act trajectory planning framework for expanding brief synthetic dialogues into process-aware, session-level psychological counseling dialogues. CounselTraj learns dialogue-act patterns from role-play counseling dialogues produced by human counselors, plans coordinated trajectories for clients and counselors, and uses them as flexible guidance for long-form dialogue generation. Experiments across three Chinese synthetic counseling datasets show that CounselTraj produces more complete and coherent sessions with higher-quality counselor interventions than both direct expansion and expansion with randomly sampled trajectories. Based on CounselTraj, we construct CounselTrajD, a dataset containing over 10K process-guided Chinese psychological counseling dialogues, and use it to train CounselTrajLM for counseling response generation. Evaluations at both the response and session levels show that CounselTrajLM outperforms publicly available Chinese counseling models in response quality, therapeutic understanding and intervention, and counseling-process coherence and progress. Our code and model are publicly available at https://github.com/UEC-InabaLab/CounselTraj.

</details>

Abstract: Automatic spelling correction must handle both keyboard typographical slips and cognitive or phonetic misspellings where writers approximate a word's pronunciation. Conventional spell checkers rely either on orthographic edit distance and keyboard proximity--which struggle when phonetic errors diverge by multiple characters--or on English-centric phonetic hashes such as Soundex and Metaphone. We present LipSpell, a unified, language-independent phonetic scoring engine paired with swappable language-specific G2P frontends, keyboard maps, and lexicons integrating: (1) a precomputed |P|x|P| phone-distance matrix from universal IPA distinctive features, (2) physical keyboard geometry, and (3) corpus unigram frequency priors, accelerated by a two-stage symmetric-delete and seven-class phonetic-code index. Across six English benchmarks (including 1,000 L2 errors from TOEFL-Spell), LipSpell outperforms Norvig and Hunspell on every dataset and beats Aspell on Norvig1 (78.52% vs. 75.56%), Holbrook (32.00% vs. 26.67%), and TOEFL-Spell (77.30% vs. 72.80%). Evaluated across six languages spanning five branches (Germanic, Romance, Slavic, Semitic, Turkic), three families (Indo-European, Afro-Asiatic, Turkic), and three scripts, LipSpell achieves strong fixed-weight transfer on Brazilian Portuguese (73.00% Top-1 on n=500 Wikipedia without NFD tolerance and 75.80% with NFD vs. 66.40% Norvig, 64.20% Hunspell, 64.00% Aspell) and Russian Cyrillic (85.67% vs. 78.50% Norvig). On 50/50 clean non-word splits with lexicon cleaning and weak-character indels, tuning yields strong gains over Norvig in Portuguese (86.97% vs. 76.75%), Turkish (81.67% vs. 79.05%), Hebrew Abjad (74.59% vs. 62.30%), and French (74.53% vs. 67.92%), while fixed weights remain optimal for English (80.37%) and Russian (85.67%).

</details>


### [290] [LoopCache: Accelerating Long-Context Self-Speculative Decoding for Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24301)
*Zizhuo Fu,Haochen Huang,Shengxuan Qiu,Linye Wei,Ziyu Tang,Runsheng Wang,Meng Li*

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Looped Transformers achieve large effective depth with compact models by repeatedly applying a weight-shared block across R recurrent steps, each maintaining a loop-specific key-value (KV) cache. To accelerate sequential loop execution, recent wave-style self-speculative methods like WaveFront Decoding (WFD) and LoopSpec pipeline draft and target tokens across loops in a single step. However, they face two critical limitations. First, they draft only at fixed loops and ignore intermediate prediction changes, wasting computation on stale trajectories. Second, batching tokens from different loops loads up to Rx the KV cache per step, severely amplifying memory traffic in long-context scenarios. To address these challenges, we propose LoopCache, an efficient self-speculative decoding framework for long-context Looped Transformers. Based on the correlation between prediction switches and output confidence, LoopCache introduces confidence-guided dynamic draft updating to refine drafts in flight across all intermediate loops. Furthermore, LoopCache employs a cyclic loop KV cache strategy that rotates through loop-specific KV caches, restricting per-step KV traffic to a single loop while batching tokens at the nearest matching cycle for lossless verification. By also selectively retaining independent KV caches for a few loop-sensitive shallow layers, LoopCache improves acceptance rates with negligible overhead. Experiments show that LoopCache achieves average 10.25x and 14.56x speedup over WFD and LoopSpec.

</details>

Abstract: Judgements of sexism are subjective and may vary across communities, yet sexism detection datasets usually reduce annotator judgements to a single majority label, obscuring disagreement and how models behave on contested cases. We introduce MuDAS, a dataset of YouTube comments in Saudi, Egyptian and Moroccan Arabic, each labelled by three native speakers of its dialect, with the individual annotations preserved. We use it to evaluate Arabic encoders fine-tuned with hard-label, soft-label and perspectivist objectives, GPT-5.6 Terra, and four Arabic-centric large language models. Model errors are more frequent on comments with annotator disagreement. Despite relatively close Macro-F1 scores, GPT-5.6 Terra and the fine-tuned encoders exhibit contrasting error patterns: GPT-5.6 Terra, even with the annotators' definition of sexism, over-classifies gender-related content such as religious advice on modesty, whereas the encoders miss implicit and prescriptive sexism. The Arabic-centric models also over-predict sexism and remain below the encoders. Disagreement-aware learning preserves Macro-F1, but soft labels improve alignment with annotators on contested comments at the expense of unanimous ones, and neither paradigm reliably identifies contested comments. Lower performance on Egyptian Arabic, clearest for the large language models, is not fully explained by the examined measures of test-set composition and dialectness. Majority-label scores can therefore hide contrasting error patterns that matter for content moderation.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal Entity Linking (MEL) aims to disambiguate mentions in multimodal contexts by grounding them to specific entities in a knowledge base. A pivotal challenge in MEL is capturing multi-level correspondences: the semantic consistency between mention-entity pairs and the complementary correlations across modalities. However, existing methods often suffer from element dominance due to their reliance on coupled interactions or coarse global aggregations. In response, we propose the Multi-Element Collaborative Interaction (MECI) framework. First, to capture multi-element mention-entity correspondences, we develop a Multi-view Experts Network that leverages a "divide-and-conquer" strategy for decoupled feature learning to mitigate element dominance, supported by a KL-guided routing mechanism that governs expert specialization and collaboration. Furthermore, to model cross-modal complementary correlations, we propose a Hierarchical Multimodal Interaction Module, where a dynamic modality-aware weighting network refines interactions across hierarchical semantic levels, thereby integrating multi-granular evidence to counteract element dominance. Finally, we incorporate a generative semantic refinement stage that utilizes large language models for zero-shot re-ranking. Extensive experiments on WikiDiverse, RichpediaMEL, and WikiMEL show that MECI consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, improving Hits@1 by 1.95%, 7.30%, and 2.31%, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: Patient history taking is a strategic, multi-turn process that refines diagnostic hypotheses through iterative questioning, mirroring clinical reasoning where each inquiry updates beliefs over candidate diagnoses. Deploying AI in this setting is constrained by privacy, cost, and latency, making Small Language Models (SLMs) a more desirable backbone compared to larger LLMs. However, SLMs capture only the fast, intuitive "System 1" side of this process and lack explicit mechanisms for the deliberative "System 2" reasoning needed to maintain and update diagnostic uncertainty over time. Bayesian Networks (BayesNets) offer a natural complement, providing an interpretable framework for auditable probabilistic belief tracking. Yet, clinical BayesNets are difficult to construct due to their reliance on expert curated structure, dependencies, and parameters.
  We introduce ProbMedTOD, which automatically synthesizes a clinical BayesNet from a small number of clinical notes in three stages: ontology building, structure prediction, and parameter estimation. ProbMedTOD then integrates the resulting BayesNet with SLMs, enabling probabilistic reasoning in multi-turn dialogues. A BayesNet Agent maintains real-time posterior distributions over diagnoses, while a Policy Agent picks diagnostically informative questions. Experiments on DDXPlus and MIMIC-IV Notes show that ProbMedTOD outperforms LLM-only and retrieval-based baselines, with especially strong gains for smaller models.

</details>

Abstract: Building a small language model requires choosing which published improvements to combine. We compare five changes in the same model, using the same data and training budget. We test selected combinations and repeat each one with a second random seed. Mixture-of-experts layers, Muon optimisation and a Gated DeltaNet hybrid give the largest gains. Extra normalisation also helps, while an attention gate with a matched parameter budget adds little on average. The gains mostly combine, but small interactions between changes appear again in the repeated runs. Repetition also reveals benefits that an initial statistical estimate treated as noise. These findings apply to our small-model training setting; the expert models also have greater total capacity. The practical lesson is to test promising components together and repeat the comparisons before deciding which ones to keep.

</details>

Abstract: Manifold-constrained Hyper-Connections (mHC) extend a Transformer's residual stream into multiple parallel streams with learned cross-stream connections. Prior work trains all streams toward a shared objective. We instead study whether assigning distinct learning objectives to different streams can improve task performance and induce objective-specific specialization. Using complementary lexical (sparse) and semantic (dense) retrieval objectives as a test case, we find that objective-specialized mHC consistently outperforms a non-specialized mHC model across the full sparse--dense fusion range, for both two- and four-stream models and at two model scales. We then examine how specialization changes the model internally using probes of stream representations, residual mixing, objective-specific gradient flow, and read/write gates. The specialized and non-specialized models behave similarly through most of the network but diverge in the final layers: the specialized model develops increasingly objective-specific gradient flow, distinct read/write routing patterns, and reduced cross-stream mixing, and streams assigned to different objectives become increasingly dissimilar while those trained toward the same objective remain highly similar. Fixing the residual mixing to the identity leaves the specialization benefit intact, indicating that the benefit stems from objective-specific read/write routing rather than learned cross-stream mixing. These results show that assigning distinct objectives to mHC streams can improve multi-objective retrieval while inducing structured, depth-dependent specialization in the residual computation.

</details>


### [297] [Late Entry in Looped Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24576)
*Ziqi Jin,Jiaxi Li,Weiwen Xu,Aixin Sun*

Main category: cs.CL

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Looped language models deepen reasoning at a compact parameter count by iterating a shared layer stack T times per token, turning recurrence depth directly into sequential decoding latency. Conventional early-exit decoding halts after initial passes without cache completion, truncating cached key-value states of past tokens to shallow depth. We study the opposite strategy, late entry, where new tokens skip initial passes and enter directly into the final K < T passes over a deep context cache. Once lightweight fine-tuning resolves hidden-state norm mismatch at the entry boundary, deep cached context substitutes for skipped token-local passes, allowing late entry to outperform matched early exit across model families and pass budgets. To turn this asymmetry into wall-clock speedups without stalling to build deep caches, candidate blocks verify through only the final K passes on the critical path while previously committed tokens complete passes 1 -> T alongside subsequent steps at zero extra serial calls. Projecting these finished T-pass wake states inside the existing output GEMM (Wake verification, governed by logit-margin tolerance tau in [0, inf]) unifies greedy-exact T-pass decoding (tau = 0, matching K = T block decoding within bf16 reduction order), margin-gated verification (tau > 0), and non-rollback lossy late entry (tau = inf) inside a single static CUDA graph. On Ouro-2.6B (T = 4), K = 2 Wake reaches 5.2x greedy-exact speedup at tau = 0 (+26% over K = 4 block decoding on GSM8K) and 6.8x at tau = inf (+16.9 points over trained early exit at matched speed). Scaling test-time recurrence across T in {8, 16, 32, 64} on Huginn-3.5B cuts K = T/4 rollbacks 21.5x with depth, driving greedy-exact (tau = 0) speedups to 6.1x (T = 32) and 8.4x (T = 64, +153% over K = 64 block) and tau = inf speedups to 10.6 to 12.5x.

</details>

Abstract: This research investigates whether a large language model or an autonomous AI Agent performs better at translating from English to Arabic, which is considered a low-resource language. The authors introduce WikiEn-Ar190, a new dataset of 190 carefully selected Wikipedia articles from 18 different areas, which were created and verified by two native Arabic speakers to ensure that they accurately reflect both meaning and style. Using this dataset, the study evaluates the translation quality of GPT-5-mini both as an independent neural translator and when used within a more complex process that includes self-assessment and improvement. As a standalone model, GPT-5-mini produced very fluent translations , achieving a COMET score of 0.8649 and receiving high human scores for adequacy, fluency, and style (averaging 4.53 out of 5). When used in a system that combined translation with self-assessment, it improved lexical accuracy by 28.76% (BLEU score of 22.83) while maintaining a similar level of semantic quality. These findings show that methods involving autonomous reasoning can improve translation consistency for language pairs with limited resources. Beyond the results, the approach offers a framework for evaluating and improving translations, potentially using them as assisting tools to expand the representation of low resource languages, such as Arabic, on the web.

</details>

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting improves the performance of Large Language Models by eliciting intermediate reasoning steps. However, CoT is far from perfect: on some tasks, models perform worse than with direct-answer prompting, and on others they generate a few hundred tokens to reach the correct answer. We start by labelling each CoT hidden state as recovering if patching it into the direct prompt yields the correct answer, and failed otherwise. A linear probe shows that the model encodes these two classes differently. We then show that the representation space of the direct prompt holds a direction for each question that leads the model, in a short generation, to the correct answer more often than CoT does. Specifically, we rotate the direct prompt's last hidden state once, before generation, towards a direction built for each question from its own recovering and failed states. Across 25 tasks, these directions raise the direct prompt's mean accuracy from $46\%$ to $85\%$ on LLaMA 3.1 8B (CoT: $69\%$) and from $52\%$ to $86\%$ on Qwen 2.5 7B (CoT: $77\%$), with a tenth of CoT's tokens. CoT is thus not the shortest route to this accuracy, and these directions can be used in future work to train models to align their states with them.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce Clover-35B-A3B-Preview (Clover), a mixture-of-experts diffusion language model with 35 billion total parameters and 3 billion activated parameters, supporting thinking-mode generation. Clover adapts Gated Delta Networks to block diffusion by combining recurrent memory of previously generated context with bidirectional softmax attention inside the current block. Historical memory readout competes with current-block tokens in a shared softmax, with learnable block-size calibration and a separate, zero-initialized right-context convolution branch. Clover leads the seven evaluated agent metrics, including 73.6 on SWE-bench Verified and 51.7 on Terminal-Bench 2.1. On internal agent workloads, it reaches a reported generation speed of 680 tokens/s using one NVIDIA GB300 GPU at BF16 precision and batch size one. We organize the evaluation around model quality, drafting and verification costs, and deployment under constrained memory. We also describe a decision-model variant that generates answers to related questions in one denoising pass. On an internal set of 500 groups, joint prediction raises question accuracy from 90.37% to 93.88% and all-correct group accuracy from 65.4% to 77.0%.

</details>

Abstract: We present Piccolo, a family of multimodal decision models (0.8B to 27B) that coevolves model capabilities and training data via Agentic Data Engineering and recursive self-improvement (RSI). The models predict probabilities over candidate answers for general-purpose text and visual judgments, agent harnesses, data workflows, and interactive games. Piccolo-27B tops the Decision Index Vision Full leaderboard with a score of 75.59, while Piccolo-9B and Piccolo-4B achieve 68.35 and 67.46, respectively. All three rank first on the Private benchmark in their respective model-size categories. Research agents analyze model behavior, construct training data, conduct experiments, and use the results to refine subsequent proposals. Semantic slices and a shared research memory connect these actions across the model family's development. With training recipes comprising hundreds of thousands of examples, data selection and feedback-driven refinement are central to the self-improvement process. Our large-scale RSI exploration yields three counter-intuitive insights for decision-model development: (1) Backbone-recipe co-adaptation outweighs raw model recency: earlier backbones can surpass newer generations on visual tasks under identical recipes; (2) Targeted data curation enables 4B-9B models to rival 27B baselines on specialized workflows; and (3) Continuous loss reduction can decouple from and even degrade discrete decision accuracy. Together with the released weights and tools from the Data-Juicer and AgentScope ecosystems, Piccolo provides an actionable blueprint for building frontier decision models and scaling data-driven RSI.

</details>

Abstract: A central challenge in adapting large language models (LLMs) is capability preservation: an adaptation can achieve its target while degrading general capabilities. We introduce CurvDelta, a scalable framework that incorporates the preservation constraint into the parameterization of the model update. By analyzing the local geometry of the preservation constraint around the pre-adaptation model, CurvDelta constructs a low-curvature space in which parameter updates leave the preservation loss nearly invariant. We make its construction practical for LLMs through Kronecker-factored approximate curvature (K-FAC) and a factorized projection. We also introduce CurvLoRA, a low-rank variant within the same preservation geometry, designed for more efficient adaptation. Both methods apply to preservation-constrained adaptation tasks such as capability learning and selective unlearning. We evaluate our methods on mathematical reasoning, code generation, and knowledge unlearning. For mathematical reasoning and code generation, our approaches achieve competitive downstream performance while better preserving general capabilities, offering a favorable acquisition--retention trade-off relative to the baselines. For knowledge unlearning, our main results on TOFU and MUSE show a better balance between targeted forgetting and knowledge retention than the baselines, with retention performance close to the from-scratch retraining reference.

</details>

Abstract: Continual pre-training (CPT) is widely used to adapt pretrained language models to dialectal and non-standard language varieties, yet it remains unclear when this additional training is necessary, what drives its benefits, and whether aggregate evaluation scores adequately capture its effects. In this paper, we present a controlled study of CPT for Québec French language understanding across nine LLMs spanning different families and scales. Using a budget-matched protocol of 30M tokens per experiment, our results show that the benefit of CPT depends strongly on the starting model's existing competence in the target variety. When CPT is beneficial, the improvements are driven more by corpus composition than by data volume, with literary text accounting for most of the gain. Paired Québec and standard French evaluations further show that CPT primarily improves expression comprehension rather than grammatical competence, and that corpus register determines which regional expressions are acquired. These gains also involve capability-specific trade-offs, including declines in reading comprehension that aggregate scores can conceal. Overall, these findings show that CPT for dialectal adaptation should not be treated as a uniformly beneficial default. Instead, adaptation strategies should begin with a careful assessment of the base model's existing competence, accompanied by source-level data analysis and task-level evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: What makes a small trained map from encoder states to the embedding space of a frozen language model sufficient for competitive speech recognition? The premise of the SLAM-ASR architecture is that a frozen language model possesses linguistic knowledge rich enough to perform the recognition task. This assumption, while reasonable, provides little insight into the mechanisms underlying these models. We therefore seek the concrete internal structures on which they rely. This work shows that SLAM-ASR models rely on induction heads of the frozen language model: the same heads that copy repeated token sequences in text are also important for mapping speech embeddings to transcript tokens. Moreover, we find pairs of heads that behave as a constrained form of differential attention, cancelling attention noise and sharpening attention onto the previously generated word. Finally, we show how induction heads could be used to improve transcription quality under noisy audio.

</details>

Abstract: Hybrid attention models combine full and linear attention to achieve a better quality--efficiency trade-off. Converting pretrained Transformers into such models requires identifying which layers should retain full attention under a prescribed budget. We identify two off-policy mismatches in FlashMorph's layer selection: (i) continuous branch interpolation and teacher-forced hidden states differ from discrete hybrid computation; and (ii) frozen model weights fail to account for parameter adaptation during subsequent recovery. We introduce FlashMorph2, an on-policy layer-selection method that (I) evaluates sampled discrete hybrid architectures end to end using their own hidden states, and (II) jointly optimizes layer-selection scores and model weights under the recovery objective to reflect post-adaptation performance. Experiments across Qwen3 model scales and linear-attention variants show that FlashMorph2 consistently outperforms existing layer-selection methods, improving average scores over FlashMorph by $+2.42$ points on RULER, $+1.08$ on LongBench, and $+0.76$ on recall-intensive tasks, while completing layer selection in only $0.72$--$3.57$ GPU hours across 0.6B--8B models, demonstrating high efficiency and scalability.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to infer psychological attributes, predict individual behaviour, and simulate human populations. However, despite generating plausible responses, they often misrepresent individuals and distort population statistics. Here we introduce Chiron, a foundation model to measure and predict human behaviour. We jointly fine-tune an LLM to represent psychological constructs, predict individual behaviour, and align population response distributions with human statistics. The training and evaluation data span 36 behavioural domains and 78 social science experiments, comprising 288,307 person records, 1,411,750 atomic behavioural decisions, and 99,953 psychometric assessments over 109 psychological constructs. In our evaluations, Chiron's psychological measurements agree more closely with human self-reports and exhibit fewer biases that undermine psychometric validity. It predicts individual behaviour more accurately than frontier LLMs (up to 28.4% relative improvement). In simulations of social science experiments, it more accurately captures the direction and magnitude of human experimental effects (82.2--94.3% lower effect-size estimation error). Together, these results establish Chiron as a unified computational instrument for measuring and predicting human behaviour.

</details>

Abstract: Language models acquire their capabilities from training data, and developers withhold data during training to keep models from learning capabilities that could be misused. How long this delays a capability, and how much data is needed to acquire it later, is unknown. Here we show that the general progress a model makes on the rest of its training data substitutes for the data that was withheld. We withheld the training signal for individual skills in models of chess, Othello, molecular strings, bracket languages and web text. The delay of a withheld skill in larger models was predicted from smaller ones. A chess model never explicitly trained to respond to check ranked the legal responses almost as well as a model that was. When models were then trained on the withheld data, they acquired the skill with a small fraction of the data needed from initialization, and this fraction decreased as the models progressed. A control rule of equal difficulty was learned much more slowly. A programming language withheld from the training text of a 7-billion-parameter model was regained in the same way. Data filtering therefore delays a capability, and the data required to regain it decreases as models improve.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models have been widely used to generate plausible research ideas, yet it remains unclear what makes automated ideation effective and how generated novel proposals arise from prior scientific work. We introduce the Creative AI Researcher (CAR), an agentic framework combining literature grounding, iterative development and explicit conceptual operators. CAR constructs a semantic knowledge graph from retrieved literature and equips an ideation agent with graph-retrieval tools and four operators: bisociation, analogy, exploration and elaboration. Each operator call is checked against structural acceptance conditions and recorded, producing a trace of intermediate transformations and declared literature sources. We evaluate CAR with two language-model backbones across 40 LiveIdeaBench research topics and two execution benchmarks. CAR achieves significantly higher methodological novelty (MN) than single-shot prompting and The AI Scientist. Its methodological novelty does not differ significantly from the Research Ideation Agent, while its feasibility scores are higher on both backbones. With a shared experiment executor, CAR achieves the highest pairwise win rate among the evaluated AI methods on FML-bench-Lite and BioXArena. In a ten-condition ablation study, MN point estimates favor CAR over one-step proposal on both backbones, but no MN contrast survives correction for multiple comparisons. The results do not establish a consistent quality benefit from explicit operators beyond free-form iteration. CAR makes the sequence of operations and declared literature sources inspectable, separating judged idea quality from procedural traceability.

</details>

Abstract: Jailbreak attempts and harmful prompts remain a challenge for the safe deployment of large language models. We study whether contrastively learned representations can support safety detection without training an additional downstream classifier. We propose Jailbreak Detection with Prototype Inference (JDPI), which uses binary safety labels to construct harmful and benign prototypes and makes predictions from relative prototype distances. JDPI uses only binary safety labels for encoder adaptation, prototype construction, and validation. In our setup, it uses fewer labeled examples than the classifier baseline (Method A) by avoiding the separate split used to train the downstream classifier. Across four benchmarks covering different input formats, contrastive fine-tuning improves JDPI's detection accuracy by 9.1-18.0 percentage points. Using the same encoder, JDPI achieves slightly higher ROC-AUC on one benchmark and stays within 0.017 of the baseline across all four. With dataset-specific adaptation, JDPI exceeds the released Llama Guard 3-8B baseline by 0.173-0.310 in ROC-AUC across the four benchmarks. In multi-turn replay on TomGibbs, JDPI uses accumulated user history as the conversation unfolds, achieving higher conversation-level accuracy than when it uses the current turn alone. Across 12 directed transfers, JDPI outperforms the classifier by an average of 1.59 percentage points in accuracy, demonstrating stronger cross-dataset robustness.

</details>

Abstract: Autoregressive vision-language-action (VLA) models predict discrete action tokens with the next-token objective of their vision-language backbone, so the action tokenizer defines the interface between the policy and the robot. In bimanual manipulation, the two arms share a repertoire of movements yet coordinate through complementary roles, and an action representation should capture both. However, most existing tokenizers encode both arms as a single concatenated action vector, in which motion patterns common to the two arms are not shared and cross-arm dependencies remain entangled within each token. The policy must therefore learn both aspects implicitly. We propose Bimanual Coordination Action Tokenization (BiCAT), which shares one motion representation across arms while conditioning each arm's tokens on its partner through symmetric cross-arm attention and a joint decoder. The policy then predicts all tokens of each arm in parallel, generating the left arm first and the right arm conditioned on it. Across ten RoboTwin 2.0 tasks and six real-world tasks on three embodiments, BiCAT achieves the highest success among the compared tokenizers. In simulation, it improves mean success by 17.6 percentage points over the strongest of eight baselines, with the largest gains on tasks that require close coordination between the arms, while requiring only 40.5 ms per action chunk. These results suggest that the action tokenizer is a natural place to model bimanual coordination.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce TAG (Testbed for Autonomous Games), a compact, low-cost robotic platform for studying learning and control in physically coupled dynamical systems. TAG combines an actively tilted and steered ball-in-maze board with a top-mounted rotary inverted pendulum. The pendulum's reaction torque and vibration disturb the board and marble, while the board's tilt, driven by the maze-solving policy, simultaneously disturbs the pendulum's balance. The main challenge in learning control policies for each subsystem is accounting for the impact of one subsystem's dynamics on the other's learning process. To address this challenge, we present complementary reinforcement learning paradigms, leveraging model-based and model-free approaches to learn the control policies for the maze and pendulum, respectively. Our key finding is that introducing the pendulum causes an immediate, sharp drop in the maze policy's success rate, but the policy adapts online and recovers to its pre-coupling performance within a small number of learning episodes. Experimental results show that both policies, trained independently, successfully control the maze board and the pendulum despite their physical coupling.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models look saturated on LIBERO, yet most of them fall apart on LIBERO-PRO as soon as an object moves or the instruction asks for something new. We argue that what is missing is not scale but decoupling.
  In these policies, what to do is tied to where things were during training, to the scene around them and to the wording of the instruction. We cut these ties in two places. In the data, LIBERO demonstrations are re-arranged by general rules: every object and fixture is moved and rotated, tasks are carried into other environments and onto other surfaces, and every instruction is paraphrased. In the policy, a perception engine on frozen DINOv3 features, trained on this augmented data, hands the action model the scene in decoupled form: a list of objects with their positions, and a compact code of what the gripper is looking at, next to image and instruction embeddings from TinyCLIP. A flow-matching diffusion transformer, trained from scratch, acts on this view.
  We call this combination of Robust Augmentation and Grounded Encoding RageVLA. With 0.21 billion parameters, RageVLA sets a new state of the art on LIBERO-PRO among VLA policies, scoring 0.72 against 0.53 for the best published model, and a larger variant reaches 0.73.
  Two further tests on LIBERO-Object show that the policy acts on what it perceives. When the object positions it is given are shuffled while the camera images stay correct, its success falls from 0.965 to 0.215. When the target object is displaced by 28 cm, it still succeeds in 0.865 of the episodes, where $π_{0.5}$, run through the same harness, succeeds in 0.105.

</details>

Abstract: Centimeter-scale robots are promising for urban search and rescue because they can enter confined spaces that are inaccessible or unsafe for humans. However, their small size limits obstacle traversal after deployment. This paper presents the RESCUE Jumper, a 125 g mobile robot with a 6.2 cm width and 6.2 cm diameter wheels that combines ground rolling with jumping to traverse steps and gaps that exceeds its rolling capability. The RESCUE Jumper uses its two actuators for both rolling mode and jumping by reversing the direction of wheel motion. Forward wheel motion enables rolling, while reverse wheel motion winds a spring steel tail to store elastic potential energy and release it for jumping. The RESCUE Jumper varies tail winding speed to control jump direction, transitioning from backward to forward jumps as winding speed increases from 0.75 rad/s to 8.73 rad/s. The launch angle of the jumps ranges from 45 degree to -11 degree with respect to the vertical axis, reaching a maximum height of 9.2 cm and a maximum lateral displacement of 11.2 cm. We model the winding speed-dependent jumping behavior in three phases: tail release to determine the contact point and robot orientation, tail straightening until liftoff, and ballistic flight to predict jump height and lateral displacement. Finally, qualitative tests highlight the robot's locomotion in cluttered, real-world environments. The model and experiments presented in this work can inform future designs of space-constrained jump mechanisms in centimeter-scale robots for search and rescue and inspection in unstructured environments.

</details>

Abstract: Many learning-based approaches for deformable linear objects (DLOs) rely on simplified deformation models that neglect dynamic effects and the mechanical response of the objects, limiting their applicability to lightweight, compliant objects and slow motions. Inspired by recent advances in learning physically structured dynamics with coupled oscillator networks (CONs), we propose $Φ$-CON for learning force-driven DLO dynamics. The model combines a floating-base coupled oscillator network (FBCON), whose latent potential energy depends only on the DLO's shape, with a learned affine diffeomorphism $Φ$ between observed positions along the DLO and latent coordinates. Its shape dynamics are stable by construction, and its Lagrangian structure yields explicit expressions for kinetic and potential energy. We exploit this structure to design a model-based proportional-derivative (PD) controller that compensates for the learned potential forces, thereby establishing closed-loop stability guarantees and ensuring convergence. We evaluate the approach on four simulated DLOs and a real bimanual robot setup. In simulation, $Φ$-CON achieves prediction accuracy comparable to neural ordinary differential equation (NODE) models and Lagrangian neural networks (LNNs), with $97.2\%$ and $90.2\%$ fewer parameters, respectively. Relative to a model-free PD baseline, the proposed controller reduces mean endpoint regulation error by $97.5$-$99.7\%$ in simulation and achieves a mean per-target reduction of $27.9\%$ across both endpoints in real-world experiments, demonstrating the importance of explicitly accounting for the mechanical response of stiff and heavy objects.

</details>

Abstract: Symbolic methods make robot designs interpretable but often overlook continuous geometry; learned methods handle geometry but offer less transparent structure. We introduce a typed production grammar that uses case-role relations, parameter inheritance, and specificity-based rule arbitration to generate both manipulation skills and robot morphologies. A geometric realization map translates grammar composition into transformations in SE(3), behaving as a monoid homomorphism when inheritance is inactive and exhibiting a measurable defect when inheritance is enabled. The grammar provides a compact representation, and its arbitration decisions can be recovered from geometric containment (agreement 0.905 +/- 0.062 across 10 seeds, versus 0.611 +/- 0.090 for a Euclidean control). We also introduce PANINI, a cross-entropy search method over grammar-based morphology representations. Across 30 methods and 24 seeds, PANINI achieves the best mean rank but is not statistically distinguishable from strong generic optimizers. Its main demonstrated advantage is therefore structural rather than optimization performance: grammar-generated designs are legal by construction, and coordinated limb placement produces 95% jointly feasible edits, compared with 55% for range-constrained resampling and none for flat parameterization in 6,000 draws. These results show how typed grammars can make robot generation more interpretable and geometrically coordinated, without implying that every feasible design will locomote or that grammar-based search universally outperforms generic optimizers.

</details>
