Abstract: Low-cost tactile arrays have made touch a practical input for robot learning, but piezoresistive designs sense only pressure, while camera-based sensors that also resolve shear are too bulky to tile over a gripper. To solve this problem, we present LuxTac, a flexible, scalable optical taxel array, about 3.5 mm thick and built from off-the-shelf parts. Each taxel is a digital RGBC light-to-digital converter under a translucent white silicone layer capped by an opaque black film, so the back-scattered LED light falls monotonically with local indentation depth. The same board accepts two interchangeable gel cartridges: a plain one for normal sensing, and one with colored walls that encodes the local shear in the color of the returned light, with no camera, magnets, or bump overlay, making LuxTac, to our knowledge, the first optical taxel array to resolve shear at individual taxels. Characterized with an automated CNC indenter and a six-axis force/torque sensor, the normal cartridge responds linearly to force ($R^2$ = 0.999) from 104 mN to at least 5.8 N, with 2.0% repeatability, 5.9% hysteresis, and a taxel-to-taxel variation of 6-10% after normalization. With the shear cartridge, per-taxel models estimate the three-axis force at every taxel; across three indenter shapes and nine held-out sessions, the summed estimates reach $R^2$ = 0.96/0.96/0.92 in x/y/z, each taxel's estimate 0.92/0.93/0.94, and the median shear-direction error is 4.8 degrees. Adding LuxTac pressure readings to an Action Chunking Transformer policy substantially improves peg-in-hole success over a vision-only baseline. Design files, code, and videos are available at https://luxtac.github.io.

</details>


### [317] [SPICE: Stochastic Policy for Interaction in Cluttered Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23585)

Abstract: Agricultural tasks such as harvesting and pruning require robots to reach through dense foliage, where contact is often unavoidable and vision is obstructed. Intermittent contact creates an imbalance between free-space and contact-rich observations, complicating the learning of contact-responsive behaviours. We present SPICE, a tactileconditioned diffusion policy for reaching targets in dense plant canopies using tactile sensing as the sole environmental feedback. SPICE exhibits three behaviours: moving through compliant foliage, manoeuvring around partially obstructing stems, and manipulating branches that block the target path. To the best of our knowledge, SPICE is the first tactile-conditioned diffusion policy for target reaching through physically cluttered plant canopies. Across over 180 real-robot trials, SPICE was evaluated against tactile-conditioned, image-conditioned, and reactive-control baselines. Spatial tactile information and explicit contact conditioning proved important, with SPICE achieving 95% branch-manoeuvring success versus 45% for the strongest tactile baseline. SPICE also achieved branch manipulation beyond the reactive controller and remained effective under visual occlusion, where the image-conditioned DP failed. Zero-shot transfer across 40 trials on unseen tomato and lime plants achieved 87.5% success rate. These results demonstrate the potential of tactile-conditioned generative policies for interaction in visually occluded plant environments.

</details>


### [318] [Recoverability, Not Invariance: Certified Safety for Robots at the Limits of Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23589)
*Thomas A. Berrueta,Joshua D. Ibrahim,Gilchrist X. Johnson,John Lathrop,Soon-Jo Chung*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Safety certification in robotics typically rests on forward invariance of the deployed controller, a property that demands high-fidelity models at the edge of the operating envelope, exactly where models are least reliable and robots perform best. As a result, many of the behaviors we most want robots to run, from learned policies to aggressive maneuvers at the limits of traction, cannot be certified at all. We introduce recoverability, a contraction-theoretic safety concept that resolves this tension by certifying a robot's ability to reengage a trusted recovery mode rather than the invariance of a deployed behavior. We prove that switching to the recovery mode from anywhere inside a certified tube guarantees exponential convergence, even under disturbances and model error. To decide when to switch, we derive the contraction insertion gradient, the first-order effect of a recovery insertion on the certificate, and embed it in a switching-time program in which feasibility alone guarantees safety and optimality only shapes performance. To supply the certificates, we develop sequential convex synthesis that monotonically grows the certified tube toward the exact viability kernel. We validate our approach in simulated quadrotor flight through uncertain obstacles, where it outperforms invariance-based safety filters, and in hardware deployments aboard a full-scale IndyNXT autonomous racecar, where it repeatedly recovers the car from an aggressive raceline at the limits of handling in the rain. Recoverability provides a certified exit strategy, extending formal safety guarantees to aggressive, learned, and experimental behaviors without requiring their invariance.

</details>


### [319] [How Uncertain Is My Action? Self-Correcting Visual Planning with Frozen World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23620)

Abstract: A frozen latent world model combined with sampling-based model predictive control is a visual planner. At deployment, the executed action can depart from the commanded one, and the frozen model has no way to notice. We show that the model can be run in reverse: for any hypothesized actuation shift, it predicts what it should have observed under that shift, and comparing this prediction with what was actually observed quantifies the shift and lets the planner correct itself. Unlike the residual energy, which mixes the shift with the model's own error, the score keeps only the part of the residual that a shift can produce. This shift score detects the shift with a locally optimal test, estimates it with Cramer-Rao error bars, names the dominant generator, and compensates the planner once an alarm fires. On PushT, TwoRoom and OGBench-Cube with a frozen latent world model, measured detection follows the prediction to within 0.05 AUROC. Self-correction raises success from 0.650 to 0.862 on PushT and from 0.378 to 0.432 on the harder Cube task, levels with an oracle that is told the true shift at the same block, better than five common test-time adaptation methods. On a real robotic arm, the score identifies a base-joint offset that no joint encoder registers and reduces the reach error from 36 mm to 3-5 mm, and under shifts drawn at random and sealed before the session, estimates recover 92-94% of the error reduction achieved with the true shift. The broader implication is that a frozen world model can act as a filter by predicting the future, testing action-interface uncertainty and validity, and finally correcting failures.

</details>

Abstract: Noise steering improves pretrained generative robot policies by learning to select their initial noise without updating model weights. We find that actions in the evaluated world-action models are more sensitive to video noise than action noise, making video noise an expressive input for steering. However, the noise-steering methods we evaluate, in their published forms, struggle to scale to the high-dimensional noise inputs of large policy classes such as world-action models. We introduce Low-Rank Noise Steering (LoRN), a reinforcement learning method that scales noise steering to world-action models. A small steering actor selects a direction through a fixed low-rank random projection, while our norm-controlled construction keeps the resulting noise near the typical scale of a Gaussian sample. This separates the actor's control dimension from the full noise dimension, enabling steering of both video and action inputs. Across three world-action models and ten model-task pairs, video steering improves average success by 26.2 percentage points over the base policy, compared with 7.1 points for DSRL action steering. For smaller action policies, the same construction achieves comparable final success while generally improving early-training success. Steering actors trained in simulation also transfer to three physical bimanual manipulation tasks across two base policies, without additional real-world training.

</details>

Abstract: Safe control around dynamic obstacles requires information about both distance and relative motion. Range-based filters commonly reconstruct the required range rate from successive range measurements or from obstacle-state estimators, which can introduce estimator delay or sensitivity to range noise when obstacle motion changes. A frequency-modulated continuous-wave (FMCW) radar measures radial relative velocity directly through Doppler. We use this measurement as the derivative of an ego-centered range control barrier function (CBF) and obtain a closed-form filter on the robot's forward velocity. A measurement margin constructed from sensor error bounds accounts for errors in range, Doppler, ego velocity, and bearing. In 508 paired encounters within the platform's retreat capability, direct Doppler produces two buffer incursions, compared with 15 for a constant-acceleration and 25 for a constant-velocity Kalman filter. On a Unitree Go1, the controller stops at $0.777 \pm 0.015$ m against the predicted $0.758$ m and reacts to a farther approaching object while a nearer static return remains. The guarantee covers the longitudinal range channel under bounded measurement errors and continuously available detections. Direct Doppler removes the need to reconstruct range rate in the safety loop, reducing the delay between a change in obstacle motion and its effect on the safety command.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action models have demonstrated strong manipulation capabilities. A limitation of VLA models is that most manipulation datasets focus on parallel-jaw gripper systems, making transfer to multi-finger hands challenging, particularly as teleoperation datasets are harder to collect with multi-fingered robots. In this work, we study how to best adapt VLAs to dexterous hands by pairing supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with diffusion-steering reinforcement learning (DSRL). Using $π_{0.5}$ and a LEAP hand, we evaluate five fine-tuning recipes across two simulation tasks using only a handful of demonstrations. Our empirical analysis yields three key insights: (1) SFT success is poorly correlated with post-DSRL success, meaning the best SFT checkpoint is rarely the most effective to steer; (2) freezing the vision-language model (VLM) and tuning only the action expert produces the most steerable policy for both training and out-of-distribution placements; and (3) low-rank adaptation (LoRA) is the least steerable approach. Consequently, we recommend an adaptation recipe that freezes the VLM, fully trains the action expert, and eschews SFT rollout success as a checkpoint selection metric.

</details>


### [323] [Measuring the Conservatism of Latent Safety Filters for World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23685)

Abstract: A latent safety filter decides from the latent of a world model whether a planned action is safe. Conformal calibration sets the threshold of the filter so that it accepts an unsafe action only with a small probability. However, the filter can still be conservative and refuse many safe actions, depending on the latent. To measure the conservatism that a latent forces on the filter, we group similar latents and actions into cells. A filter that gives one decision per cell can refuse the unsafe transitions of a cell only by refusing the safe ones in it as well. We call the share of safe transitions that lie in such cells the overlap. We prove that the overlap is the minimum share of safe transitions that such a filter has to refuse in order to accept no unsafe transition in the data. Both the overlap and the conformal calibration can be applied to any latent. We use this to compare three existing designs, in which the safety labels train a classifier on top of the world model, the world model itself, or a separate encoder beside it. In a visual navigation task, the separate encoder has the lowest overlap, and at the same conformal level its filter refuses the fewest safe actions and lets the planner reach the most goals of the three.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have become a powerful paradigm for robotic manipulation and show strong promise for real-world deployment, making adversarial robustness critical for reliable robot operation. Existing adversarial attacks on VLA-controlled robots mainly optimize prediction-level discrepancies or rollout-level deviations to disrupt manipulation. However, they rarely account for the fact that VLA-predicted commands are not executed directly, but must be realized through downstream control and robot embodiment. In this paper, we propose ExecGap, an adversarial patch attack framework that exploits this command-execution gap to expose embodiment-specific execution vulnerabilities. Specifically, ExecGap biases VLA-predicted task-space commands toward low-executability directions, which are estimated from kinematic inefficiency and dynamic weak actuation. This induces execution degradation, where the predicted command becomes difficult to realize effectively, thereby impairing task completion. Extensive simulation experiments across multiple VLA policies show that ExecGap induces larger command-execution mismatch and weaker realized execution than existing patch attacks. Real-robot experiments further validate that the deployed patch can induce execution degradation on physical platforms.

</details>


### [325] [RecallFlow: Episodic Memory as the Source Distribution of Generative Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23731)

Abstract: Diffusion and flow-matching policies have become a standard choice for visuomotor imitation learning, owing to their ability to model multimodal action distributions. In memory-dependent manipulation, the robot must act on information that is no longer visible. Existing methods feed the observation history to the policy as an additional condition, while action generation still starts from Gaussian noise. However, the policy must then pick out the relevant target from a long and redundant history, leading to spurious correlations and unreliable decisions as the memory load grows. Additionally, the initial noise carries no information about what the robot has seen, leaving generation without guidance toward the remembered target. To address these issues, we propose RecallFlow, a flow-policy framework that builds the source distribution from episodic memory. Recall-Relocalization relates the memory to the current view through a few recall slots, so that the source reflects where the remembered targets are now. Recall Anchors then turns each slot into a Gaussian component of the source, enabling generation to start close to the intended action instead of from noise. The transport network then completes the action from the current observation alone, keeping inference nearly as cheap as a memoryless policy. We further build a suite of memory-dependent tasks grouped by memory load and compare all policies under a matched protocol. Extensive experiments show that RecallFlow achieves a state-of-the-art average success rate of 80.4% on the public MIKASA-Robo memory benchmark, and outperforms the strongest baseline by 20.3 points on our suite and by 18.8 points on four real-robot tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Once deployed on vehicles, end-to-end driving models continuously encounter challenging scenarios, generating valuable human takeover data that capture both model outputs and human behaviors. However, human interventions are often imperfect, as drivers may intervene too late or unnecessarily. Consequently, direct imitation can reinforce late responses or discourage otherwise appropriate maneuvers. To address this challenge, we propose an offline post-training framework that transforms imperfect takeover data into proactive supervision. We first construct diverse trajectory candidates by augmenting the plans of the deployed model and shifting recorded human corrective maneuvers earlier in time. We then train a scene-conditioned trajectory scorer to rank these candidates using expert demonstrations and unpaired preference signals from takeover logs. Using this scorer, we selectively replace pre-takeover training targets with higher-scoring candidates while retaining the original targets in normal driving scenarios. Finally, we distill the resulting targets into the driving model without changing its inference architecture. In closed-loop evaluations using real-world scenarios reconstructed with 4D Gaussian splatting, our method achieves the highest overall success rate, outperforming the deployed model, direct imitation learning and trajectory post-optimization. These results highlight a practical path toward efficient updates of deployed driving models using existing fleet logs.

</details>

Abstract: When humans learn to manipulate an unfamiliar object, they may already be able to anticipate how its state will change while still being unable to control it skillfully. This gap between prediction and control motivates us to study how the priors acquired by video generation models can be used effectively by an action policy. We present ZERITH V2.5, a video-action model (VAM) in which the action policy reads intermediate features of a video generation model, and use it to study, under fixed demonstrations and a fixed total number of updates, what kind of predictive training improves subsequent action learning.
  We find that the way video predictive training is supervised affects subsequent action learning. In our experiments, adapting at many noise levels yields a large gain, while adapting only at the noise level used at deployment yields none. Weight-transfer experiments further show that the gain stays with the adapted weights when they remain in the video generation model and disappears when they are used only to initialize the action policy. Initial loss and gradient measurements at the start of joint training indicate that target-domain predictive training gives the video model a better starting state, making subsequent joint learning of prediction and action easier. Under matched data and training budgets, predictive adaptation improves control. We evaluate the complete policy on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, Meta-World MT50, and real-robot tasks.

</details>


### [328] [Improve How You Improve: Recursive Policy Self-Improvement for Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Multi-Object Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23774)

Abstract: To advance the development of embodied general navigation, zero-shot open-vocabulary multi-object navigation has attracted increasing attention, aiming to enable robots to efficiently search multiple objects in unseen environments without training. For this task, the key lies in improving navigation policies from earlier searches to locate subsequent goals more efficiently. Recent methods automate this process through trajectory reflection and rule revision, enabling policy self-improvement. However, the mechanisms for diagnosing problems and generating revisions are typically designed by humans and remain fixed across iterations. Consequently, the system's capacity for continued self-improvement remains constrained, even as its navigation policy evolves. To this end, we propose RPSI-Nav, a Recursive Policy Self-Improvement framework for zero-shot multi-object navigation that revises both the navigation program and its revision mechanism. RPSI-Nav diagnoses search behavior, requests diagnostic views when existing observations cannot distinguish competing causes, and generates targeted navigation revisions. Feedback from subsequent goal searches is linked to the rules that produced these revisions, guiding changes to diagnosis, observation acquisition, and revision planning. The updated improver governs subsequent navigation improvements, forming a recursive loop in which RPSI-Nav revises its own improvement process. Across goals, the robot conditionally inherits accepted program updates and spatial memory, enabling search experience to improve both future navigation and adaptation. Benchmark experiments and qualitative real-robot evaluations validate RPSI-Nav's effectiveness.

</details>


### [329] [CoT-WAM: Collaborative Training of Vision-Language Models and World-Action Models via Plan Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23803)

Abstract: General robot manipulation requires both semantic competence and physical foresight. World action models (WAMs) acquire an understanding of and predictive capability over the physical world by jointly modeling visual foresight and action distributions. However, existing WAMs still lack semantic competence: they have limited ability to perform open-world scene understanding, semantic reasoning, and long-horizon planning. Although vision-language models (VLMs) provide these capabilities, effectively integrating their semantic priors into WAM prediction and control remains challenging. To address this challenge, we propose CoT-WAM, a jointly trained WAM-VLM framework. CoT-WAM uses a learnable embedding to exchange information between the VLM and the WAM, which is supervised by textual and goal-image signals during training to encode rich intent information. A tailored two-stage training strategy enables joint optimization and more effective collaboration between the VLM and WAM. We evaluate CoT-WAM on six benchmarks covering control performance, zero-shot generalization, and long-horizon planning. CoT-WAM achieves SOTA performance on LIBERO, CALVIN, and RoboCasa-Kitchen, and demonstrates stronger zero-shot generalization on LIBERO-Plus and LIBERO-Pro without fine-tuning. On RoboCasa365, it further improves long-horizon planning over baselines with and without external planners. Real-world experiments further demonstrate the effectiveness of our method in practical deployments.

</details>

Abstract: Tactile feedback helps visual manipulation policies respond to local contact, but changes in tactile observations do not always require corrective motion, and sustained corrections do not always require new tactile features. We introduce TacPilot, a tactile residual framework that separates representation updates from action intervention around a frozen visual policy. TacPilot uses a perception gate to determine when tactile features need refreshing and updates only affected regions while reusing unchanged features. A separate intervention gate determines whether bounded Cartesian and gripper residuals modify the nominal visual action. This separation allows tactile perception and action correction to follow different spatial and temporal demands during manipulation. In offline expert-trajectory replay, local feature reuse reduces encoded regions by 56.4% relative to periodic full encoding, with essentially unchanged action predictions. In an exploratory simulation evaluation spanning two cylinder sizes, TacPilot succeeds in 26 of 30 trials (86.7%). We further validate TacPilot on OpenArm v2 equipped with Paxini tactile sensors across four contact-rich manipulation tasks.

</details>

Abstract: A patch to a robot's navigation software can pass every unit test and still drive the robot off its path. Checking how the repaired robot drives catches such patches. It does not show that the agent found everything that was wrong. We introduce RoboDx, a generator of repair tasks for simulated robot navigation software with known injected faults. A hidden verifier marks a repair as verified only if the robot then drives correctly in hidden scenarios, which the agent never sees. A second track hides the code and asks the agent to pick the cause from a list of hypotheses. A task with two faults can contain a quiet fault, a fault that makes no hidden scenario fail unless the other fault is also present. On our set of 100 tasks, RoboDx-Hard-100, almost every repair by Claude Opus 5.5 with standard coding tools is verified. We analyse 94 of the tasks, 44 with one fault and 50 with two. On the 50 two-fault tasks, only 17 of Opus's 49 verified repairs remove both injected edits. Most faults left behind are configuration constants whose correct value the task's documentation does not state. Opus names both faults on 10/50 of these tasks when it cannot see the code. We then give four current models the same measurements of each faulty robot, picked so that, in the simulator, only the true cause produces all of them. The models name the cause on at most 38 of 94 tasks. They are right on almost all when they also get the simulator's output for every hypothesis. Their own predictions of that output give no detectable gain. On 97 tasks from real bug-fix pull requests, Claude Haiku 5.5, the weakest model on the generated tasks, scores lowest, and the other three models are not ordered.

</details>

Abstract: Hamilton-Jacobi Reachability computes the states from which a system can be kept safe under worst-case disturbances, but it assumes the state is known exactly, whereas real robots act on noisy estimates whose uncertainty evolves with their motion. We extend Hamilton-Jacobi Reachability to the robot's belief, estimate and uncertainty, treating unexpected measurements as a bounded adversary and enlarging the unsafe set by the current uncertainty, so that certified states are safe with a prescribed probability. The formulation is compatible with existing solvers. In simulation, the resulting safety filters nearly eliminate the collisions of certaintyequivalent HJ and are less conservative than equally safe uncertainty-padding baselines. The implementation code is available at https://github.com/HimanshuPaudel/Estimation-Aware-Reachability

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have emerged as a promising paradigm for general-purpose robotic manipulation, yet their performance often degrades on long-horizon tasks that require multiple interdependent manipulations. Instead of burdening the VLA policies with long-horizon reasoning, memory, action generation, progress tracking, and error recovery, we argue for leveraging their short-horizon manipulation capabilities within an execution-aware framework. To this end, we introduce \textnormal{\textbf{RePlan-VLA}}, a VLA-agnostic framework that translates a VLA's short-horizon capabilities into reliable long-horizon execution. RePlan-VLA explicitly monitors the robot's motion for progress evaluation, and adaptively revises the future plan based on observed outcomes. We preserve the underlying VLA policy entirely, requiring no additional fine-tuning. Experiments on RoboCasa365 and CALVIN benchmarks show an improvement of 21.9% and 10.4% in success rate over the next strongest baseline. We also validate our approach for real-world deployment on a Franka Research 3 robot.

</details>


### [334] [LAGR: Language-Aware Grasp Re-Ranking for Task Oriented Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23858)

Abstract: Grasping is a fundamental capability for robotic manipulation, with the appropriate grasp depending on the downstream task. For example, a hammer should be held by its handle for hammering but by its head when it is handed over. Selecting such a grasp requires consideration of two criteria: physical feasibility on the object and suitability for the task. Developing such an approach requires dense grasp labels with both feasibility and task scores on real observations, but existing task-oriented grasping datasets are small, synthetic, or costly to annotate. To address this gap, we construct a dataset of 2.68 million scored 5-DoF grasp annotations over 100,911 image-task pairs from $17$ object categories. Additionally, we introduce LAGR, a task-oriented grasp detection method that explicitly addresses both these challenges. Given vision-language inputs, LAGR first generates task-independent grasp proposals along with a physical feasibility score, and then scores the generated grasps with a language-conditioned re-ranker that reasons over all proposals jointly. Experiments show that LAGR outperforms the next best method by 9.3 percentage points. We further validate LAGR on real objects by executing task-oriented grasps with a Franka Research 3 robot.

</details>


### [335] [Navigating Robots from Unknown and Uncalibrated Exocentric Cameras via Map Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23894)

Abstract: Exocentric cameras, mounted on walls or ceilings in indoor environments, offer wider vantage points and are less easily occluded than robot-mounted cameras, making them an appealing alternative for perception in robot navigation algorithms. Previous methods that use such cameras, however, require their pose and intrinsics to be known before deployment, demanding calibration that is time-consuming, requires specialized personnel, and must be repeated whenever a camera moves or a new room is navigated. We propose Robot-centric Obstacle Maps from Unknown and uncalibrated eXocentric cameras (ROMUX), an agent that navigates a robot toward point goals while avoiding obstacles using exocentric cameras that are both uncalibrated, meaning the system does not require their pose or intrinsic information as input, and unknown, meaning it is not fit to a specific camera during training and does not require pre-deployment fine-tuning. We train a cross-view transformer on large-scale simulation data to predict metric robot-centric obstacle maps from exocentric images, without conditioning image tokens on camera information. A small policy trained with Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) then navigates on the predicted maps toward a goal coordinate relative to the robot. We benchmark our agent in simulation with cameras randomized per episode, and it matches the success rates of a PPO policy that navigates from a robot-mounted camera. Further, ROMUX works with one camera or multiple cameras with partial views and has been transferred zero-shot to a real robot using a camera at varying positions and orientations across various room layouts.

</details>


### [336] [Scalable Iterative Multi-Agent Path Finding under Turn Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23911)

Abstract: Large-scale multi-mobile-robot systems require fast and reliable path planning under realistic motion constraints. Multi-Agent Path Finding with turn actions (MAPF$_T$) explicitly models orientation changes and turn-induced delays, but these constraints increase the computational burden of search-based methods. Priority-inheritance-based planning offers a highly scalable alternative, yet directly extending it to MAPF$_T$ is nontrivial because turn actions break the immediate node-vacation assumption underlying conventional priority inheritance. To address this challenge, this paper proposes PIBT$^*_T$, an efficient iterative solver for MAPF$_T$. PIBT$^*_T$ advances scalable multi-agent coordination by preserving the lightweight single-timestep planning mechanism of Priority Inheritance with Backtracking (PIBT), while introducing dedicated mechanisms to handle turn-induced dependencies, local deadlocks, livelock, and inefficient repeated pushing. Experiments on standard MAPF benchmarks show that PIBT$^*_T$ achieves high success rates and strong scalability, solving instances with up to 1000 agents within seconds. For realistic engineering environments, Action Dependency Graph (ADG)-based execution simulation and a flexible manufacturing system case study show that turn-aware planning better reflects robot execution and reduces actual makespan compared with classical MAPF planning. Experiments with TurtleBot4 robots further validate the practical feasibility of the proposed method.

</details>


### [337] [ReLoco: Unified Humanoid Locomotion and Fall Recovery via Behavior-Conditioned Motion Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23916)

Abstract: Humanoid robots must recover from falls and resume commanded motion to sustain locomotion. Learning these behaviors in one policy requires supervision that accommodates both upright movement and ground-contact recovery. We present ReLoco, a framework that uses behavior-conditioned adversarial motion priors to learn locomotion, get-up from arbitrary fallen postures, and command resumption within one proprioceptive policy. During training, robot posture selects the expert pool and conditions a shared discriminator with two behavior-specific outputs. Task rewards and shared regularization support command tracking and recovery, while temporary assistance facilitates exploration. A simulation-based refinement stage supplies robot-executed motion references for this supervision. Ablations examine conditional supervision, output separation, and reference quality under matched settings. At a common training iteration, the full policy achieves $96.80\%$ independent get-up success and $96.96\%$ push-recovery success, including command resumption. Its torso planar velocity root-mean-square error is $0.1266$~m/s, $14.7\%$ lower than the conditional single-output variant, with similar joint-angle discrepancy measured by dynamic time warping. Deployment on the 64.5-kg Tiangong~3.0 humanoid demonstrates walking, running, get-up, and locomotion resumption after falls using one proprioceptive actor, without controller switching or on-robot policy fine-tuning.

</details>


### [338] [Communications-free Collective Intelligence Via Allocentric Belief](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24858)
*Cameron Mehlman,Arjun Mulchandani,Hovik Grigoryan,Anurag Koyyada,Gregory Falco*

Abstract: A team of skilled card players share no information amongst each other, and yet still are able to coordinate their play by watching the same players and reading the same tells. Autonomous systems have failed to exploit this logic, relying instead on explicit communication or complex state estimation schemes that can fail when it matters most. Without it, decentralized agents diverge in their world beliefs, and coordination collapses. Here we show that anchoring all agents to a single shared external actor, a common witness whose reactions encode team state, produces spontaneous, communication-free coordination. An allocentric belief network trained on witness behavior allows each agent to independently reconstruct full team state, keeping group beliefs consistent with no inter-agent exchange. Across five competitive environments allocentric belief alignment outperforms egocentric approaches in collective performance, and even scales to sixty-agent teams where standard methods fail. These results establish a new principle for collective intelligence: in the absence of communication, a shared gaze replaces a shared channel.

</details>


### [339] [CommDreamer: Radio-Geometric World Models for Mapless Cellular-Connected UAV Trajectory Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24244)
*Yuqi Ping,Tianhao Liang,Tingting Zhang*

Abstract: Reliable cellular connectivity and collision-free flight are essential for unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) operations in low-altitude urban canyons, where dense 3D buildings obstruct flight clearance, shadow serving base stations, and create strong co-channel interference at street intersections. Existing planners either require prior 3D city meshes and channel knowledge maps unavailable onboard, or rely on model-free reinforcement learning with low sample efficiency and poor cross-city generalization. We propose CommDreamer, a mapless radio-geometric world model that fuses raw onboard 3D LiDAR scans, multicell cellular reports, and shift-invariant relative kinematics to jointly infer local obstacle clearance and directional signal-to-interference-plus-noise ratio (SINR) dynamics. Using a categorical recurrent state-space model, CommDreamer compresses multimodal measurement histories into a coordinate-invariant latent belief, enabling the UAV to foresee deep shadow zones and train a lightweight online policy purely via multi-step latent imagination without prior maps or antenna patterns. Zero-shot evaluations across unseen urban morphologies in our open-source ray-tracing simulator, AeroCity-RT, show that CommDreamer converges within 150 episodes where model-free reinforcement learning fails to complete flights, achieving 100.0% mission success with zero collisions while lifting median route SINR to 8.02 dB and cutting effective SINR outage by over 50% relative to radio-blind world-model and reactive LiDAR baselines.

</details>


### [340] [GTI: Grounding Task Intent Throughout Long-Horizon VLA Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23926)

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models generate local actions from visual observations, language, and robot state, but long-horizon manipulation requires the global task intent to remain grounded in the currently executable context. We propose GTI (Grounding Task Intent), which uses the current observation and execution history to localize the active subtask and its execution phase. For the same local subtask, GTI generates two phase-dependent visual targets: an interaction/contact state and a local completion state. The local language instruction and the corresponding phase-specific visual target jointly condition the VLA, specifying both what to do now and what state should be reached at the current phase. During execution, anomaly monitoring and local recovery re-establish an intent-consistent execution context through rollback, re-observation, and phase re-evaluation after persistent deviation. We evaluate GTI on RoboCasa365, VLA-Arena Horizon-Long, and real-world PiPER tasks. Relative to matched Global-FT baselines, GR00T success on seen composite RoboCasa365 tasks increases from 14.6%/12.1% to 31.8%/20.4% under ID/OOD evaluation. On VLA-Arena, GTI+$π_{0.5}$ reaches 47.6% overall success versus 45.2% for an adapted ForeAct baseline, with gains of 5.2/6.0 percentage points on the harder L1/L2 tasks. In the three real-robot task experiments, mean full-task success increases from 21.7% to 59.2%. The results indicate that persistent semantic intent localization and phase-conditioned visual grounding improve multi-stage execution, while failure-triggered local recovery provides additional deployment-time support.

</details>

Abstract: Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) are expected to achieve semantic reasoning, intelligent decision-making, and long-horizon navigation under strict onboard constraints. While recent foundation-model-driven approaches have shown promising progress toward this goal, existing methods remain limited by challenges in 3D data availability, trajectory feasibility, long-horizon reasoning, and computational efficiency in complex environments. To bridge these gaps, we propose VLN-AR, a hierarchical vision-language-navigation (VLN) framework for aerial robots. VLN-AR decouples multimodal reasoning and low-level control, enabling structured prediction of 3D pose commands and navigation intentions that are translated into dynamically feasible actions. To mitigate data scarcity and domain gaps, we introduce a fully automated 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS)-based pipeline to generate scalable photorealistic UAV navigation data. A progressive three-stage training strategy further enhances grounding, scene reasoning, and complex navigation. In addition, a lightweight cognitive agent improves long-horizon reasoning via memory-aware task decomposition. Finally, we design onboard optimization techniques, including token sparsification and model lightweighting, achieving 7.1x-9.4x speedup while maintaining high navigation performance on resource-limited UAV platforms. Experiments show great improvements over existing methods in both simulation and real-world UAV navigation. Project website: https://wyz000.github.io/VLN-AR/

</details>

Abstract: Diverse, semantically synchronized, and low-latency facial expressions are essential for human-robot interaction. While large language models (LLMs) have endowed anthropomorphic robots with fluent speech, facial expression still struggles to follow the conversation. Motivated by this gap, we present Emo-H2A (hidden-to-actuation), a paradigm that drives a facial robot directly from LLM hidden states during autoregressive decoding. Without a separate emotion label, expression stays continuous and context-sensitive, and the face moves with the unfolding reply. We instantiate this paradigm as EmoManifold, a training-free method that extracts a valence-arousal-dominance (VAD) coordinate from each hidden state, projects it, and drives the face at token granularity. The readout is checked on six language models, and the same projection drives our 29-degree-of-freedom (DoF) facial robot. When driven by this projection, the face shifts its expression in the matching direction on 87.5% of within-reply valence transitions, whereas holding a single label for the whole reply leaves directional tracking near the 50% chance level, and first moves in 0.96s rather than waiting 10-26s for the reply to finish and a turn-level label to return.

</details>

Abstract: Dense SLAM systems based on 3D Gaussian splatting build maps that render photorealistically, but their surface geometry is poor, and obtaining a mesh requires extraction or fusion stages. Triangle splatting avoids this with surface primitives whose vertices can be triangulated directly into a connected mesh, yet SLAM with this representation has so far relied on photometric tracking, which is too slow for localization at sensor rate. We present Facet-SLAM, the first real-time triangle splatting SLAM system for RGB-D cameras. Fast geometric registration is the natural alternative, but it is not sufficient alone, since the mapper's optimization displaces triangles from the observed surface and makes them unreliable registration targets. We therefore separate the two roles: the tracker sets each triangle's centroid from fused depth and registers frames to these centroids by GICP, while the mapper is restricted to non-translating updates via a change of variables. Our system achieves competitive tracking accuracy at around 100 FPS on a desktop GPU and runs at sensor rate on a Jetson AGX Orin. Compared with the photometric approach, it is over 20 times faster, matches rendering quality, and improves surface accuracy, reducing the chamfer distance of the Delaunay mesh on Replica from 1.55 to 0.86 cm. We will release the implementation at https://github.com/edward62740/Facet-SLAM.

</details>

Abstract: In contact-rich dexterous manipulation, occlusion by the fingers and interacting objects obscures local geometry, preventing visual observations from fully capturing contact locations. Visually similar scenes may also differ subtly in the relative positions of interacting objects, leading to different contact states. We propose 3DTouch, a tactile-geometric conditioning approach for vision-language-action (VLA) models. 3DTouch constructs a shared 3D visuo-tactile representation by expressing RGB-D scene points and dense five-finger tactile measurements in the robot-base frame, preserving each taxel's spatial location and force vector. Hand-object and object-object relations derived from hand landmarks and pose-aligned reconstructed surfaces explicitly describe the relative arrangement of interacting entities. Together, these representations condition the VLA action expert on spatially localized contact forces and relative surface geometry, providing explicit cues for contact adjustment and object alignment. We evaluate 3DTouch on five real-world contact-rich dexterous manipulation tasks using a UR7e robot arm equipped with an XHand dexterous hand. 3DTouch improves the average success rate by 42 percentage points over the corresponding VLA baseline and by 20 percentage points over the strongest evaluated alternative. Ablation studies reveal the complementary benefits of scene geometry and spatial tactile feedback, with further gains from shared spatial grounding and explicit geometric relations.

</details>

Abstract: In many robot telepresence interfaces, movement and viewing are managed through handheld inputs. This can divide attention between steering, looking, and inspection. We compared joystick versus omnidirectional treadmill locomotion and joystick-based versus head-guided camera control in a within-subject 2x2 study in which 17 participants used a virtual reality headset to guide a quadruped robot through a physical maze while locating marked wall symbols and capturing them as visual evidence. Mixed-effects analyses showed that head-guided camera control was associated with faster completion, lower wall-contact cost, lower workload, and more favorable presence, usability, technology acceptance, and flow than joystick-based camera control. The strongest overall objective performance was observed in the joystick locomotion plus head-guided camera condition, whereas the omnidirectional treadmill plus head-guided camera condition emerged as the strongest experiential compromise. Exploratory camera analyses showed that head-guided camera control reduced switching, increased reliance on the pan-tilt view, and supported a more stable viewing strategy across the maze. These strategy differences were consistent with the performance advantage, especially for wall-contact duration. Targets captured remained near ceiling and simulator sickness remained low across all conditions. Overall, the results indicate that viewpoint control was the stronger differentiator in this combined navigation and inspection task, while locomotion still shaped the objective and experiential tradeoff.

</details>


### [346] [CoRA-VTLA: Contact-Guided Attention and Queryable Response Memory for Tactile Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23963)

Abstract: In precision manipulation, small errors in alignment or force can cause jamming or deformation, making contact feedback essential for adapting pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models to these tasks. A single tactile observation leaves the response to recent actions ambiguous. Direct tactile fusion during post-training can also disperse the action expert's visual attention and reduce the share it allocates to contact regions. To address these challenges, CoRA-VTLA augments pretrained VLAs with lightweight tactile modules. A queryable action-response memory retrieves recent contact responses so that the action expert can combine them with immediate tactile feedback. Contact-guided gaze supervision concentrates the visual attention of the tactile semantic token on image regions corresponding to fingertip contact. CoRA-VTLA achieves 73.33% mean success across three MuJoCo tasks, compared with 50% for direct tactile fusion with the same sensor inputs. It also achieves 67% success on an optical-tactile task from the public UniVTAC benchmark and 66.7% mean success across three real-robot tasks. These results show that tactile post-training helps pretrained VLAs adjust to changing contact.

</details>


### [347] [LaST1.0: A Unified Latent World-Action Model with Control-Centric SpatioTemporal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23974)
*Hao Chen,Xuheng Zhang,Zhuangzhe Wu,Jiaming Liu,Shaobo Zhang,Jialin Gao,Zhonghao Yan,Di Wu,Sixiang Chen,Peng Jia,Chi-Wing Fu,Shanghang Zhang,Pheng-Ann Heng*

Abstract: World-Action Models couple action learning with future prediction, enabling robotic policies to capture environmental dynamics for robust control. However, prevailing methods take a reconstruction-oriented approach to model future videos, allocating substantial capacity to appearance details that are incidental to control. In this work, we reformulate world modeling for manipulation as control-centric future prediction in latent space, and present LaST1.0, a unified latent World-Action Model. Rather than modeling an observation-complete video future, LaST1.0 predicts compact, geometry-aware latent targets derived from future wrist views, focusing the supervision on the evolving robot-object interactions required for fine-grained manipulation. To better support this control-centric paradigm, LaST1.0 revisits the visual input interface and attention mechanism of its unified architecture. It adopts a vision-encoder-free design with a lightweight patch embedder, exposing fine-grained visual cues directly to the backbone, and introduces LaST-Attention for structured spatiotemporal interactions across modalities. Evaluations across two robot embodiments, five challenging manipulation tasks, and three simulation benchmarks---RLBench, LIBERO, and LIBERO-Plus---demonstrate that LaST1.0 achieves state-of-the-art success rates and generalizes to novel scenarios. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of control-centric latent world modeling for robust manipulation.

</details>


### [348] [Modelling the human-exoskeleton interaction interface - Benchmarking MuJoCo's deformable model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24008)

Abstract: The human-exoskeleton interaction interface, consisting of the exoskeleton attachment and the underlying human soft tissue, can affect the transfer of assistance. MuJoCo's \textit{flexcomp} has been previously used to model the soft tissue, but its accuracy has not been benchmarked against a finite element model. We benchmarked the \textit{flexcomp} Saint Venant-Kirchhoff (StVK) and Stable Neo-Hookean (SNH) models against COMSOL's Yeoh model in three cases: \textit{case 1}) spherical indentation, \textit{case 2}) compression, and \textit{case 3}) compression of a thigh. We compared force and strain energy as a function of indentation depth, hysteresis and computation time. MuJoCo's models capture force trends with a correlation of 0.97-0.99, but showed a stiffer response, which could be attributed to the volumetric locking of the linear finite elements. The MuJoCo models systematically underestimated hysteresis as MuJoCo's Rayleigh damping could not substitute a viscoelastic model. Moreover, MuJoCo's computational advantage was speed dependent: it was 2-2.5 times faster for spherical indentation, but COMSOL was faster for the other cases, which have more degrees of freedom and contacts. Although MuJoCo's \textit{flexcomp} offers a combination of soft-tissue modelling and multibody dynamics, the limitations regarding the stiffening and hysteresis should be addressed to be used for the analysis of the human-exoskeleton interaction interface.

### [349] [WAM-Policy: World Action Models with On-Policy Distillation for Quadrupedal Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24011)

Abstract: Arm-free quadrupeds can perform loco-manipulation, walking on three legs while manipulating with a front leg, yet no existing policy for it follows language instructions. World action models (WAMs) have emerged as effective language-conditioned manipulation policies, but are pretrained for robot arms and sample slowly. WAM-Policy turns one WAM into a quadrupedal loco-manipulation policy via two stages. The first, Cross-Morphology Policy Adaptation and Acceleration, adapts the arm-centric WAM to quadrupeds and distills the transferred policy into a student with one video and two action steps by on-policy self-distillation (OPSD). This student, however, is bounded by its teacher, which only imitates demonstrations. The second stage, Task-Specific Improvement and Merging, therefore tunes the teacher by reinforcement learning (RL) and merges the slow specialists into one few-step student by multi-teacher on-policy distillation (M-OPD). On QLM-Bench, ten simulated tasks with 1,000 demonstrations synthesized without teleoperation, the transferred policy reaches 77.0%, and the OPSD student keeps 76.7% while sampling 14 times faster. Task-specific RL and M-OPD lift it to 85.0%. We also deploy the OPSD student on a real Unitree Go2 Air. WAM-Policy thus achieves a stronger quadrupedal policy at a fraction of the inference cost.

</details>


### [350] [JEPA Dots: Dual-Latent World Model for Language-Guided Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24062)

Abstract: Predictive world models for language-guided robot control face a fundamental conditioning challenge: instructions should influence action selection without altering the predicted consequences of fixed actions under identical observations. To address this challenge, we introduce JEPA Dots (Dynamics-Oriented Task Separation), a compact dual-latent framework that jointly learns visual dynamics and language-guided control through separate dynamics and task representations. Language-conditioned task features guide a flow-matching policy, while masked attention restricts world prediction to instruction-independent dynamics history and supplied actions. In closed-loop evaluation on 40 trained LIBERO tasks, JEPA Dots achieves 84.9% overall success with approximately 39.1M parameters, compared with the published 78.7% of NanoVLA-S with 161.0M parameters under a different evaluation protocol. In the reference experiment, JEPA Dots also improves overall success from 76.0% for our task-ID-conditioned adaptation baseline to 84.9%, with LIBERO-Goal success increasing from 67.1% to 88.5%. Instruction-switching experiments further confirm that task representations and policy outputs respond to language changes, while dynamics representations and world predictions remain invariant under fixed observations and supplied actions.

</details>


### [351] [SemPhys: Semantic-Physical Co-Learning for Humanoid Motion Generation via Differentiable World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24074)
*Zejian Kang,Yeqiang Wang,Zekai Chen,Boyang Shen,Mingyu Ma,Bingqian Wu,Fan Zhou,Jianjie Zhang,Chentao Shen,Wentao Yang,Rui Qian,Xiangru Huang*

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: In human-robot collaboration, natural language lets people direct a humanoid's whole-body movements without programming joint trajectories. A common approach generates a kinematic reference from an instruction and relies on a tracking controller to execute it. However, as the controller maintains balance under contact constraints, the executed motion can depart from the reference in ways that change the action it conveys. Our central insight is that semantic alignment must be evaluated directly on the complete motion the robot physically executes. Building on this insight, we introduce \textbf{SemPhys}, a framework that trains motion generators using semantic feedback from simulated executions. A differentiable world model and a fixed tracking controller predict a short segment of a recorded execution. SemPhys inserts this prediction at its original position, scores the complete trajectory against the instruction, and backpropagates through the predicted segment. A paired anchoring loss stabilizes adaptation by keeping each generated reference close to its counterpart from the initial generator. On BONES-SEED, SemPhys largely preserves instruction retrieval accuracy from reference to execution and achieves the highest retrieval accuracy on executed motions among the evaluated methods.

</details>


### [352] [Meaning Reaches Object Choice but Not Completion: Where Is a VLA Vulnerable to Landmark Synonyms?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24076)
*Ryoichi Kurihara,Kota Sunamoto*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: VLA policies are fragile to rewording. Success rates alone cannot say whether a policy fails to choose the object or to complete the task, nor where in the policy to intervene. We study the released GR00T N1.7 on two LIBERO-Spatial tasks differing only in the landmark word telling which of two bowls to take. Synonyms absent from the training instructions send the policy to the bowl they name, unlike made-up words, yet complete 67.6% of one task's closed-loop episodes, against the original word's 100%. Before the connector between the vision-language model and the action generator, replacing only the landmark tokens' vectors with the other landmark's (pasting) sends nearly every episode to the other bowl. After the connector, pasting at the landmark, the remaining instruction, or the image tokens alone rarely moves the choice, but any two do. Hiding the landmark tokens from the action generator after the connector still lowers completion for the synonyms that fall short. Finetuning on diversified landmark words repairs that one task's completion for synonyms absent from finetuning; finetuning on the original instructions does not. Training only the connector on the diversified words suffices, as does copying the connector of a policy fully finetuned on them into one finetuned on the original instructions. An open-loop score on starting frames misses that weights outside the connector also carry part of the repair. For now, on this evidence, robustness to rewording should be judged in closed loop, with actions executed, intervening on both sides of the connector, scoring choice and completion separately.

</details>


### [353] [Lost Between Mind, Model Collaboration, and Motion: Understanding and Measuring Cross-Model Action Misalignment in Commercial Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24100)

Abstract: Embodied robots increasingly rely on large language models (LLMs) to interpret user intent and coordinate with robot-specific action models for physical interaction. This multi-model pipeline creates a critical semantic boundary across mind, model collaboration, and motion: semantic consistency may break between what the large model understands, how the small action model translates that understanding into executable commands, and what physical actions the robot ultimately performs. As a security consequence, commercial robots may execute unintended physical actions even when user inputs contain no explicit action commands. To measure this problem, we develop TARS, a black-box framework that explores the command-reasoning-action pipeline and automatically discovers and validates unintended physical behaviors. We evaluate TARS on four commercial robots across 44 experimental settings, conducting 133,400 executions. TARS uncovers widespread cross-model action misalignment and achieves up to 94% verified action coverage.

</details>


### [354] [Asymmetric Decomposition for Multi-Agent Path Finding with Unassigned Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24108)

Abstract: Robots without assigned tasks may obstruct active robots, while routing them to designated parking locations can cause unnecessary travel. This article proposes flow-oracle conflict-based search (FOCBS) for Multi-Agent Path Finding with unassigned agents (MAPF-UA). FOCBS searches over assigned-agent paths using conflict-based search (CBS) and routes targetless unassigned agents through a min-cost flow oracle. When the selected assigned-agent paths leave unassigned agents no collision-free paths, flow-conflict branching converts min-cut blockers into assigned-agent constraints, which guide the search without excluding optimal solutions. A batched zero-cost warm start accelerates the oracle, and start-avoiding tie-breaking reduces unassigned-agent movement. For feasible instances on finite graphs, we prove termination and global minimization of assigned-agent sum of service time, measured by last-arrival times. Unassigned-agent movement is minimized conditional on the selected assigned-agent paths, without a global lexicographic optimality guarantee. Benchmark experiments show that FOCBS scales well with the number of unassigned agents, and ablation studies show that, compared with discarding non-completable assigned-agent paths, flow-conflict branching solves substantially more instances while retaining optimality. Simulation and physical-robot experiments demonstrate plan executability, with laboratory tests reducing fleet travel by 40.8% relative to the parking-based coordination baseline. The source code and related resources are available at https://github.com/Chin-hsi/focbs

</details>


### [355] [Ego2X: Contact-aware Transfer from Egocentric Human Data to Any Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24138)
*Xinke Lyu,Bowen Yang,Zhicheng Wang*

Abstract: Embodied models depend on real interaction data, yet collecting it on physical robots is slow and labor-intensive. Recent ego-to-robot pipelines retarget first-person view human videos to robot data via transferring hand and arm motion. They judge fidelity mainly by visual appearance. However, trajectories often fail in terms of physics: fingers hover beside objects, and grippers close on empty space. We argue that how the object moves and the contacts that drive the motion count. Thus, we present Ego2X, an object-centric framework that converts monocular egocentric video into physically executable robot data across dexterous hands and parallel grippers. Ego2X centers on CORE (Contact-centric Object-interaction REpresentation), which records each contact on the object by its surface anchor, as well as relative force, grasp mode, and interaction state. Throughout the pipeline, CORE corrects hand-object drift via object-relative constraints, steers inverse kinematics toward contacts the robot must realize, and preserves held objects during arm erasure and robot compositing. On HOT3D, contact-driven correction reduces in-contact hand depth error by 33.7%, while on EgoDex, contact-driven retargeting realizes 62.9% of annotated contacts, surpassing the official HuRo pipeline (27.9%). In RoboTwin 2.0, post-training X-VLA on Ego2X episodes alone, without simulator demonstrations, raises task success rate from 40.5% zero-shot to 67.0% across 200 paired scenes. We will publicly release our conversion pipeline and CORE-annotated dataset for dexterous hands and parallel grippers.

</details>


### [356] [BiPathCPG: A Novel Load-Sharing Design Architecture for Compound Planetary Gearboxes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24142)
*Deepak Kapa,Aman Singh,Saketh Vegesna,Suryank Joshi,Mayank Goswami,Shishir Kolathaya*

Abstract: Quasi-Direct Drive (QDD) actuators are widely used in legged robots due to their low reflected inertia, high backdrivability, and high torque density, commonly employing planetary or cycloidal gearboxes. Single-stage planetary gearboxes offer high efficiency but limited torque density; multi-stage designs improve torque density at the cost of efficiency; and cycloidal gearboxes provide high stiffness but suffer from torque ripple. Compound Planetary Gearboxes (CPG) offer a compromise, but conventional designs result in increased actuator width, mass, and reduced torsional stiffness in 3D-printed actuators. This paper proposes the BiPath Compound Planetary Gearbox (BiPathCPG), a novel architecture that introduces an idle second-stage sun gear and load-sharing second-stage planets within the otherwise unused space of a conventional CPG to simultaneously improve stiffness, reduce actuator width, and reduce mass. An optimization framework computes BiPathCPG parameters for a given motor and target gear ratio. The architecture is validated through a 3D-printed proof-of-concept actuator design, benchmarked against a conventional CPG using custom-built test rigs. The BiPathCPG achieves a torsional stiffness of 1394 Nm/rad, a 1.70x improvement over the conventional CPG, with a 32% reduction in backlash, 25 g lower mass, and approximately 6.4 mm reduction in axial width, while achieving a peak stall torque of 36.49 Nm.

</details>


### [357] [Assessing Vision-Language Models for Early Prediction of Unsafe Situations in Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24146)

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) process visual information and textual instructions and, by using their ability to understand and reason about context, are well-suited for predicting robot failures before they occur. However, evidence supporting this remains limited. To address this, we present an empirical evaluation of VLMs for predicting robot failures. In particular, we experiment with Qwen, InternVL, and LLaVA-OneVision across three robotic case studies (HuRoN, Pal Robotics, and CrowdBot), analyzing both video and odometry data for early failure warning (i.e., before an unsafe situation occurs). Specifically, we evaluate their performance in classifying situations as safe, potentially unsafe, and unsafe, together with uncertainty quantified using various metrics, and study the relationship between uncertainty and performance. Our results showed that Qwen provides the strongest overall classification and prediction-reliability performance, while InternVL shows stronger early-warning capability in several configurations. The results further show that the effect of robot-state information and temporal history depends on the model and evaluation objective. Overall, our findings indicate that general-purpose VLMs can support early robot-safety monitoring, but the most suitable configuration depends on whether the priority is accurate assessment of safety-state, reliable confidence and uncertainty, or timely warning before unsafe events.

</details>


### [358] [BEV-Diffusion: Camera-Only Traversability Navigation with BEV Memory and Efficient Cross-Robot Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24155)

Abstract: Robot navigation requires both an understanding of the scene and an efficient adaptation to new robots, yet current methods rely on expensive LiDAR sensors, long mapping and modular pipelines. We present BEV-Diffusion, a camera-only model that learns a bird's-eye view (BEV) traversability map, its uncertainty and the path in a single network. A frozen DINOv2 backbone encodes consecutive RGB frames, which inverse perspective mapping and Bayesian fusion lift into the BEV, and a ranking loss keeps outdoor traversability graded rather than binary, separating asphalt from grass (median 0.94 vs. 0.75). On this map, a transformer denoiser generates polar waypoints in a few DDIM steps, and uncertainty-aware guidance refines the path and ends it where the map is no longer reliable. At deployment, a metric BEV memory keeps obstacles after they leave the camera view and warm starts each sample from the previous, ego-motion compensated path. Because the planner jointly learns the path, the map, and its uncertainty, it selects routes through terrain it can confidently traverse. In an unseen courtyard, a wheeled-legged robot autonomously traveled 120 m, completing 96% of the route without intervention, compared with 75-88% for the baselines. Even under low-light conditions after sunset, the proposed model completed the route without requiring any intervention. Retraining only the BEV head, about 3% of the parameters, adapts the model to a small legged robot in 2.3 min. Indoors, the memory gets this robot out of maze-like traps: it retains 62-66% of the nearby out-of-view obstacles, against at most 3% for the baselines, and the robot achieves 67-100% task progress, compared with 20-40% for the evaluated baselines.

</details>


### [359] [Efficient World-Model Planning via Physics-Guided Search Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24171)

Abstract: World models support autonomous driving by evaluating candidate trajectories through predicted future scene evolution. However, accurate future prediction does not ensure road-compliant execution; when all candidates violate road boundaries, re-ranking cannot select a feasible trajectory. We propose Physics-Guided Search Distillation (PGSD), a single-view planning framework that combines future representations and execution feedback for verified beyond-pool repair through three components: (1) Execution-Aware Selection checks ego-vehicle road compliance under simulated execution and selects the highest-scoring feasible trajectory; (2) a Clearance-Aware Search Teacher generates beyond-pool corrections and selects demonstrations with larger minimum road-boundary clearance during execution to buffer imitation errors; and (3) a Future-Conditioned Repair Student predicts one correction from rewarder features and execution feedback for single-rollout verification. The pretrained planner and rewarder remain frozen; no future scenes are generated online. On NAVSIM v1, single-view PGSD achieves 90.97 PDMS, exceeding all compared prior single-view planners and retaining 73.2% of the search teacher's PDMS gain over execution-aware selection. On NAVSIM v2 navhard, it reaches 38.96 EPDMS, improving on WorldDrive by 11.24 points (40.5%). The student replaces up to ten search rollouts with a single verification rollout per repair trigger. HUGSIM Hard examples provide qualitative evidence of zero-shot transfer.

</details>

Abstract: Ground-based free-flyer testbeds reproduce the planar motion of a free-floating spacecraft using air bearings, but not the relative dynamics of spacecraft in orbit. We present a dual-actuation architecture that physically emulates Clohessy-Wiltshire dynamics on an open-source free-flyer. Two model predictive controllers run in parallel: one drives the solenoid thrusters with an orbital dynamics model, representing the spacecraft under test; the other drives bidirectional propellers that physically apply the orbital accelerations, continuously updating its reference to account for thruster-induced velocity changes. A dynamic similitude maps the orbital scenario onto the limited floor area and actuation rates of the testbed, letting the spacecraft controller run directly at orbital scale. We validate the architecture on hardware and against a high-fidelity orbital simulation, where the orbital accelerations instead arise naturally, both driven by the identical spacecraft controller. Their agreement indicates that the testbed reproduces the relative orbital motion the controller would encounter in flight.

</details>

Abstract: Visual inspection in field robotics and environmental monitoring must localize multiple targets under strict compute and sensing caps. Exhaustive sweeps waste calls on absent classes, while myopic and search-free policies stumble over heterogeneous tariff cliffs or discard viewport edits whose value emerges only after re-querying. We formulate inspection over frozen detectors as neuro-symbolic observation planning under partial observability, decoupling a compact recurrent world model (WM) from exact symbolic feasibility and tariff rules. Receding-horizon beam search with online replanning jointly schedules class queries, crops, enhancements, and termination (stop), paired with slack-aware shadow pricing and legal fallback at larger budgets. Across eight terrestrial, optical, and sonar benchmarks, multi-step lookahead outperforms static schedules and search-free offline RL under tight budgets -- gaining up to 28.4 AP percentage points over static priority on RUOD and 15.1 on ARIS -- while dynamic pricing lifts large-budget performance on all eight domains at 11 ms planning cost. Ultimately, pairing lightweight learned dynamics with exact symbolic constraints turns frozen detectors into adaptive, budget-aware inspection agents without retraining.

</details>

Abstract: Precise docking requires a differential-drive robot to correct lateral offset through coupled rotation and translation within a short approach distance. We combine global navigation with terminal control using complementary LiDAR and visual observations. A line fitted to the cabinet front gives normal distance and heading. An AprilTag identifies the workstation; intersecting its center ray with the LiDAR plane determines tangential position. Taught observations define the docking reference and can cancel repeatable fixed biases under unchanged mounting and geometry. Lateral pre-alignment and continuous visual servoing coordinate the approach, with predictive stopping to account for low-speed actuation lag. Across two sessions using the same navigation and docking code at five workstations on Robot 2, all 111 tasks completed, including 101 on the first attempt. Relative to the taught observations, onboard longitudinal, lateral and heading residual root-mean-square values were 1.51 mm, 5.93 mm and 0.238 degrees, respectively. Joint acceptance within plus or minus 10 mm per position axis and plus or minus 1 degree heading was 96.4% (107/111), or 95.5% (106/111) with a plus or minus 0.5 degree heading limit.

Abstract: 3D object reconstruction is essential for robotic perception and manipulation, yet conventional approaches predominantly rely on visual observations that can be limited by occlusion or unavailable in visually constrained environments. In this work, we propose a vision-free, training-free, and category-agnostic framework for reconstructing 3D object meshes from sparse tactile contacts without relying on pretrained shape models. A 6-DoF robotic arm equipped with a 20-DoF multi-finger robotic hand collects tactile observations using 3x3 pressure sensor arrays mounted on the finger pads. The acquired tactile measurements are transformed into 3D contact constraints through robot kinematics and integrated into a multi-scale Gaussian Process Implicit Surface (GPIS). To address sparse and incomplete surface observations, the proposed method incorporates contact confidence weighting, weak pseudo-surface constraints, and an observation-driven geometric prior to preserve local surface geometry and provide structural guidance in unobserved regions. Real-world experiments on six objects achieve a Chamfer Distance (CD) of 1.610, an Earth Mover's Distance (EMD) of 7.738, and an F-Score of 95.00, with reconstruction completed in an average of 49.99 s per object. These results demonstrate that sparse tactile sensing can provide sufficient geometric information for continuous 3D object reconstruction without visual or semantic information.

</details>

Abstract: Intuitive robot programming approaches, such as Programming by Demonstration, enable non-experts to teach robots tasks through hand-guided demonstrations. Abstract: Visual planning with a frozen joint-embedding world model supports robotic reaching and object manipulation by evaluating candidate action sequences against an image goal before execution. Standard cross-entropy method (CEM) planners roll out every candidate independently through a billion-parameter predictor, even when nearby actions induce similar scene changes. This redundant computation grows with population size and search rounds, slowing closed-loop replanning. We observe that candidates within a planning step share an observation history and thus a common action-to-feature map. Based on this insight, we propose ProbeShare, which measures local responses with a few representative probes and reconstructs each candidate's feature grid through scene-conditioned, geometry-aware weighting. When promising candidates move beyond existing probe coverage, ProbeShare evaluates a new probe to rescore nearby candidates. Across four RoboCasa tasks, ProbeShare accelerates episode planning by 43.8--128.9 times over dense RoboJEPA and at least $14.2\times$ over competitive baselines, with greater mean goal progress in a matched-time comparison.

</details>

Abstract: Robotic ultrasound-guided needle interventions have primarily focused on target localization and accurate needle placement, while the subsequent acquisition of diagnostic material remains comparatively underexplored. Moreover, pleural fluid aspiration and tissue biopsy are typically addressed as separate procedures, despite sharing common requirements for ultrasound guidance, safe access, and sample transport. This work presents a unified robotic framework for ultrasound-guided fluid and tissue extraction using a single vacuum-assisted end effector. The proposed system combines force controlled ultrasound scanning, semantic target identification, automated needle deployment, and procedure specific material acquisition. A coaxial needle assembly and shared pump assisted pathway enable direct pleural fluid aspiration, while controlled cannula actuation provides the additional cutting action required for solid-tissue acquisition. End-to-end evaluation using a Franka Emika Panda robot over 50 phantom trials achieved successful extraction in 23/25 pleural fluid aspiration trials (92\%) and 22/25 tissue acquisition trials (88\%). Following successful tissue-target access, acquisition and transport were completed in all 22 trials.

Abstract: Interactive imitation learning enables robots to learn from human interventions during deployment. These interactions naturally produce sparse human corrections and abundant autonomous robot experience without human action labels. Yet existing methods either leave this experience unused for action supervision or imitate historical robot actions, which can reinforce suboptimal behavior. To address this limitation, we propose Set-valued Relabeling of Autonomous experience (SeRA), a framework that learns from autonomous robot experience through set-valued supervision of policy relabels. Later policy checkpoints relabel this experience, and agreement across checkpoints filters the relabels. Since agreement does not guarantee correctness, SeRA constructs desired action sets around accepted relabels. Following Set-Supervised Diffusion Policy (SDP), it trains an action-chunking diffusion policy to increase the probability of actions within these sets. A step-masked denoising loss combines accepted relabels with available human corrections in partially supervised chunks. Experiments across five simulated manipulation tasks in online and offline settings show that SeRA improves policy performance over SDP and outperforms baselines that imitate historical robot actions or exact relabels. On a real-robot tomato-picking task, SeRA achieves 80% success, compared with 40% for SDP, using the same human feedback and training budget. These results demonstrate that relabeled autonomous robot experience can complement human corrections to improve policy performance.

</details>


### [368] [QDD-MM: A Simple and Compact Open-Source Muscle Module with Quasi-Direct-Drive Motor for Tendon-driven Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24279)
*Kento Kawaharazuka,Yoshimoto Ribayashi,Takahiro Hattori,Shunnosuke Yoshimura,Kei Okada*
