Abstract: Tendon-driven robots, which actuate like biological muscles by using motors to wind wires, offer advantages such as variable stiffness and redundant actuation. Despite these advantages, musculoskeletal structures inherently involve a large number of diverse muscles, making it desirable to compactly modularize each muscle unit and flexibly accommodate diverse muscle arrangements. While various small muscle modules have been proposed to date, existing designs tend to be complex, difficult to maintain, and suffer from low backdrivability and limited actuation speed due to their high gear ratios. In this work, we propose QDD-MM, a muscle module that leverages recent advances in quasi-direct-drive motors to achieve a simpler and more compact structure with higher backdrivability and faster actuation than previous modules. By utilizing quasi-direct-drive motors, the module eliminates the need for dedicated force-sensing mechanisms, significantly simplifying the overall design, while also supporting flexible wire routing in multiple directions, various module-to-module connections, and even the use of the module body itself as a structural component. QDD-MM integrates these capabilities into a reusable muscle module, and its design is openly available at https://github.com/haraduka/qddmm. We developed tendon-driven robots with one joint and either two or three muscles, as well as a partial musculoskeletal humanoid, using the proposed QDD-MM and verified their effectiveness. We expect that this muscle module will democratize tendon-driven robotics and further accelerate progress in the field.

</details>

Abstract: Remote endovascular teleoperation requires network performance to be evaluated in relation to physical navigation and operator experience, while the effects of network degradation on the usability of remote endovascular systems remain insufficiently characterized from the perspective of clinical users. We developed a network emulation and benchmarking framework that combines an endovascular robot with data streaming. We conducted a user study involving eight clinicians from interventional neuroradiology and general interventional radiology and eight engineering students, who performed 480 navigation attempts across 15 network conditions. Communication performance and received video quality were assessed alongside navigation outcomes, confidence, workload and clinician rated acceptability. Under the most impaired combined condition, success was 78.1\%, compared with 93.8\% under the reference condition, and mean attempt time increased from 63.5 to 94.3\,s. At 5\% configured packet loss, 87.5\% of attempts succeeded, although all eight clinicians rated the condition unacceptable. Perceived video quality was associated with clinician confidence and acceptability, with mean within participant Spearman correlations of 0.72 and 0.78, respectively. Framework integration on a second robotic platform additionally supported successful remote navigation over a real network. Our framework provides a reusable basis that can help identify communication bottlenecks and assess their effects on navigation and clinician acceptance for endovascular intervention telesurgery tasks.

</details>


### [370] [Proprioceptive Simultaneous Base and Terrain Elevation Estimation for Legged Robot Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24310)

Abstract: Legged robots in visually degraded environments must rely exclusively on proprioceptive sensing, under which global position and yaw are unobservable. Vertical drift is particularly detrimental, as it corrupts the height reference required by swing-leg trajectories and can destabilize the robot. We treat the terrain itself as a persistent proprioceptive landmark: every footstep provides a height measurement that anchors the vertical axis. We propose Simultaneous Base and Elevation Estimation (SBEE), a real-time open-source framework in which a Left-Invariant Extended Kalman Filter (LI-EKF) for floating-base estimation and a grid-based elevation map are coupled in a bidirectional loop, mirroring SLAM with terrain elevation in place of landmarks. Stable foot contacts insert uncertainty-aware height samples into the map, and previously mapped cells are fed back as uncertainty-weighted vertical pseudo-measurements that act analogously to loop closures. Unlike contact-aided invariant filters, the LI-EKF consumes a kinematic velocity measurement instead of augmenting the state with a contact model per leg, so its state dimension is independent of the number of legs. We evaluate SBEE in MuJoCo on quadrupedal and bipedal platforms and on the ANYmal-D GrandTour dataset over 296 m of locomotion, reducing the absolute trajectory error by 22.1% relative to the best of three state-of-the-art proprioceptive estimators.

</details>

Abstract: Fine-tuning a vision-language-action (VLA) model on a few dozen demonstrations is the standard way to put a manipulation policy on a robot, yet the resulting policy silently assumes that the camera and the robot base stay exactly where they were during data collection: a bumped tripod or a base re-docked a few centimetres away makes it reach for the wrong place. We show that both failures are one phenomenon, a change of the camera-to-base extrinsic that the policy has memorised, and that the robot carries everything needed to undo it. We introduce WACO, a world-anchored canonicalization framework for VLA models: the arm, placed by forward kinematics at the measured joint angles, serves as a metric calibration target, and the static scene serves as the world anchor that tells a moved camera from a moved robot. From a single RGB-D frame, WACO estimates both shifts without ground-truth extrinsics or extra data, canonicalises the image to the calibration viewpoint and the state, action and start pose to the calibration base frame, and fine-tunes the unmodified policy under the artefacts of its own estimator. We also introduce LIBERO-PoseShift, the first benchmark for RGB VLAs with graded camera, base and compound shifts. Across LIBERO-PoseShift, the LIBERO-Plus camera track and four real-world tasks on a UR5e robot, WACO consistently outperforms the standard few-shot recipe and prior viewpoint-robust methods, raising shifted success by 13.2 points in simulation and by 36.7 points on the real robot, recovering 90% of the simulation gap to a ground-truth oracle and 87% of the real-robot gap, and establishing a new state of the art among methods that use no perturbed training data, with no test-time calibration. Project page: https://peacelwh.github.io/WACO/.

</details>

Abstract: Object search in unknown environments requires robots to gather useful observations while avoiding obstacles and accounting for limited sensor visibility. We present SearchDiffusion, a diffusion-based planner that generates position and heading trajectories using a learned dense utility map. The map represents geometric accessibility, useful visibility, and semantic progress. It conditions trajectory generation and provides an objective for sampling guidance. Sampled map hypotheses provide additional geometric context, while geometric and heading guidance, validity checks, and analytical selection support execution. Across 90 simulated tasks on 24 map layouts, SearchDiffusion achieved 95.6\% target detection at greater computational cost. Ablations show complementary benefits from utility and guidance, with limited additional detection benefit from runtime map hypotheses. In ten physical quadruped trials using OneMap relevance maps, eight were successful under operator assessment, without fine-tuning on real-robot data. These results support combining learned spatial utility with guided trajectory generation for object search under partial observability.

</details>


### [373] [Teaching VLMs What to Say, Not How to Reason: Rethinking Counterfactual Reasoning in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24376)
*Hayeon Oh,Donghwan Lee*

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: End-to-end autonomous driving systems have been shown to suffer from shortcut learning and causal confusion, limiting their ability to generalize robustly under distribution shift. To mitigate these limitations, recent works based on Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have explored incorporating linguistic reasoning capabilities into driving decision-making, reporting improvements in driving performance. In particular, counterfactual (CF) reasoning has been proposed to enable self-reflective, high-level causal understanding. However, it remains unclear whether the language-based reasoning mitigates shortcut learning or merely shifts it from visual representations to linguistic patterns, creating an illusion of causal understanding. Moreover, existing approaches often conflate intervention-based reasoning with CF reasoning, leaving the effects of genuine CF reasoning unexplored. In this work, we examine what CF reasoning actually induces in VLM-based autonomous driving and observe that it shifts the action distribution toward more conservative behaviors while increasing uncertainty in reasoning and decision-making. This effect persists under reinforcement fine-tuning, suggesting that it is not merely a transient artifact. Furthermore, visual attention analysis shows that CF reasoning does not alter visual grounding, while causal intervention experiments reveal that masking causally relevant and non-causal factors in accident scenarios results in comparable decision change rates. Overall, these results suggest that CF reasoning does not induce genuine causal reasoning grounded in visual evidence. Instead, it acts as a linguistic shortcut that reshapes the action distribution, teaching models what to say but not how to reason, highlighting a gap between language-based reasoning and causal understanding in autonomous driving.

</details>

Abstract: Guidance vector fields (GVFs) are a popular framework for tasks such as robotic path-following and multi-robot motion coordination, but existing approaches may be non-differentiable or have large derivatives, limiting their use in controllers that require derivatives of the guidance signal, which is common when the GVF is used as an input in a cascaded controller. This paper proposes an infinitely differentiable constructive GVF. The resulting vector field depends on a smoothing parameter and is a generalization of [1], recovering the original GVF in the limit as this parameter approaches zero. It also admits a simple and efficient implementation that preserves smoothness under discretization and can reduce computation time by using substantially fewer curve samples while maintaining small path-following errors. We establish the main theoretical properties and demonstrate its practical advantages through numerical and real-world experiments.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies implicitly assume that third-person cameras remain fixed at their data-collection poses; once a camera is bumped, remounted, or swapped at deployment, task success degrades precipitously even though the underlying task and robot remain unchanged. We present Homeward, a training-free, plug-and-play observation adapter that canonicalizes observations from an uncalibrated deployment camera back into the training view for frozen VLA policies. Our design stems from a closed-loop diagnostic paradox in simulation: while re-rendering perturbed views into the training camera with oracle geometry recovers much of the lost performance, substituting an off-the-shelf feed-forward 3D foundation model can leave the policy worse off than applying no correction at all. This degradation concentrates on the thin, articulated robot arm, which feed-forward depth prediction tears and scatters - yet the arm's exact 3D state is already known from robot kinematics. Homeward exploits this asymmetry: it estimates what is unknown - camera parameters and static scene depth - from the live RGB frame and a single training-view reference, and renders what is known - the moving robot links - directly from the kinematic model. Evaluated across $\sim$300,000 closed-loop episodes spanning four frozen VLAs, Homeward boosts success across all 1,599 camera variants of LIBERO-Plus from 62.5%, 76.5%, 68.0%, and 3.6% to 77.6%, 95.1%, 91.2%, and 26.4% for OpenVLA-OFT, $π_{0.5}$, $π_0$, and OpenVLA, respectively. Without any scene fine-tuning or deployment camera calibration, Homeward extends to 6-DoF random placements, focal-length changes, and, with per-step pose estimation, continuous intra-episode camera drift, while matching or surpassing a calibrated, scene-trained view-synthesis baseline on three of four policies on LIBERO-Plus.

</details>

Abstract: Planning with a visual world model requires deciding both which actions to execute and how long to search for them. We introduce Selective CEM Refinement (SCR), a learned rule for deciding whether to continue cross-entropy method (CEM) search with a frozen LeWorldModel. After five it- erations, the gate measures how far the mean first action chunk has moved, how much the mean terminal cost of elite plans has fallen, and how widely their first chunks vary. It returns the current plan for execution or continues the same search for ten more iterations. The gate requires no addi- tional world-model rollout. On 16 PushT scenes evaluated with two planning seeds, SCR succeeded in 25 of 32 trials, compared with 28 of 32 for fixed 15-iteration search (Fixed-15). All 25 SCR suc- cesses occurred in trials that Fixed-15 also solved, yield- ing 89.29% paired success retention. Across complete episodes, including replanning, SCR evaluated 23.81% fewer candidate plans and reduced mean controller time per eight-environment batch by 17.22%. Its observed suc- cess rate was 5.36 percentage points above an analytic mix- ture of the two fixed-depth controllers at the same expected candidate workload, although the difference changed sign across planning seeds. Compared with gating only at the initial solve under the same random-draw padding proto- col, gating again at replanning produced nine additional early exits. This reduced total candidate evaluations by 15.79%, while successes fell from 27 to 25.

</details>


### [377] [RECLAIM: Robot-Vision-Informed Adversarial Patch Defense via Embodied Attention Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24494)

Abstract: Vision-language-action policies control embodied agents through a perception-action loop grounded in physical interaction, where adversarial patches can corrupt action predictions and thereby alter subsequent observations. To mitigate these threats, many existing defenses rely on policy adaptation or auxiliary models, but require additional training or inference. We introduce RECLAIM, a training-free, inference-time defense that uses a frozen VLA's own attention response to local visual interventions to guide purification during closed-loop embodied manipulation. Our key insight is that attention changes induced by local edits provide feedback for choosing where and how to purify as the robot interacts with its environment. RECLAIM combines this feedback with robot geometry to refine patch localization and select local treatments, then revalidates them as the agent's embodied actions change the observed scene. We evaluate RECLAIM against patches generated by four adversarial attack methods across multiple manipulation task suites under frozen VLA control. Under adversarial patch attacks, RECLAIM succeeds in at least one rollout on 95% of the evaluated tasks and raises rollout success on LIBERO-Object from 0% without defense to 70.0%. Its mean decision time is only 548.9 ms per control step. These results highlight policy-guided visual purification as an effective approach to defending embodied agents against adversarial patches and recovering closed-loop task execution.

</details>

Abstract: Robot learning systems require synchronized, high-quality demonstrations that combine visual, proprioceptive, and action data. However, collecting such datasets with mobile manipulators is often slowed down by fragmented teleoperation interfaces and manual post-processing workflows. This paper presents an integrated framework for teleoperated multimodal data collection on a Clearpath Dingo-O mobile base equipped with a Kinova Gen3 Lite manipulator and an Intel RealSense RGB-D camera. The framework combines single-controller operation of the base, arm, and recording process with an automated ROS-based pipeline for data capture, image-quality filtering, temporal synchronization, visualization, and export to machine-learning-ready Zarr datasets. We validate the system on a pick-and-place mobile-manipulation task in which the robot transports a bottle between two surfaces. The results show that the unified controller supports complete task execution and recording without external commands, while the data pipeline produces synchronized datasets for short demonstrations and identifies corrupted visual frames. The proposed framework provides a practical basis for producing structured demonstrations for robot-learning research.

</details>

Abstract: In this work, we introduce OmniFish, a bioinspired modular soft robotic fish designed to emulate the full spectrum of body and/or caudal fin (BCF) locomotion modes within a single robotic platform. The design combines morphology averaging across representative fish species with a tendon-driven compliant segmented spine whose effective stiffness can be reconfigured through progressive mechanical jamming. By selectively constraining body segments, OmniFish shifts deformation from whole-body undulation to posterior body bending and localized tail oscillation, enabling bioinspired Anguilliform, Subcarangiform, Carangiform, Thunniform, and Ostraciiform swimming using a single tendon-driven actuator. The design was guided by morphometric analysis and evaluated through caudal-fin thrust characterization, numerical body-drag simulations, kinematic analysis, and swimming performance experiments. Kinematic analysis confirmed the intended robot transition from distributed body undulation to localized tail oscillation. Swimming experiments demonstrated distinct mode-dependent performance, with the Ostraciiform configuration achieving the highest straight-line velocity of 8.14 cm/s and the lowest cost of transport of 2.42 at 1.5 tail beats/s. These proof-of-concept results show that a mechanically simple stiffness-reconfigurable architecture can generate distinct BCF swimming modes and provide a versatile strategy for developing multimodal soft robotic fish.

</details>

Abstract: Visuomotor policies have demonstrated that imprecise low-cost robots can autonomously complete complex manipulation tasks. However, most inexpensive manipulators rely on compact and rigidly position-controlled servos for actuation, which complicate simulation, operation, and safety. This work introduces TinyARM-X, a framework for easily manufacturable, configurable, and torque-controllable low-cost (<350 USD) serial manipulators. By using a single hardware/software architecture, a <7 Degree of Freedom (DoF) TinyARM can be manually reconfigured to an operational >3 DoF robot within 10 min. Actuator modeling and dynamic compensation enable torque-controlled applications like mixed-reality impedance control teleoperation and simulator force feedback rendering. Experiments include mechanical benchmarking and real-world force-aware telemanipulation. The TinyARM-7 has a payload of 33.5 g, a max speed of 0.77 m/s, and a reach of 210 mm. Moreover, with dynamic compensation, the robot has a backdrive-breakaway force of 1.4 N and a backdrive-running force of 0.7 N. Additionally, Cartesian-Impedance Control (CIC) attains a translational stiffness of >15 N/m. Lastly, the TinyARM-X stack is open-sourced to disseminate accessible desktop-scale torque-controlled robotic manipulation to the community.

</details>

Abstract: Egocentric human recordings are far cheaper than dexterous robot demonstrations, promising stronger pretraining and direct skill learning without task-specific robot data. Yet humans and robots differ in morphology and kinematics, so human recordings lack the joint positions and actuator commands that supervise robot policies, providing only retargeted end-effector (EEF) actions. This raises two questions: how human data should supervise the policy, and how a dexterous robot should read out actions at deployment. We address both in SHAPE (Supervision from Human Action tokens with Parallel Executable streams), a vision-language-action (VLA) model whose backbone predicts discrete action tokens of EEF chunks as an auxiliary task, while a shared flow-matching expert outputs two executable streams, EEF and joint. On a bimanual robot with 22-DoF hands, a matched study shows that discrete supervision helps learn from human data, driving backbone gradients and shaping action-informative representations across domains. Under standard control pipelines at deployment, direct joint readout excels at fine-grained control with robot demonstrations, scoring 0.80 across six tasks against 0.63 for π0.5, whereas IK-based EEF readout excels at human-to-robot transfer. Pretrained with human data and discrete tokens, SHAPE learns new tasks from human demonstrations without task-specific robot data.

</details>

Abstract: Inspired by seminal work by E.M. Purcell, we discuss the problem of predicting and optimizing the performance of autonomous robots swimming in confinement. Our approach is based on the explicit numerical resolution of the fluid flow induced by the swimmer, and it is validated experimentally on a cm-size prototype composed of a cargo head and a rotating propulsive tail. Using hydrodynamic scaling, we predict the performance of replicas of our prototype scaled down to mm- and sub-mm-size to test the feasibility of autonomous micro-robots navigating in narrow vessels. The main result of our theoretical, computational, and experimental analysis is that, while hydrodynamics seems to place no fundamental obstruction to the vision of autonomous micro-robots navigating in narrow lumens inside the human body, it does provide useful and strict guidelines for rational design at different length scales.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents the development of an initial full-vehicle simulation model of the PixKit modular electric test platform in IPG CarMaker. The objective is to create a physics-based and extendable model structure that can serve as a low-level foundation for future digital twin applications. The reference vehicle is a skateboard chassis combining unconventional vehicle characteristics such as four independent wheel-hub motors and rear axle steering capable of high steering angles. Since complete measurement data of the vehicle is not yet available, the model parameterization is based on a combination of manufacturer documentation, derived values and engineering assumptions. The modeling approach focuses on the system-level integration of the main vehicle subsystems rather than on a fully validated representation. The resulting model was assessed through basic driving scenarios with different steering inputs and configurations. The simulations showed stable model behavior and physically plausible interaction between the subsystems. The all-wheel-drive configuration produced symmetric wheel responses in longitudinal driving, while counter-phase rear axle steering increased the yaw response and reduced the simulated turning circle diameter compared with front axle steering only. The circular driving scenario further indicated bounded roll and slip behavior. Overall, the implemented CarMaker model provides a traceable and functional simulation basis for the PixKit vehicle. It can support future model refinement, calibration with real vehicle data and development with the goal of creating a validated digital twin architecture.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents a hierarchical admittance-impedance controller that combines an inner Cartesian impedance controller, an outer 6D admittance supervisor, and workspace-aware mobile-base assistance for guided load transfer. The method is motivated by assistive tasks such as airport baggage handling, where a robot should support a payload while a human worker guides its motion through small physical forces. Following an interaction-role interpretation of admittance and impedance, the torque-controlled Franka Research 3 manipulator behaves as a Cartesian impedance at the robot-object interface, providing compliant load support around a commanded equilibrium pose. At the human-facing interaction level, the outer admittance supervisor converts measured human wrench input into smooth Cartesian reference motion. The workspace-aware assistance layer shares the admittance-generated translational motion between the manipulator and a velocity-controlled base when larger motion is required, while front-side load alignment keeps the carried object near the robot's frontal region for safer and more intuitive guidance. The system was implemented in ROS and validated through a suitcase transfer experiment, showing that the robot can support the load while allowing the human to guide the transfer with relatively little effort.

</details>


### [385] [HINT-WAM: Hindsight-Informed Training for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24789)
*Phuong Tuan Dat,Qi Li,Xinchao Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) have shown promising generalization and data efficiency in robot manipulation by coupling action generation with predictions of future visual dynamics. Translating these capabilities into reliable task execution, however, remains a challenge for existing post-training methods. Offline supervised fine-tuning provides dense supervision on expert trajectories, but its flow-matching action expert is trained only on noised demonstration actions and never on the intermediate states produced by its own sampler; task-specific adaptation can also compromise pretrained capabilities. On-policy reinforcement learning trains on the policy's own behavior, yet sparse task rewards make exploration and credit assignment difficult, while dense reward shaping still provides scalar evaluations rather than direct action targets. We introduce \textbf{HINT-WAM}, the first on-policy self-distillation framework that supervises the action expert on its own sampling trajectories using hindsight, the future video of the demonstration, as privileged information available to the WAM itself. For each demonstration observation, the student generates its action through its own denoising trajectory, and at the states along this trajectory the same model acts as a teacher conditioned additionally on this hindsight, providing a conditional action distribution as a dense training target, while the student sees only deployment-time inputs and needs no environmental rewards or simulator interaction. To transfer this supervision to a flow-matching action expert, HINT-WAM distills the teacher into the student with an energy score, a distribution-level objective that matches the teacher's action distribution from the student's own samples rather than regressing its velocity field. The project web is available in https://tuandattt.github.io/hintwam/.

</details>


### [386] [Planner-Controller Interaction for Ackermann Navigation: A Matched Simulation and Hardware Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24800)

Abstract: Global planners and path-tracking controllers are often evaluated independently, although their interaction determines how a vehicle follows a planned path. Whether simulation accurately predicts the performance of specific planner-controller combinations on Ackermann-steered hardware remains uncertain. This study evaluated three geometric controllers (Regulated Pure Pursuit, Stanley, and Vector Pursuit) paired with two global planners (Hybrid A* and NavFn) on a 1/10-scale Quanser QCar and in a matched Gazebo simulation. Both environments used shared custom planning and control algorithms, matched route geometry and goals, ground-truth pose references, and a common evaluation pipeline. Five runs per configuration and environment yielded 60 benchmark runs. Controller rankings for tracking accuracy and command smoothness transferred consistently between environments, with Spearman correlations of 0.77-0.94. However, planner-dependent effects showed substantially weaker agreement. In simulation, planner selection changed the root-mean-square (RMS) angular acceleration of the commanded velocities by no more than 0.030 rad/s^2, whereas Hybrid A* increased it by 0.141-0.843 rad/s^2 on hardware, depending on the controller. These differences coincided with planner-dependent achieved speeds that the simulation's idealised longitudinal response did not reproduce. Mean controller computation times remained below 0.5 ms on the Jetson TX2. The findings demonstrate that agreement in controller rankings does not guarantee accurate prediction of planner-dependent performance for specific controller pairings on hardware, highlighting the importance of physical validation.

</details>

Abstract: Learning shared action representations is central to cross-embodiment manipulation. However, identical joint values can produce different spatial configurations across robots, limiting representations learned from joint values alone. We introduce RoboMorpher, a morphology-conditioned action tokenizer that jointly reconstructs joint angles and link poses to associate control coordinates with their kinematic effects. MorphGen supplies scalable kinematic supervision by generating serial-arm descriptions from survey-derived geometric priors. We pretrain RoboMorpher on 40 million morphology-state pairs and integrate the frozen tokenizer into a vision-language-action policy for discrete prediction and continuous refinement. We also introduce EmbodiBench, a ten-task benchmark with 30 generated URDFs per task. With downstream URDFs excluded from tokenizer pretraining, the policy achieves 56.40% success on RoboTwin 2.0 and 23.27% on EmbodiBench, exceeding continuous joint-space control by 9.73 and 15.34 percentage points. Removing link-pose supervision reduces EmbodiBench success by 17.94 percentage points, supporting morphology-aware geometric supervision for joint-action representation learning.

</details>

Abstract: Learning robot manipulation is constrained by the availability of action-labeled robot data, for both imitation and world models. Humans can demonstrate skills in seconds, but their demonstrations lack the required robot actions. We introduce DiWALI, a framework that learns and improves diffusion policies within a world model trained on human and robot play. DiWALI infers latent actions from consecutive frames using an inverse dynamics model, enabling world model training without action labels, and grounds them in robot commands through a decoder trained separately on robot play. Skill policies are trained over latent actions from action-free human demonstrations and improved with reinforcement learning in imagination. On the CALVIN benchmark, DiWALI policies trained solely from human demonstrations substantially outperform baselines trained with robot demonstrations. Incorporating human play into the world model training also yields stronger policies after fine-tuning than using the same amount of robot play alone. Experiments on a physical robot show that skills learned primarily from human demonstrations are further improved in imagination with a world model trained on human and robot play. Together, these results demonstrate that human experience can support both manipulation skill acquisition and subsequent policy improvement, reducing reliance on action-labeled robot data. Videos, dataset and code are available at https://diwali.cs.uni-freiburg.de.

</details>

Abstract: Large-scale, multi-agent self-play trains robust driving policies effectively in simulation, but controlling these models to generate specific, safety-critical edge cases remains a key challenge. Existing goal-conditioned self-play frameworks rely primarily on static map-location goals and fail to reliably produce complex agent interactions such as aggressive tailgating or adversarial cut-offs. These agent interactions are necessary for rigorous autonomous vehicle (AV) testing. To address this, we present an extended multi-agent self-play framework that introduces agent-relative dynamic goals. By conditioning policies on this extended goal representation, our framework enables targeted, controllable scenario synthesis. We demonstrate that our approach successfully generates interactive driving behaviors that static-goal baselines cannot simulate, providing a flexible tool for long-tail scenario generation in AV testing.

</details>

Abstract: Robotic haircutting requires accurate motion execution and reliable communication to safely reproduce the operator's commands. Internet-based teleoperation is a key enabling technology to remotely perform precise manipulation tasks such as haircutting. However, communication uncertainties, including variable transmission delays and packet jitter, degrade synchronization between the master and slave systems and affect tracking performance. This paper presents a unilateral Internet teleoperation framework for robotic haircutting that takes into account experimentally measured communication uncertainties. Three delay-compensation techniques, namely Zero-Order Hold (ZOH), First-Order Hold (FOH), and Low-Pass Filtering (LPF), are evaluated together with a classical proportional-derivative (PD) controller and a passivity-based synchronization controller. Unlike conventional studies based on synthetic delay models, the proposed framework is validated using communication data acquired from a real cloud-based teleoperation platform, where both packet delay and effective delay are characterized. The results demonstrate that combining delay-compensation techniques with passivity-based synchronization improves the robustness of unilateral Internet teleoperation under realistic communication uncertainties, providing a reliable control framework for robotic haircutting applications.

</details>

Abstract: Predicting the consequences of an agent's actions requires modeling both the body's motion and the environment's response. Most video world models attempt to learn both from numerical actions, even though geometry and kinematics already specify the intended motion. We introduce Doppel, an embodiment-agnostic video world model conditioned on camera-aligned renderings of future body configurations and numerical action trajectories. By explicitly providing the intended motion, Doppel can focus on predicting object motion and interaction-driven changes in the environment. Rendered motion also provides a common visual interface across different embodiments and action spaces. To train on robot datasets without camera extrinsics, we recover camera poses and align rendered trajectories with recorded observations. We jointly train a single shared video backbone and conditioning adapter on eight datasets spanning eight robot and human embodiments, from single- and bimanual robot arms to a dexterous humanoid and human hands and bodies, using about 600 hours of video without embodiment labels. Across three robot-arm datasets, Doppel improves PSNR over action-only baselines by 1.00 dB on held-out trajectories and 1.20 dB under out-of-distribution evaluation, while predicting more consistent object motion. Training on all eight embodiments (Doppel-8) improves robot predictions over the three-robot model (Doppel-3) on four of six evaluation sets. Project page: https://doppelwm.github.io/.

</details>


### [392] [Grana: Compositional Reward Modeling for Long-Horizon Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24926)
*Zi-ang Cao,Kaiyuan Zheng,Yinzhen Xu,Haonan Duan,Jiasheng Gu,Fengyuan Hu,Nan Zhu,Kun Lei,Kevin Lin,Ruijie Zheng,Shenyuan Gao,Fangqi Zhu,Zhe Zhang,Radha Poovendran,Linxi Fan,Guanzhi Wang,Yuke Zhu*

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Robot reward modeling faces two challenges: generalizing across tasks and accurately tracking progress in long-horizon manipulation. General-purpose reward models demonstrate broad task transfer, but transfer from short-task pretraining to long-horizon tasks remains underexplored. Long-horizon progress models often rely on task-specific subtask classifiers or fixed action vocabularies, limiting the reuse of learned progress knowledge when adapting to new tasks. To address these challenges, we introduce Grana, a vision-language robot reward model that learns to identify unfinished subtasks and estimate their continuous progress using open-vocabulary subtask descriptions. We train a language-conditioned model to estimate progress in short-horizon tasks and individual subtasks of long-horizon tasks. For long-horizon tasks, we combine the selected subtask's predicted progress with credit for preceding completed subtasks to estimate whole-task progress. General progress prediction requires training the model across diverse tasks with progress supervision that accounts for imperfect execution. Elapsed-time proxies can misrepresent progress when relative subtask durations vary, or failures reverse local progress. We therefore introduce an annotation protocol that converts sparse subtask boundaries and action-quality annotations into dense progress targets, without requiring annotators to assign numerical progress scores. Pretraining with this supervision on 200 diverse, mostly short-horizon manipulation tasks enables zero-shot reward prediction on unseen tasks and improves progress prediction after adaptation to multi-minute, long-horizon tasks. Experiments demonstrate (i) generalizable reward prediction, including adaptation to five long-horizon tasks, (ii) benefits from pretraining and composing subtask progress estimates, and (iii) improved downstream policy performance.

</details>

Abstract: World-action models jointly predict future observations and actions, yet reconstruction-oriented visual representations do not explicitly emphasize the spatial relationships and object-state changes needed for manipulation. We introduce Phoenix, which learns representations of scene structure for world-action modeling. Specifically, Phoenix Encoder enriches DINOv3's general visual semantics through dual-teacher distillation of geometric and spatiotemporal semantic knowledge. Next, CS-VAE causally aggregates and compresses Phoenix Encoder features into generative latents while preserving scene information and spatial layout. Finally, Phoenix jointly predicts these latents, complementary appearance latents, and robot actions, linking scene evolution with action generation. To study pretraining data efficiency, we curate approximately 39,000 hours of egocentric and robot manipulation data for representation learning and use the robot demonstration subset for WAM pretraining. Following benchmark-specific post-training, Phoenix achieves state-of-the-art performance across simulation and real-robot benchmarks spanning single-arm, bimanual, and dexterous manipulation. It demonstrates strong robustness to perturbations, achieving success rates of 94.8% on LIBERO-Plus and 72.4% on RoboTwin 2.0 C2R, alongside strong cross-embodiment transfer and real-world manipulation performance on Bench2Dex and RoboDojo-RealWorld, respectively. Pretraining-scale analysis shows larger performance gains from data scaling than a video-VAE-only baseline, indicating stronger scaling benefits and more effective data utilization. Moreover, Phoenix maintains high success rates when adapted with limited downstream demonstrations, demonstrating strong few-shot adaptation. Our code, pretraining data, and models are available on the project page to help the community reproduce our results.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce $Z$-sight, a unified robot foundation model that couples vision-language-action (VLA) and generative latent world modeling. Video-based world action models (WAMs) show the value of joint future-and-action generation for control, yet inherit the heavy cost of video generators. Recent latent-space policies avoid pixel generation, yet typically align intermediate features or rely on frozen target encoders, decoupling future prediction from the VLM's native feature space. $Z$-sight instead predicts the future directly in the visual token space in which its VLM perceives the present. Coupling a foresight expert and a continuous action expert with the VLM in a mixture-of-transformers, $Z$-sight jointly denoises future spatial tokens and actions at inference without pixel reconstruction or external encoders. Trained on human video and robot trajectories, $Z$-sight achieves 81.9% success on SIMPLE (+9.6 points over the strongest baseline) and 73.3% across six Unitree G1 tasks (+20.0 points) at 110 ms per chunk, while remaining competitive on tabletop benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: This paper proposes a decentralized event-triggered topology-switching protocol for multi-agent systems under estimation uncertainty. We consider an intermittent communication setting in which each agent switches between connected and disconnected modes and makes communication decisions using locally computable certificates, while the closed-loop state is required to remain safe. The proposed protocol follows a safety-driven triggering principle in which agents reconnect before their certificates reach the safety boundary and disconnect only after recovering to a stricter bound below the safety limit. Our design enables intermittent connectivity while maintaining the estimation error within a certified safety bound and excluding Zeno behavior. We evaluate the proposed protocol on a quadruple-tank example, showing that it keeps all tank deviations within the safety bound while allowing agents to remain disconnected for most of the run.

</details>

Abstract: Although cascading failures are relatively rare events in power systems, they pose significant risks and can lead to severe economic and societal consequences within a short period. Therefore, developing effective mitigation strategies to prevent cascade propagation and to minimize their impacts is essential. This paper presents a deep reinforcement learning (DRL)-based framework for adaptive transmission line switching to mitigate cascading failures. Given the important role of network topology and component interactions in cascading failure propagation in power systems, graph-based DRL approaches, including Graph-DQN, Graph-A2C, and Graph-PPO frameworks, are developed to capture and utilize the topological information of power systems. The proposed frameworks are evaluated on the IEEE 39, 57, and 118 bus systems and benchmarked against the unmitigated case, random and heuristic-based switching strategies as baselines, as well as their corresponding graph-agnostic DRL counterparts. The results demonstrate the importance of incorporating graph information into these mitigation frameworks, leading to reduced cascade size, load loss, and network clustering.

</details>

Abstract: Rapid power variations from artificial intelligence (AI) workloads pose frequency-security challenges for islanded data-center microgrids. This paper proposes a generator step- response-based framework to rapidly screen candidate load pro- files and derive frequency-secure operating limits for IT workload scheduling. Identified generator responses are superimposed to assess the cumulative frequency effects of successive load changes without repeated dynamic simulations. The framework identifies potentially unsafe intervals and determines allowable load magni- tudes, ramp rates, and minimum event spacing, while accounting for prediction uncertainty and governor limits. Validation on a 2.5 MVA islanded microgrid with eight generator configurations and 14 synthetic AI workload profiles shows that 94.75% of 40,208 assessment windows receive decisive screening labels, with no frequency-violating windows classified as secure.

</details>

Abstract: We implement and compare two approaches to improving flight control system (FCS) performance using reinforcement meta-learning (meta-RL). Both approaches are evaluated in a terminal phase hypersonic intercept engagement requiring hit-to-kill accuracy against a highly agile maneuvering target. In the first approach (FCS-RL), meta-RL is used to optimize a full FCS that maps acceleration tracking error and other measurements directly to commanded channel deflection rates of change, combining pitch, yaw, and roll control into a single policy. In contrast, the second approach (FCS-3L-RL) starts with a standard FCS (FCS-3L) implementing a three-loop autopilot for pitch and yaw control, but adapts the scheduled autopilot gains in real time using a policy optimized with meta-RL. Both approaches are compared to the FCS-3L in a 6-DOF simulator that captures most factors relevant to guidance and flight control. Simulations show that under ideal conditions the three systems have comparable performance. However, under conditions of aerodynamic uncertainty, radome refraction, or target evasive maneuvers, the FCS-RL significantly outperforms the FCS-3L-RL with improved intercept probability and less control effort. In turn, the FCS-3L-RL outperforms the FCS-3L by a similar margin. Importantly, we demonstrate that both FCS-RL and FCS-3L-RL generalize well to novel engagement scenarios, flight conditions, and target maneuvers that are not experienced during the optimization process.

</details>

Abstract: Sampling-based model predictive control (MPC) enables real-time control of complex nonlinear systems but often incurs substantial computational costs due to repeated trajectory simulations. In this paper, we bridge policy optimization in reinforcement learning and sampling-based MPC to improve computational efficiency through sample reuse. Starting from a KL-regularized distributional formulation of trajectory optimization, we derive an exact performance-difference identity based on a signed exponential advantage function. This identity motivates two algorithms, PPO-MPPI and SPO-MPPI, which adapt proximal policy optimization techniques to sampling-based MPC, enabling multiple optimization updates using previously evaluated trajectories without additional forward simulations. We establish population-level monotonic improvement guarantees for both methods and prove convergence to first-order stationarity for PPO-MPPI under fixed-covariance Gaussian sampling distributions. Experiments on quadruped velocity tracking and quadrotor path tracking demonstrate that the proposed methods achieve competitive control performance at substantially lower computational cost than multi-stage sampling approaches. Our results establish a principled connection between policy optimization and sampling-based MPC and highlight the potential of trajectory reuse for sample-efficient predictive control.

</details>


### [400] [ADSPICE: A Method of Automatic Differentiation for Gradient-Based Lumped Circuit Optimization with Native SPICE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23571)

Abstract: This work presents a novel computer-aided design (CAD) tool for gradient-based circuit optimization using SPICE and automatic differentiation, which we term ADSPICE. Automatic differentiation (AD) is a technique for evaluating a derivative by breaking it into its elementary differentiation operations and repeatedly applying the chain rule, in contrast with a finite-difference-based derivative computed from a small perturbation. Native SPICE (realized with ngspice) is equipped with automatic differentiation toward gradient-based optimization, particularly adjoint-mode optimization. This is implemented with custom C code and shared library control, allowing easy integration with existing SPICE infrastructure. We show a computational performance analysis, demonstrating faster optimization vs. an existing finite-difference method, SPICE OPUS, the closest competitor in SPICE optimization tools. The result is a speedup factor of 383x for the largest circuit compared within the state-of-the-art, SPICE OPUS. For circuit sizes above approximately 3,500 nodes, the time taken by ADSPICE optimization decreases with increasing problem size, as a proportion of total computational time. The speed bottleneck is shown to be SPICE solves rather than ADSPICE enhancements. Voltage gradients needed for forward-mode AD are benchmarked against Xyce, another state-of-the-art simulator, using an integrated CMOS operational amplifier test circuit. The results match with a maximum error of 0.203%, further validating the accuracy of our method. ADSPICE is shown to enable SPICE, and SPICE-based simulators, with automatic differentiation for gradient optimization, without requiring creation of a new circuit simulator.

</details>

Abstract: The rapid deployment of artificial intelligence data centers (AIDCs) introduces gigawatt-scale, highly nonlinear, and fast-varying electronic loads that challenge grid voltage regulation, frequency stability, and disturbance ride-through. Capturing their transient behavior during grid faults and rapid computational workload swings requires high-fidelity electromagnetic transient (EMT) simulations rather than root mean square models. However, limited methodologies exist to audit the component-level adequacy of EMT AIDC models. This paper proposes a software-agnostic, 41-point fidelity framework spanning 11 categories that synthesizes 37 criteria from an existing modeling guideline with four novel requirements: frequency ride-through compliance, disaggregated fan and chiller cooling loads, temperature-dependent thermal response, and DC information technology (IT) load representations. Applying this framework to the four publicly available AIDC models in PSCAD/EMTDC reveals varying degrees of completeness: while the models capture IT load transitions, uninterruptible power supplies, and transfer switches for bulk stability studies, critical gaps remain in electro-thermal cooling coupling, internal protective relaying, and site-level controls. Addressing these gaps will enable comprehensive protection coordination and ride-through compliance studies.

</details>

Abstract: The passivity of synchronous machines enables power system stability to be established from local conditions. A recent state-feedback design enforces robust passivity using battery actuation at the machine terminal bus. This paper extends that design to a model that includes the field winding and the dynamics of a steam turbine and its governor. The extended model provides two additional actuator signals: the exciter input and the governor input. The nonlinear terms in the model depend on speed and field current deviations, and are represented as bounded uncertainties. Robust positive real conditions are derived in terms of a single algebraic Riccati equation. From its solution, a state-feedback controller is designed in closed form. Numerical results show that, unlike the design with battery actuation alone, the proposed controller leads to passivity at all loading levels when the damping is low.

</details>

Simulations on the RTS-GMLC test system show that active-power flexibility mitigates local price spikes and relieves congestion, whereas reactive support from the BESS inverter or a STATCOM is needed to keep the POI voltage away from sagging around its 0.95~pu limit. Sensitivity analyses show that cost-driven charging of large batteries can induce new morning congestion and that storage largely substitutes for wider workload deferral.

Abstract: Multi-tenant data center (DC) facilities rent computing capacity on demand, placing controllable load within reach of anyone willing to pay for it. This paper considers a stealthy adversary who rents workstations and runs deliberately unoptimized large language model (LLM) training, synchronizing their consumption into large periodic oscillations while each workload remains individually indistinguishable from benign training. The periodicity of such workloads is characterized experimentally and used to model facility consumption, showing that oscillations emerge from the interaction of concurrent workloads, whether aligned by an adversary or arising naturally. A combined small- and large-signal assessment, under a hardware-grounded threat model whose feasibility is supported by experiments on a supercomputer, locates the oscillatory weak points of the network. MW-scale oscillations are shown to saturate the generators' control limiters, amplifying their effect, while Floquet analysis reveals a lightly damped mode emerging near the forcing frequency. To counter both origins, signal-energy-based detection and mitigation are proposed, the latter re-timing the workloads to minimize the facility's oscillatory energy. The approach is validated through power hardware-in-the-loop (PHIL) experiments and real-time simulations on a surrogate model of the Saudi Arabian transmission network.

Abstract: Solar radiation pressure (SRP) significantly influences satellite dynamics in high-altitude orbits, depending not only on the satellite's trajectory and attitude but also on its surface optical characteristics. Metallic surfaces, such as solar cells with periodic metal bars, exhibit anisotropic reflection that produces an anisotropic SRP. The difficulty in dealing with anisotropic SRP arises because anisotropic SRP does not have a general analytical solution, and accurate computation therefore requires numerical integration. An analytical formulation of anisotropic SRP is useful for stability analysis and controller design for satellites that exploit SRP. To this end, this paper derives an analytical approximation method to calculate the anisotropic SRP modeled with the Ashikhmin-Shirley model. The anisotropy is approximated using multiple spherical Gaussians. Because the product of spherical Gaussians is also a spherical Gaussian, anisotropic reflection can be expressed in an analytically tractable form. The approximation accuracy of the anisotropic SRP is numerically verified for two cases: weak anisotropy and strong anisotropy.

</details>

Abstract: Separating hyperplanes are widely used in Control Barrier Function (CBF)-based collision avoidance, yet their orientations strongly govern control intervention-the deviation of the applied control from the robot's nominal command. Existing methods typically select hyperplane orientations from geometry alone. Jointly optimizing orientations and control yields a continuous, non-convex problem in which local nonlinear solvers suffer from initialization sensitivity, local minima, and unpredictable latency, while discretized orientation grids scale exponentially with obstacle count. For planar navigation of a polygonal robot among polygonal obstacles, we present a solver-free, control-aware separating hyperplane selection method that directly computes a global optimum of this joint problem at each step whenever it is feasible. Exploiting the geometry of Minkowski difference polygons, we reduce this continuous non-convex optimization to evaluating a polynomial-size set of candidate control interventions via closed-form algebraic checks, selecting the feasible candidate of minimum norm. Across 250 simulated navigation scenarios, our method reduces cumulative intervention cost by 3.6% at the median and 27.3% at the 90th percentile compared to a local nonlinear solver, and completion time by 0.6% and 5.6%, respectively. In scalability benchmarks, it achieves a 6.1x per-step speedup at 16 simultaneous obstacles.

</details>

Abstract: Low-inertia power systems increasingly rely on Fast Frequency Response (FFR) to keep the frequency nadir above under-frequency load-shedding thresholds without oversized Frequency Containment Reserve or synchronous inertia. This paper proposes two complementary methodologies that interface with an existing day-ahead FFR capacity market. On the Transmission System Operator (TSO) side, a dynamic procurement rule maps forecasted kinetic energy and contingency magnitude onto an offline neighborhood-need surface, yielding interval-wise FFR capacity requests rather than constant seasonal or annual volumes. On the aggregator side, a risk-aware, TSO-certifiable bidding strategy quantifies reliable FFR capacity from fleets of electric vehicles (EVs) and heat pumps (HPs) by offering lower conditional quantiles of day-ahead headroom at a prescribed delivery reliability. The resulting requests and offers are cleared through a pay-as-bid capacity auction. A Cyprus 2025 case study shows that dynamic neighborhood-based procurement yields an annual clearing cost of about EUR 476,000, which is 30%-33% lower than the seasonal and annual static rules and also below the UFLS VoLL of EUR 637,500 estimated from the fifteen real 2025 events with fmin <= 49 Hz. EV and HP offers meet their target delivery reliabilities, and RMS validation finds 46 of 50 operating points above 49 Hz at both full and 85% FFR delivery.

</details>

Abstract: The emerging ambient Internet of Things (A-IoT) envisions connectivity among large populations of low-cost, energy-constrained devices. Supporting such connectivity requires efficient coordination of device access, particularly in reader-triggered operation, where multiple devices may respond to paging over limited shared radio resources. Indeed, simultaneous responses can lead to severe contention, delayed transmissions, and lost reports. This article explores how compact extensions to the current 3GPP Release-19 signaling framework could enable more selective and adaptive device access. The proposed approach allows readers to broadcast compact access guidance, which devices interpret according to their local conditions, while optional feedback supports continuous adaptation. Through a device-reporting case study, we demonstrate how such coordination can improve report delivery and timeliness under congested conditions, while highlighting the trade-offs across different network configurations. We also examine practical signaling considerations and discuss opportunities for more intelligent, adaptive, and resilient protocols.

Abstract: Model Predictive Path Integral control (MPPI) is a sampling-based control method that handles nonlinear dynamics and non-convex costs without gradient information. Since MPPI samples the entire input sequence, longer prediction horizons lead to a higher-dimensional sampling space, potentially degrading control performance. This paper proposes Laguerre-MPPI, which parameterizes the control input sequence using discrete-time Laguerre basis functions and samples the basis coefficients. The resulting search dimension is independent of the prediction horizon. Hence, a longer prediction horizon does not require more samples to maintain the sampling density. For linear systems with quadratic costs, we derive a closed-loop stability condition in the infinite-sample limit. Under finite sampling, we provide a closed-form expression for the limit of the ratio of the effective sample size to the number of samples. We also show that the upper bound of this limit for Laguerre-MPPI is no lower than that for conventional MPPI. Numerical experiments demonstrate smoother inputs in obstacle avoidance and maintained performance over long horizons. Moreover, they verify the lower bound on the number of samples given by the finite-sample analysis.

</details>

Abstract: Cyber-physical systems (CPS) increasingly contain data-driven controllers, which raise safety concerns given their black-box nature. A common mitigation is to enforce system safety via a shield around the controller, which restricts the inputs based on assumptions about the physical system. However, these assumptions are usually fixed and may become invalid as the system evolves (during development or due to component degradation), possibly leading to unsafe behavior. We address this limitation in a model-driven approach that treats both the data-driven controller and safety shield as first-class models that are integrated into a virtual single underlying model (V-SUM) alongside system models. We describe system evolution as changes in the associated models, such that consistency checking yields a continuous assessment of safety guarantees. In case of inconsistencies, consistency-preservation rules automatically update the models and restore a valid safety shield. We evaluate our approach on a reach-avoid task for a unicycle system. The results show that consistency-driven evolution effectively maintains safety guarantees under system changes and provides a principled basis for evaluating and restoring performance.

</details>

Abstract: Transmission network security can substantially alter where large-scale data centers can connect and how much workload flexibility can unlock. This paper develops a hosting-capacity framework that couples computing demand, batch workload scheduling, and N-1 security constraints. A modified IEEE 118-bus case study reveals that two data center sites with identical hosting capacities under normal operation differ by 874.5 MW under the contingencies operation when workload flexibility is enabled. Six-hour batch deferral increases N-1 hosting capacity by up to 24.5\% relative to immediate execution. These gains arise from reducing demand at critical hours while accommodating deferred tasks within subsequent transmission limits and deadlines. At bus 92, a four-hour window captures 90.9\% of the six-hour capacity gain. The findings establish workload flexibility as a location-dependent connection resource whose capacity value requires joint assessment of contingency exposure and deadline constrainted workload recovery, beyond the power reduction available at a single critical hour.

Abstract: Grid connection constraints increasingly limit the expansion of AI data centers, yet the additional hosting capacity enabled by computational workload flexibility remains insufficiently quantified. This paper develops a two-stage electricity--computation co-optimization framework to quantify the individual and coordinated hosting capacity values of spatial and temporal workload flexibility. Data center capacities are determined endogenously, with computational task arrivals scaling with admitted capacity. Spatial routing of eligible interactive requests and temporal deferral of batch workloads are linked to server operation and facility power demand, subject to workload completion requirements and transmission constraints. The first stage maximizes aggregate hosting capacity; the second fixes the resulting site capacities and optimizes annual workload scheduling and generation dispatch to minimize generation and carbon costs. An 8760-hour case study with three data centers on a modified IEEE 118-bus system shows that temporal and spatial flexibility individually increase hosting capacity by 21.26\% and 5.37\%, respectively. Their coordinated use raises hosting capacity from 4635.1~MW to 6231.5~MW, a 34.44\% increase without network reinforcement. The coordinated gain exceeds the sum of the individual gains by 361.9~MW, demonstrating complementarity between spatial migration and temporal deferral. By redistributing computational demand across locations and operating hours, these mechanisms enable fuller utilization of existing transmission capacity. The findings establish workload flexibility as a quantifiable planning resource for accommodating additional data center capacity within existing transmission networks.

</details>

Abstract: While conventional behavioral data-driven control represents linear time-invariant (LTI) systems directly from data, extending these data representations to nonlinear systems remains an open challenge. We address this gap for general polynomial systems, characterizing their finite-length trajectories as a real algebraic variety defined by implicit polynomial relations rather than explicit equations. First, we provide conditions on measured data under which these data can be used to represent the system. Building on this representation, we formulate data-driven simulation and predictive control problems that are equivalent to their model-based counterparts, and solve the resulting polynomial systems globally using homotopy continuation. Numerical case studies confirm the effectiveness of this approach by successfully simulating a stiff Van der Pol oscillator in situations where explicit models fail. Furthermore, these data-driven simulation and control methods are illustrated on a Duffing oscillator, demonstrating that our method outperforms Koopman-based approaches in predicting long trajectories.

</details>

Abstract: Formal verification and correct-by-design policy synthesis for stochastic dynamical systems face significant scalability challenges due to the curse of dimensionality. These challenges are exacerbated when the system dynamics are unknown and abstractions must be constructed from data. This paper introduces a data-driven framework for constructing factored interval Markov decision process (fIMDP) abstractions from noisy transition observations. Assuming conditional independence of successor coordinates and known coordinate-wise Lipschitz bounds on the dynamics, the framework estimates marginal transition probabilities rather than joint probabilities, resulting in an exponential reduction in the number of probability entries to be estimated and stored. We establish finite-sample confidence guarantees that the abstraction captures the concrete transition probabilities uniformly over each abstract region, enabling certified lower bounds on satisfaction probabilities for $ω$-regular specifications. Experiments on benchmark systems, including pendulum swing-up, unicycle control, and a four-dimensional planar robot model, demonstrate improvements in certified satisfaction probability lower bounds of up to 25 percentage points compared with a state-of-the-art flat IMDP abstraction method. Our framework required up to 23x less memory and was consistently faster in constructing the abstraction.

</details>

Abstract: Lateral velocity is safety-critical for vehicle-dynamics control but cannot be measured with production sensors. Model-based estimators such as Kalman filters are established, whereas data-driven estimators are more accurate within the training regime but suspected to lose accuracy beyond it. Their transfer to a structurally different vehicle has rarely been tested. We record real-world test drives of three series-production vehicles, train a neural-network estimator on production CAN signals of a compact car, and evaluate it zero-shot on an SUV and a van against a GNSS-aided inertial reference. On the unseen vehicles, the network's error exceeds that on the training vehicle by an order of magnitude, a gap not quantified on production vehicles before. A systematic comparison of input sets from ten production CAN signals identifies a five-signal set that transfers most robustly. To narrow this gap, we introduce three physically motivated augmentations of training data. Two improve generalization, namely exploiting the vehicle's left-right symmetry and emulating accelerometer bias. Combined, they reduce the error on the validation maneuvers of the SUV and van by 13% and 15%. Contrary to common expectation, on the SUV the data-driven estimator trained with both augmentations matches a model-based estimator tuned on the same training data.

</details>

Abstract: Future satellite systems are shifting toward regenerative payloads that interconnect many modem banks into a reconfigurable on-board network. However, conventional constellation-level routing overlooks intra-payload congestion and load imbalance, which can limit the traffic each satellite can accommodate. This paper proposes a hierarchical software-defined networking (SDN)framework for traffic engineering (TE) across both the constellation and payload levels. The system is represented by a two-level graph, where each satellite node in the constellation graph is expanded into an internal network of interconnected modem banks. The proposed hierarchical payload-aware allocation (HPAA) algorithm coordinates the two levels through the exchange of assigned traffic demand and advertised capacity budgets. Simulation results show that, under heavy load, HPAA delivers about 9 times the throughput of shortest-path routing, with 17% lower end-to-end delay and no packet loss. These findings highlight that hierarchical control accounting for the on-board network constraints of regenerative payloads is a key lever to improve the performance of satellite constellations.

Abstract: Magnetic Navigation (MagNav) is a promising alternative to traditional systems like Global Navigation Satellite Systems (GNSS), offering resistance to jamming or spoofing attacks to which the latter are vulnerable. MagNav matches scalar magnetic measurements over time against a known map of Earth's magnetic anomaly in order to position a vehicle. MagNav is therefore sensitive to the quality of these maps, especially to errors in the survey data that the maps are derived from. In order to be a reliable and mature navigation technology, MagNav must satisfy integrity requirements for mitigation of such faults. To this end, a solution-separation monitoring algorithm is developed that performs a role equivalent to Advanced Receiver Autonomous Integrity Monitoring (ARAIM) in GNSS, which detects faults as well as generates worst-case bounds (protection levels) on estimation error for faults too small to be detected. It is shown how these integrity methods can be developed in a \emph{windowed-in-time} sense, where subsets of measurements over a time window are compared with each other in order to detect outliers that can potentially result from errors in the map. The applicability of the approach is demonstrated using results obtained using synthetic aircraft data with injected faults.

</details>

Abstract: Reliable uncertainty quantification (UQ) is essential for evaluating retinal AI systems beyond classification accuracy, yet it remains unclear whether existing UQ strategies generalize across diseases, model architectures, and reliability criteria. We introduce a large-scale, multi-disease benchmark comprising over $242,000$ fundus photographs from $32$ datasets covering diabetic retinopathy, age-related macular degeneration, and glaucoma. We evaluate six representative UQ strategies across three Vision Transformer configurations, assessing discrimination, probabilistic calibration, selective prediction, and conformal prediction-set coverage. Our findings reveal no universally superior model or UQ strategy: methods that improve reliability in one disease or architecture may underperform in another, while improvements in discrimination, calibration, and selective prediction do not necessarily align. Notably, computationally intensive methods do not consistently outperform simple confidence scores, and domain-specific foundation models and calibration-oriented loss functions do not guarantee greater reliability. In contrast, conformal prediction provides a complementary framework for achieving near-nominal marginal prediction-set coverage across diverse settings, subject to its statistical assumptions. These findings challenge universal UQ recommendations and highlight the need for disease-specific, model-specific, and multidimensional reliability evaluation before clinical deployment. We release standardized dataset splits, trained checkpoints, evaluation code, and an interactive research demonstration to support reproducible benchmarking.

</details>

Abstract: Hospitals record thousands of hours of surgical video annually, yet these archives remain difficult to search by content. Surgeons cannot readily retrieve similar operations, complications, or workflow events. Existing surgical computer vision primarily produces labels rather than retrieving relevant videos. We introduce SurgCBVR, the first benchmark for content-based video-to-video retrieval in surgery. It integrates eight public datasets across four domains-ophthalmic, laparoscopic, robotic-assisted, and colonoscopy-covering six procedure types, 679 cases, and 76,489 clips (106 hours). SurgCBVR comprises two tiers: Scene, retrieving the same operative state across cases and sites, and Workflow, retrieving and localizing phase transitions and deviations. Scene includes 74,765 clips spanning 40 classes, three temporal granularities, and three playback speeds; Workflow contains 1,724 transition clips. Fixed clip lengths and frame counts, together with phase-classifier oracle and duration-only baselines, help assess label and timing shortcuts. We evaluate six generic and surgical foundation models, two video retrieval methods, and contrastive heads trained on SurgCBVR, using within-dataset, cross-dataset, and pooled-gallery retrieval restricted to the same procedure type. Frozen representations barely outperform a timing-only baseline, transition retrieval approaches chance, and trained heads lose up to half their accuracy across datasets. These findings expose substantial limitations in current video representations and establish SurgCBVR as a benchmark for advancing surgery-aware retrieval.

</details>

Abstract: Purpose: To develop and validate SWIMSS (SWInUNETR for Multiple Sclerosis white matter lesion Segmentation), a SwinUNETR-based deep learning (DL) model for reliable automated white matter (WM) lesion segmentation, in a large multicentric cohort of people with multiple sclerosis (pwMS). Methods: A real-world longitudinal dataset of 1,385 pwMS from seven Swiss clinical centers was used to train, validate, and test SWIMSS and two state-of-the-art DL architectures: UNETR and MSSegDiff. Inference results obtained with LST-AI were also compared. Preprocessed magnetization-prepared rapid acquisition gradient echo and fluid-attenuated inversion recovery MRI sequences, acquired with various MR systems and field strengths, served as model inputs. Model performance was evaluated using the Dice similarity coefficient (DSC) with 95% confidence intervals (CI). Results: SWIMSS achieved the highest performance, with a DSC of 81.37 (95% CI: 80.63-82.11), outperforming UNETR (79.06; 95% CI: 78.33-79.79), MSSegDiff (75.70; 95% CI: 74.97-76.43), and LST-AI (63.19; 95% CI: 62.08-64.30). Normalized DSC followed a similar trend, with SWIMSS reaching 84.88. Lesion detection F1 achieved 81.14. Statistical analyses confirmed its superior performance (p < 0.001). Conclusion: SWIMSS achieved state-of-the-art performance for WM lesion segmentation. By making this model publicly available, we enable broader access to high-quality segmentation tools, allowing smaller centers, often lacking large annotated datasets or computational resources, to benefit from a model trained on a diverse, large-scale cohort. This promotes more equitable access to advanced imaging analysis and supports reproducibility and standardization in MS research.

</details>


### [424] [Streaming Generative Video Compression with Embedding Alignment and In-Context Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23352)
*Zhitao Wang,Hengyu Man,Han Liu,Wenrui Li,Debin Zhao,Xiaopeng Fan*

Abstract: Video compression at ultra-low bitrates presents substantial challenges in preserving perceptual quality. Although pretrained video diffusion transformers (DiTs) provide powerful generative priors, existing generative video compression (GVC) methods built on them typically rely on computationally expensive VAE decoders to reconstruct video frames. Moreover, jointly refining multiple frames to exploit the inherent spatiotemporal modeling capabilities of video DiTs introduces additional buffering delay. To address these challenges, we propose SGVC, a streaming generative video compression framework that adapts a pretrained latent video DiT into a causal pixel-space refiner. Specifically, SGVC projects decoded features from a contextual pixel codec directly into the DiT input embedding space. To bridge the representation gap between codec-derived embeddings and pretrained DiT embeddings, we introduce Embedding Alignment, which aligns the projected embeddings with input embeddings extracted from the original frames during training. The refined DiT hidden states are then mapped directly to the pixel domain through a lightweight reconstruction path, bypassing the pretrained VAE decoder. To further exploit the spatiotemporal priors of the video DiT while maintaining causal decoding, we introduce In-Context Propagation, which reuses layer-wise K/V states across frames to exploit historical generative context, improving temporal consistency without requiring clip-wise buffering. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SGVC achieves competitive rate-perception performance, with average BD-rate savings of 71.97% over S$^2$VC in terms of DISTS. Project Page: https://chigland.github.io/SGVC/

</details>


### [425] [LIC-Audit: How Comparable Are Reported Gains in Learned Image Compression?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23419)
*Haisheng Fu,Jie Liang,Runyu Yang,Feng Ding,Xin Dong,Zixiao Jin,Jingning Han*

Main category: eess.IV

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Learned image compression (LIC) papers often report BD-rate improvements of only a few percent. They are measured against VTM reference software whose version keeps changing: the claims we audit name 7 versions, 5 in 2025 papers alone. But how comparable are these numbers across papers, and what evidence should future papers give for such small gains? We introduce LIC-Audit, an analysis of 353 released rate-distortion curves from 36 repositories, and a controlled re-evaluation of eight public codec releases with 28,193 encode-decode records. We identify three problems. First, different VTM reference runs change BD-rates by up to 3.56 percentage points, and on CLIC Pro published numbers order 10 of 55 method pairs differently from a common computation; comparing codecs directly over a fixed quality range removes this dependence. Second, releases use different and mostly undocumented conventions for counting bits, computing PSNR, and padding images. Under consistent measurement, the GLIC method's 1.00% lead over the HPCM method on Tecnick becomes a 1.43% deficit, changing only the padding reverses the comparison of HPCM and CMIC, and most gaps narrow when MS-SSIM replaces PSNR. Third, average curves hide how much results vary from image to image, so whether a small gain is statistically significant depends on the assumed variation. An optimistic assumption calls 12.0% of comparisons between equally good codecs significant on test sets as small as Kodak, instead of 5%; a cautious one leaves 18 of 45 differences between recent codecs undecided, yet per-image results show 12 of them to be significant. Beyond auditing past results, LIC-Audit provides a versioned evaluation protocol, a public leaderboard website with a scoring tool, source-linked benchmark data, per-image measurements, and reporting guidelines, to help future LIC papers make comparable and verifiable claims of progress.

</details>
